# The End Is the Beginning

*A horror novel*


---

# PART ONE: THE NAMELESS


---

# Chapter One
## The Boy the Sand Forgot

> *When the grey comes creeping and the names are lost,*
> *A child of the feather will carry the cost.*
> — from the Song of the Phoenix-Born, as sung over cradles in the desert villages

---

He is burning.

The Ash hangs full and white above the Crown Dune, so close and so swollen that it looks like something that has fed. The highest sand in the world lies under it, and on that sand a boy stands with his arms open, as if he is about to catch something falling out of the sky.

The fire doesn't start at his hands. It starts in his chest, under the mark, the small golden feather that has glowed in the dark his whole life. It splits like a seed splits, from the inside, because the thing inside it has grown too big to stay. Light comes through the cracks in his skin. It runs down his arms and up his throat. It comes out of his eyes, and his eyes go with it.

You can smell it. Nobody ever tells you that part in the songs. The light smells of honey and hot metal, and under that, very faintly, of hair.

And the boy doesn't scream.

That's the part I always remember. I used to think it was courage. Lately I've wondered whether the fire takes the scream first, the way a thief cuts the bell off a goat before he leads it away.

The light keeps growing until it isn't a boy any more. It's a shape, two great wings of gold unfolding across the dark, wide enough to cover the dunes from one edge of the sky to the other. Then it falls. Gold rains down over the desert, and everywhere it lands, the grey draws back.

Draws back. I want to be careful with my words, because you'll hold me to them later.

Somewhere below, someone is shouting a name. The wind is listening to every letter.

---

But that is the end.

Let me begin where the boy had no name.

---

You'll want to know who I am, I suppose. Everyone always does, and they never like the answer.

I am a fox. Small, sand-coloured, with ears tipped black as if they've been dipped in ink, or in something worse. My tail glows like a coal when someone tells the truth, which makes me the worst possible company at a funeral. I have one amber eye and one silver eye. We'll get to the silver one later. Don't look at it too long. Things look back.

I'm older than every city in this desert. I'm older than the city that turned to glass. I have walked beside eleven children who carried the Light, and I have watched nearly every one of them climb the same dune under the same full Ash. Children. Notice that word, because the songs don't. *The Phoenix-Born*, *the chosen*: very good words for not saying *child*. The Boy of Salt was nine. He asked me, at the top, whether it would be like putting his hand in the cooking fire, and I told him no, and my tail went out like a pinched wick.

Some of them were brave. Some of them ran. I'm still angry at the ones who ran. I'm angrier at the sky for asking. I'll tell you about all of them eventually, if you're good and stop wriggling.

This is the story of the twelfth.

He was the one I loved most. Don't tell the others.

---

The village was called Harrow's End, which tells you most of what you need to know about it.

It sat on the last hard ground before the Destiny Desert, forty clay houses crouched around one deep well like old men guarding a single coin. To the north and east, the dunes rolled away gold and endless, and they moved every night. They *moved*, whole mountains of them, sliding in the dark with a sound like something very large breathing through its nose, rewriting the paths while everyone slept. Some mornings there were long dragging furrows in the sand that ended at the doorsteps and went no farther. Nobody talked about it.

The people of Harrow's End feared the sand the way you'd fear a large animal that hadn't decided yet whether to eat you.

They feared the boy a little more.

He was fifteen that year, as far as anyone could guess. The desert had dropped him on the edge of the village after a sandstorm, a baby wrapped in grey cloth that no weaver in Harrow's End could name. No mother, no tracks. Only a baby, lying in a perfect circle of smooth sand in the middle of the storm's wreckage, crying.

And a fox, sitting a short way off, watching.

I'll admit nothing about that.

The village kept him the way you'd keep a stray dog that wandered in during a storm. They fed him when they remembered and kicked him when they didn't. They called him *Sandborn* when they were feeling kind and *the Nameless* when they weren't, which was most days. Nobody ever gave him a real name. You don't name the lamb that's been set aside.

So he gave himself one. He was seven years old, sitting alone on the edge of the well, and he decided he would be called *Zayn*. He didn't know where the word came from. It simply arrived in his mouth one evening, already shaped, as if someone had left it there for him. He told no one. A name was the only thing in the world that belonged to him, and he had already learned that the people of Harrow's End took things.

They weren't the only ones. But he hadn't learned that yet.

---

On the morning this story begins, Zayn was underground.

There were ruins north of the village, half-swallowed by the dunes: the bones of some town so old that even the stones had forgotten its name. Every few weeks the sand opened a new room. Once, on one wall, very faint, he'd found a list of names scratched into the plaster, every one of them scratched out again afterwards, letter by letter, by someone with a great deal of time.

The village called the ruins cursed, which was convenient, because it meant the only person willing to dig through them was the boy they already thought was cursed. Zayn was a scavenger. It was the only work anyone would give him.

He crouched now in a chamber no taller than a goat, his scavenged knife between his teeth, prying at a strip of copper that ran along the base of a wall. Light fell in thin gold blades through the cracks above. Every time he moved, sand hissed down from the ceiling in a long slow exhale, and then stopped, and then, a moment later, hissed again. As if the room were breathing with him. As if it were learning how.

"You know," I said from the doorway, "most people your age are asleep at this hour. Or eating. Or doing anything that isn't crawling around inside a dead building that very much wants to keep them."

He didn't answer. He never did when I talked. Back then he thought I was a strange fox that hung around his tent, and that the words he heard were his loneliness playing tricks on him. I let him think it. Better a boy who thinks he's going mad than a boy who knows what I am.

The copper came loose with a crack. Zayn grinned around the knife, the brief, crooked grin of a boy who doesn't get many reasons to smile and doesn't waste them.

Then the wall groaned.

It was long and low and unhappy, a great weight changing its mind. Dust poured from the ceiling. Zayn froze. His hand went to his chest, to the spot just under his collarbone where the mark sat beneath his shirt. He always did that when he was afraid.

The mark was a feather. A birthmark the colour of honey, with fine lines in it like the barbs of a real one. On dark nights it glowed. Faintly, so faintly you could pretend you hadn't seen it. Zayn had spent fifteen years pretending he hadn't seen it.

Under his palm it was warm. Warmer than skin should be.

The wall held. The groaning stopped.

And in the silence afterwards, from somewhere deeper in the ruin, from a part of it no one had dug out in a thousand years, something knocked. Twice. Politely. The way a guest knocks.

Zayn didn't move for a long time.

Then he tucked the copper into his bag and crawled out into the Fire, and he did not look back, and I loved him a little more for having the sense not to.

---

The Fire is what we call the sun out here. The moon is the Ash, and the stars are the Embers: all the little bits of light left over after the sky burns down each night. Desert people are dramatic. You also notice everything in it is named after something burned.

By the time Zayn walked back into Harrow's End, the Fire was high and white and merciless, and his bag held enough copper to buy water for three days.

He got water for one.

"Copper's cheap this month," said old Garrem at the trading post, sliding a single clay water token across the counter. Each token was worth one cup from the village well. "Too many scavengers."

There was exactly one scavenger in Harrow's End, and both of them knew it.

Zayn didn't argue. He looked at the token for a long moment, then picked it up and turned to go.

He made it halfway across the square before someone said, "Look. The Nameless is back from his grave."

Bram.

There's one in every village. Bram was Zayn's age, broad-shouldered and quick-tongued, the son of Hadric the well-keeper, which made him almost a prince in a place where water was money. He sat on the edge of the well with three other boys around him like flies around a date.

"Find any ghosts down there, Nameless?" Bram called, hopping down from the well. "Find your mother? Maybe that's where they found you. In a hole, with the rest of the rubbish."

Zayn kept walking.

There was a scar on Zayn's back, a pale, puckered line just below his left shoulder blade. He'd been six. A group of children had decided that the strange boy with the too-light eyes was a demon, and demons could be driven out with stones. Bram had been five years old that day and standing at the front. Neither of them ever spoke of it. Neither of them ever forgot.

"Hey." A rock hit the sand by Zayn's foot. "I'm *talking* to you, monster."

That stopped him.

It always did. He could take *Nameless* and *rubbish* and *grave-rat*. But *monster* went into him like a hook. Maybe because, on the dark nights, when the feather glowed under his shirt and he lay awake feeling it pulse in time with something that was not his heart, a small and frightened part of him wondered whether it was true.

He turned around.

Bram was grinning. Waiting. Hoping.

And Zayn said, very quietly, "I'm not a monster."

He said it because it was true. That was the thing about Zayn, and it's the first clue in this story, so pay attention: **he could not lie.** Whenever a lie got as far as his tongue, the feather on his chest would burn as if a coal had been pressed into it, deep enough that he could smell it, sharp enough to bring water to his eyes. Once, at eight, he'd tried to push a lie through the pain and woken in the sand with a blister the shape of a feather.

So he told the truth, always, even when the truth got him hit. Mostly *because* the truth got him hit.

"Not a monster," Bram repeated, in a high, mocking voice. The boys laughed again.

Zayn turned and walked away with his hand pressed flat against his chest, and nobody followed.

From the shadow of a doorway, I watched Bram's grin fade once Zayn was gone. He looked down at his hands for a moment, as though he wasn't sure whose they were.

My tail flickered. People are rarely only one thing. Remember that. You'll need it, later, when you want to hate him.

---

Zayn's tent sat a little way outside the village, on a patch of hard ground between the last house and the first dune. It was patched in nine colours, and the wind came through the seams at night and whistled, and on some nights the whistling sounded almost like words. It was his.

There was a bundle waiting outside it.

It appeared every few days, with no footprints leading to it. A square of faded blue cloth, knotted at the top. Inside was flatbread, still faintly warm from the baking stones. And sometimes, on the good days, a twist of waxed paper holding four sticky dates rolled in honey.

Today was a good day.

Zayn sat down in the doorway of his tent and held the dates in his palm for a long time without eating them. Dates and honey were the finest thing he knew of in the world. He always saved them, for the hardest days, as if he could store sweetness the way you store water against a drought.

He didn't know who left the bundles. He had decided, when he was small, that it must be the Weaver, the one who "writes the sands", and the Weaver had made a mistake with him, felt bad about it, and left bread to apologise.

He was wrong about that. The Weaver doesn't apologise. I've waited a very long time to hear it try.

It was Auntie Sabba.

She was the old widow who baked bread for half the village in a clay oven behind her house. She never once spoke to Zayn in public or defended him. She was old and alone and afraid, and in a place like Harrow's End, kindness out loud could cost you everything. So she was kind quietly, at dawn, and left before anyone could see.

The world is held together by people like Auntie Sabba: too frightened to be brave, good anyway in small, secret ways. Zayn never learned her name in all his years in that village. But the sand remembers. That's what he used to tell himself on the worst nights, lying alone in his patched tent and listening to the wind try out its words.

*The sand remembers.*

He meant it as a comfort. He didn't think very hard about the other side of it, which is that a thing that remembers everything also forgets nothing. Not a kindness. Not a cruelty. Not a name.

---

When the Fire went down and the first Embers came out, Zayn went to see Old Hesset.

Hesset's tent sat at the far edge of the village, even farther out than Zayn's. It was hung inside and out with dried herbs and with dozens of tiny bronze bells on threads. The bells were silent most of the time. They rang only when someone in the tent told a lie.

He was the oldest man anyone in Harrow's End had ever seen. His skin was brown and creased like a dry riverbed, his beard was white as salt, and his eyes were milky and pale. He was blind, and had been for longer than anyone could remember. The village came to him for medicine and left as fast as they could.

Only Zayn came for the stories.

"You're late," Hesset said, before Zayn had even pushed aside the tent flap. "And you smell like copper and disappointment. Garrem cheated you again."

"One token," said Zayn.

"For three days' worth of copper." Hesset clicked his tongue. "Sit. There's tea."

There was always tea. It tasted of smoke and mint, and Zayn drank it every night and hated it every night, and would have died before he admitted that. Hold on to that taste for me. You'll remember it longer than he does.

"Which one tonight?" Hesset asked.

"The Phoenix-Born," said Zayn. He asked for that one every time.

Hesset smiled. Fond and sad at once, the way a man smiles at a letter from someone he once let drown.

"Again?" he said. "You know it better than I do."

"Tell it anyway."

So he did.

He told of the time before time, when the Weaver first wrote the sands, and of how a darkness came, born from fear, that made people forget who they were. Whenever the darkness grew too strong, the Weaver would place a piece of its own light inside one child. That child would carry the light across the desert to the highest dune in the world. There, under the full Ash, the child would set the light free and drive the darkness away.

"Whenever the darkness grows too strong," Zayn repeated. "How often is that?"

Hesset's hands paused on his cup. "Often enough," he said.

The bells didn't stir. True. Horribly, precisely true.

"The Phoenix-Born," Hesset went on softly. "That's what the old songs call them. They rise like the fire-bird from the ashes. They save everyone."

Zayn's eyes were shining. They always shone at this part.

You have to understand what that story meant to a boy who was nameless, thrown away and called a monster. It said that sometimes the forgotten ones are chosen. He had never told anyone his secret dream. But I knew it the way you know the shape of your own paw.

He wanted to be a hero.

Not rich. Not even loved, though he wanted that too. He wanted to *matter*. He wanted the people who threw stones to look up one day and understand that they had been wrong about him.

I have heard eleven children want that. The sky has never once failed to give it to them. That's the cruelty of it. It always keeps its word.

"What happens to them?" Zayn asked. "After? When the darkness is gone?"

He had never asked that before.

Hesset went very still. On their threads, the bronze bells waited. I watched one of them tremble, very slightly, the way a dog trembles when it hears a footstep it knows.

"They are remembered," Hesset said at last.

No bell rang. It was true, after all. Just not the whole truth.

*The whole truth* and *true* are two very different animals, and the space between them is where most of the pain in the world lives. Children fall into that space. Hesset knew exactly what happened to the Phoenix-Born, better than anyone alive. He chose not to say. I've been angry at him about that for a very long time. But that's later.

"It's late," Hesset said, and the moment passed. "Go home. And Zayn—"

The boy paused at the tent flap.

"Remember the rule," said the old man. "Never say your name to the wind."

The elders said the darkness had ears, and the wind carried everything to it eventually, and a name spoken carelessly into the open air could be found by things that collect names. In the old villages, mothers gave their babies a false name for the first year, so that if anything came calling in the night it would ask for the wrong child.

It never asked for the wrong child. But it was a nice thought.

Zayn nodded. He stepped out into the night.

And because he was fifteen, and lonely, and because the only thing in the world that was truly his was a name nobody else would ever say, he walked to the top of the nearest dune, faced the open desert, and whispered it.

"Zayn."

The wind took it, gently. Too gently. It carried it away north and east over the moving sand, toward the dark.

He stood there a long while afterward, his hand pressed to his chest, feeling oddly lighter, as though he'd sent a letter to someone who might one day write back.

Then the wind turned.

It came back over the dune from the north, cold, much colder than a desert night has any right to be, and it brushed past his ear, and in it, very faint, very far away, something said:

*"Zayn."*

In his own voice. Exactly his own voice. Only empty, the way a cup is empty, the way a room is empty after a death. Said back to him the way you'd repeat a word in a language you don't speak, to see how it feels in the mouth.

Then he saw it.

Far out on the horizon, where the gold of the dunes met the black of the sky, there was a line of grey. It was low and flat, like mist lying on water, except there was no water in the Destiny Desert. It hadn't been there in fifteen years of nights. It wasn't moving fast. It didn't need to.

It had no edge. Where the dunes ran into it they went on being dunes for a little while, paler and flatter, as if forgetting how.

Above it, the Embers were going out.

Not dimming. Going out. One by one, low on the sky, the way lamps go out in a house where someone is walking from room to room, pinching the wicks. And he understood, with the cold, sure understanding you have in nightmares, that the thing on the horizon was not coming toward the village. It was coming toward the place where his name had been spoken.

The feather on his chest began, very faintly, to burn.

And for one instant, no longer than a blink, he wasn't on the dune at all. He was somewhere higher, much higher, with a white Ash swollen above him, and there was heat in his mouth, and the smell of honey and hot metal and hair. Then it was gone, and he was on an ordinary dune with his heart going like a trapped bird.

He thought he'd imagined it. He was very good, back then, at that.

He did not see me sitting at the foot of the dune behind him. My tail glowed steady and bright, because everything about that moment was true, and I have never hated my own tail quite so much.

"Well," I said to no one, to the wind, to the Weaver if it cared to listen. "Here we go again."

Twelve. I counted it out on the sand with one claw, the way I always do, and then I smoothed the marks away before he could see them.

The grey was coming.

And this time, it had learned his name. It was practising.

---

*End of Chapter One*


---

# Chapter Two
## When the Grey Came Creeping

> *The grey does not bite. The grey forgets for you. And then it wears what is left.*
> — a warning carved above the doors of old desert wells, most of it worn away

---

Nobody in Harrow's End believed him.

Nobody there had believed anything Zayn said in fifteen years. Never mind that he couldn't lie. They'd never bothered to notice.

He ran into the village before dawn, sand in his hair, and banged on the door of the headman's house until a light came on. He told the headman what he had seen. A line of grey on the horizon, low and flat, and the Embers going out above it one by one.

He didn't tell him about the voice in the wind. There was no way to say it that didn't sound mad.

The headman, a heavy man named Oszkar with a beard like a thornbush, listened with his eyes half closed. Then he said, "Mist."

"There's no mist in the desert," said Zayn.

"Dust, then. A storm."

"It wasn't moving like a storm. It was—" Zayn searched for the word. "Listening."

Oszkar looked at him the way you'd look at a goat that had started reciting poetry. Then something crossed his face, quick and ugly, and he glanced past Zayn's shoulder toward the east. "Go back to your tent, Sandborn," he said, and shut the door. I heard the bar drop across it on the other side.

I was sitting on the wall across the lane. I could have told the headman myself. I could have said in perfectly clear words: *Your village has about one day before the Hollow puts its hand inside it.* Anyone can hear me, if I decide to speak.

But I have rules. The bearer has to see it first. He has to be the one who knows. The times I've broken that rule, things went worse. Or so I tell myself. I've had a great deal of time to practise.

So I said nothing. I'm not proud of that. It's becoming a theme.

---

The grey came the next day.

Not all at once. The Hollow doesn't charge like an army. It *seeps*. It arrives the way cold arrives in a house at night: first in the corners, then along the floor, until one moment you realise you can't remember the last time you were warm. Or what warm was. Or why you'd want it.

It started with the colour.

Auntie Sabba noticed first, though no one asked her. She came out of her house in the morning with a basket of dough on her hip, and stopped. The blue door of the house beside hers, painted the colour of the sky by a man who had died twenty years ago, wasn't blue any more. It wasn't any colour at all. It was the flat, dead grey of old ash.

Above the door, where every house painted the names of its dead so the sand would know whom to remember, there had been three names in white. There were two now. She was sure there had been three. She tried to remember the third and found only a smooth place in her mind, like a tooth socket the tongue keeps returning to.

She went back inside and didn't come out again until evening.

By noon, the whole eastern edge of the village had gone grey.

The red cloth over Garrem's trading post was grey. The date palms were grey. A goat wandered out of the grey part of the village and stood in the square, and its brown coat was the colour of cold porridge, and its eyes were the white of a boiled egg. It stood with its head at a slightly wrong angle, as if it were listening to something underneath the ground, and when a child threw a date at it, it didn't flinch. It simply turned its white eyes, very slowly, toward the child, and kept them there.

The child's mother carried him inside. The goat watched the door all day.

And it was quiet. That was the part that frightened people most, though few of them could have told you why. In the grey part of the village there was no sound at all. No wind in the palms. No dogs. No creak of doors.

People began to gather in the square. They talked in low, nervous voices. An old woman said she'd heard stories from her grandmother's grandmother about a thing that came out of the deep desert and made people forget, and that you knew it was close when the dead started losing their names off the walls. Oszkar told her to stop talking nonsense, too loudly, and didn't once look at the names above his own door.

Zayn stood at the very edge of the crowd, where he always stood, close enough to hear and far enough to run.

His hand was pressed flat against his chest.

The feather mark had been burning since the dune. A slow, low heat, like a coal banked under ash, and it got hotter whenever he looked east. Every time he closed his eyes he smelled honey and hot metal. *My birthmark is warm and I keep smelling something burning* wasn't the kind of thing that made people trust you.

"It's moving," someone said.

Everyone turned.

At the eastern end of the square, the grey had reached the first house. It climbed the clay wall the way damp climbs a cloth. It slid across a child's wooden toy left in the dirt, a little painted camel. The toy had been yellow. It wasn't any more. It had been lying on its side. When the grey had passed, it was standing upright, facing the crowd.

Nobody had touched it.

"Everyone inside," said Oszkar. "Shut your doors. Put out your lamps. Stay away from the windows."

As if a door could stop it.

---

The Fire went down red and swollen over the western dunes. Night came on. And with the night came the Husks.

They were people. That's what you must understand first. Every one of them had been a person once, a herder or a trader or somebody's son, until the Hollow found them and emptied them out. It takes names first, then memories, then all the small invisible threads that tie one person to another. What's left still walks. It still has a face. But the eyes are white and blank as boiled eggs, and the skin is the colour of ash, and the face doesn't do anything.

Except, sometimes, smile.

That's the worst of it. Not the eyes. The smile. Somewhere in what's left there's a memory of what faces are *for*, and every so often a Husk will try. The corners of the mouth go up, too far and too slowly, like a curtain being drawn by someone who has never seen a window. The eyes don't change. Nothing behind them changes. It's a smile the way a footprint is a foot.

And they talk. Not to you. They say the last thing they said before the grey took them, over and over, in their own voices, worn smooth, like a prayer in a language nobody speaks any more. A trader might walk for a hundred years saying *"Two tokens, and that's my last price."* A mother might say *"Come in now, it's getting dark."* It isn't a message. It's what's left when the person is gone: one sentence, rattling around in the empty house like a bead in a gourd.

They are drawn to light, like moths that never, ever stop.

They came out of the grey just after dark. First one, walking slowly up the eastern lane. Then three. Then a dozen, and more behind them, a grey crowd moving between the houses with no sound but the murmur of their dozens of last sentences, all overlapping, like a market heard through a wall.

Zayn saw them from the top of the hill behind his tent. He saw the shapes drifting through the dark streets, stopping at doors, laying their palms flat against the wood and standing there, as though listening for a heartbeat. He saw them turn, all together, toward the one house that had forgotten to put out its lamp.

He was running before he'd decided to.

---

I followed him. Of course I followed him. I'd been following him for fifteen years.

"This is a bad idea," I said, loping alongside him down the slope. "I want that on record. For the funeral. Short speech, I thought: *he was warned.*"

He didn't answer. He didn't even look at me. His whole face was fixed on the village, his knife already in his hand. The scavenged one, rusted along the spine, its edge sharpened on a flat stone.

A knife. Against the Hollow.

I ran faster, and listened to the voices coming up the lane to meet us, and tried very hard not to recognise any of them.

---

The house with the lamp belonged to the well-keeper.

Bram's house.

Zayn came round the corner of the lane and stopped so suddenly that I nearly ran into the backs of his legs.

The Husks had surrounded it. Twenty of them, perhaps more, standing in a silent half-circle around the door, their white eyes all fixed on the yellow light leaking from the shutters. They swayed together, the way grass sways. From inside, Zayn could hear someone crying. A small child, by the sound. And a man's voice, low and frightened, saying *hush, hush, hush*.

And out in the lane, under the Husks' murmuring, three or four of the grey shapes had begun to say it too.

*"Hush. Hush. Hush."*

Not mocking. Just learning it. The way the wind had learned Zayn's name.

In front of the door, between the Husks and the house, stood Bram.

He had a shovel, the heavy iron kind they used to clear sand from the well. He held it like a sword, shaking so badly the blade rattled.

"Get back," he was saying. His voice cracked in half on every word. "Get *back*. I mean it. Get back."

The Husks didn't get back.

One of them stepped forward. Slow. Unhurried. It had been a woman once, a grey woman in a grey dress, with grey beads still threaded in her grey hair. Her lips were moving.

*"Mind the step, love,"* she said. *"Mind the step, love. Mind the step, love."*

She said it kindly. She'd said it to someone once, on some doorstep, and then the grey had come up the path behind her, and her life had stopped on that one sentence like a cart stuck in a rut.

She reached out a hand toward Bram's face. And she smiled.

Bram swung the shovel.

It connected with a dull, hollow thud, like hitting a sack of wet sand. The Husk's head went sideways, much too far sideways, and stayed there. She didn't fall. She didn't stop smiling. She straightened, slowly, her head coming back up with a soft grinding sound, and reached out again, and this time her fingers brushed Bram's cheek.

*"Mind the step, love."*

Bram went rigid.

I watched it happen. The colour drained out of the side of his face where she'd touched it, as if someone had wiped away paint with a wet thumb, and left the clay underneath. His eyes went wide and lost. The shovel slid out of his hands and fell into the sand.

"I—" Bram said. He looked down at his hands. "I was… I was doing something. What was I…"

He had forgotten. Not everything. Not yet. A Husk's touch steals a little at a time: a memory here, a feeling there. I could see him searching for what he'd lost, patting at his own mind the way you pat your pockets for a token that isn't there. He had forgotten *why* he was standing there. He had forgotten there was a child crying behind the door who needed him.

The child cried harder. Bram didn't turn around. He'd forgotten there was anyone to turn around for.

The Husk reached for him again.

And Zayn moved.

---

He didn't think about it. Later people would tell stories about this moment. They'd say a lot of foolish, flattering things, the way people always do about children who are about to be sent up a hill.

He didn't know anything. He was fifteen and terrified, and the boy who'd thrown stones at him since he was five was being erased in front of him one memory at a time. Zayn simply could not stand there and watch.

So he ran into the middle of twenty Husks with a rusty knife.

He slashed at the grey woman's arm. The blade passed through her sleeve and hit something that wasn't quite flesh, cold and dense like packed clay. It left a thin line. No blood. Inside the line, for a moment, there was nothing at all. Not darkness. Nothing. Then it closed.

She didn't even look at him.

But the others did.

All of them, at once. Twenty grey faces turned slowly from the lamp in the window toward the boy in the middle of the lane, and twenty mouths stopped murmuring in the same instant, and the silence that came down was the loudest thing I have ever heard.

Then, one by one, they began to smile at him.

They were drawn to light. I've told you that.

And somewhere under Zayn's shirt, the feather mark had started to glow.

---

He felt it before he saw it. A heat in his chest that wasn't fear, though there was plenty of fear too. It was a *burning*. It started at the centre of the mark and spread outward along his ribs and up his throat and down into his arms, like swallowing a coal and feeling it catch.

The Husks came toward him. Their voices started again, all at once, all different, all close. *"Two tokens and that's my last price." "Come in now, it's getting dark." "Mind the step, love." "Who left the gate open?" "Hush. Hush."*

He backed up until his shoulders hit the wall of the house. The knife shook in front of him, and the burning in his chest was getting worse—

And the light came.

Not gold. Not at first.

It flickered out of his skin, out of the cracks of his clenched fists, and it was *grey*. A thin, dim, sickly grey, like light seen through a dirty window. It sputtered. And the Husks didn't slow. They came faster, the way a cat comes faster at a mouse that's started to limp. Some of them tilted their heads at it, almost tenderly, as if the grey light were a word in their own language.

That's what fear does to the Light. It makes it sound like home to them. I should have warned him.

The grey woman reached for his face. Her fingers were an inch from his eye. He could smell her: dust, and cold stone, and very faintly, underneath, the rosewater she must have worn once.

Zayn shut his eyes.

And behind the door, the child cried out. One word, high and thin and broken.

"*Bram!*"

Something in Zayn's chest *turned over*.

I saw his eyes snap open, and I saw him look past the Husks to Bram. Bram, standing lost and grey-cheeked in the lane, staring at his own hands. A boy who had forgotten someone he loved.

Zayn knew exactly what that was like. He'd spent his whole life being the someone nobody remembered.

He stopped thinking about himself.

And the light went *gold*.

---

It came out of him like the Fire coming up over the dunes.

One moment the lane was dark and grey and full of smiling shapes. The next it was blazing. Golden light poured out of Zayn's hands, out of his chest, out of the cracks between his fingers, out of his eyes. It filled the lane from wall to wall. It lit every face, every grey robe and every white eye, and threw their shadows huge and black against the houses. For one instant I saw the shadows clearly, and they were not the shapes of the people casting them. They had too many joints. They were all leaning toward him.

The Husks *recoiled*.

They didn't scream. They can't. But they flinched back from the light the way your hand flinches from a hot stone, all of them at once, stumbling and lurching away into the dark. The grey woman with the beads in her hair staggered backward. And for one heartbeat, I saw her eyes change. The white faded, and underneath was brown, ordinary brown, the eyes of a woman who had once threaded beads into her hair because she liked how they caught the light.

She looked at Zayn. Her mouth opened. For a moment I thought she would say something new, something that was hers.

"*Where are my*—" she said.

Then the grey closed back over her like water over a dropped stone, and the smile came back, slow and wrong, and she said, *"Mind the step, love,"* and was gone with the others into the night.

I have wondered for a long time what the end of that sentence was. Children, probably. It usually is.

---

The light faded.

It drew back into Zayn the way water drains out of sand.

He slid down the wall and sat in the sand.

His hands were shaking. There were thin golden lines on his palms, like cracks in glazed pottery, faintly glowing. They hurt the way a burn hurts the next day. He stared at them as if they belonged to someone else.

And then it happened. The thing that would happen every time he used the Light, for the rest of his life.

He saw fire.

A full white Ash, enormous, filling half the sky. A dune higher than any dune he had ever seen. And heat, inside him, rising up his throat like something climbing out. He could feel his own skin tightening. He could feel the fine hairs on his arms curl and shrivel. He could taste honey, and under the honey, smoke.

It lasted less than a breath. Then he was sitting in the sand of Harrow's End, gasping, his hands slapping at his own arms to put out a fire that wasn't there.

He thought it was a trick of a tired mind. He would learn otherwise. I sat beside him in the sand and didn't say a word, and I counted, as I always do, and the number was still twelve.

---

The door of the well-keeper's house opened.

Hadric stood there, a lamp in one hand and a carving knife in the other, his face white. Behind him, peeking around his legs, was Bram's little brother, a boy of six or seven, his face streaked with tears.

Around the lane, other doors were opening. A light bright as midday had come through the shutters and brought the neighbours out.

They looked at the lane. At the empty sand where twenty Husks had stood, now marked with footprints that all faced the same way. At Bram, still standing there, blinking slowly, one side of his face grey as ash.

And at Zayn, sitting against the wall with golden cracks glowing in his palms.

Nobody said anything for a long moment.

Bram turned his head and looked down at Zayn. His eyes were clearing. What the Husk had taken was seeping back. I saw him remembering the shovel, the door, his brother crying. I saw him remembering the light.

He opened his mouth.

I like to think, even now, that it might have been *thank you*.

But he never got the chance, because that was when I decided to open *my* mouth.

---

"Well," I said loudly, from the top of the well-keeper's garden wall. "*That* was overdue."

Every head in the lane turned toward me, including Zayn's.

He stared up at me. His mouth fell open. For fifteen years he'd told himself the voice was loneliness, or the wind. And now forty people were staring at the same fox, their faces as white as the Ash.

They had heard it too.

"Oh, don't look at me like that," I said to the lane in general. "You've all seen worse tonight. Twenty dead neighbours with good manners, a boy who glows, half your village the colour of old porridge. A talking fox is honestly the *least* of your problems. I'd go and check the names above your doors, if I were you. Count them. Then count them again in the morning."

My tail was glowing bright as a lamp. Everything I'd said was perfectly true.

Somebody screamed.

I think it was Garrem. It was a very high scream for such a large man.

And then everyone was shouting at once. Oszkar came barrelling down the lane in his nightshirt bellowing for order. Through all of it, Zayn sat in the sand against the wall and stared up at me with his glowing hands in his lap.

"You," he said. His voice was barely a whisper. "You can… you *talk*."

"I've always talked," I said. "You just never listened properly. Most people don't. It's one of the great tragedies of the world. Not the greatest. I'll get to that."

"*Why didn't you tell me?*"

I hopped down from the wall and sat beside him in the sand. For a moment I didn't say anything. I looked at the golden cracks in his palms, and at the mark on his chest, still faintly warm under his shirt. I looked at his face. It was very young, and very frightened.

I have sat beside eleven children like this. I have watched the moment when they first understood that their life was not going to be the life they'd planned. One of them laughed. The Falconer asked me, very politely, whether he could go home now.

It doesn't get easier. You'd think it would.

"Because," I said finally, quietly, so only he could hear, "once I told you, it would all start. And I wanted you to have a little longer before it did."

He looked at me.

My tail glowed steady and gold in the dark.

---

Somewhere at the edge of the village, out where the grey was thickest, I heard a sound that made the fur stand up along my spine.

It was a child's voice. Small and confused, and calling out into the empty night.

"Mama?" it said. "Bram? Where… where is everyone? Who…"

A pause.

"Who am I?"

And then, from all around it, very softly, from the grey shapes standing out there in the dark, a dozen voices that were learning, said it back.

*"Who am I?"*

*"Who am I?"*

*"Who am I?"*

Beside me, Bram had gone completely still.

Because his little brother, the one who had been crying behind the door, wasn't behind the door any more. In all the shouting, he had slipped out into the lane to find his big brother.

And he had walked straight into the grey.

---

*End of Chapter Two*


---

# Chapter Three
## Monster

> *Beware the child who glows; the desert has plans for him, and the desert is always hungry.*
> — a saying of the desert grandmothers, told to children who stay out after dark

---

Bram ran first.

I'll give him that. Whatever else he did, before and after, Bram was the first one into the grey that night.

"*Tobin!*"

The grey swallowed the name. Sound doesn't carry in the Hollow. You shout, and the shout leaves your mouth and simply stops, like a stone dropped into thick mud. Bram shouted his brother's name three times, and each time it died a hand's width from his lips.

And each time, somewhere deeper in, something said it back to him. Softly. Getting it a little more right each time.

Zayn was on his feet before he knew it. His legs were shaking. His palms still burned where the golden cracks ran across them. He looked at the flat dead place where the lane stopped being a lane, and took one step toward it.

"No," I said.

He took another.

"I said *no*. You're half dead on your feet, and that is the Hollow. Not a story. The thing itself. You walk in there tonight and something else might walk out wearing you."

He looked down at me. "He's seven," he said.

And then he went in after Bram, and I went in after him, because apparently that's who I am.

---

The grey feels like nothing.

It's the feeling of a room after everyone you love has left it, spread over the whole world. The colour goes first. Then the sound. Then, if you stay long enough, the reasons you came. Every time I come out of it, I'm a little less sure which of my memories are mine.

Zayn walked with his hand flat against his chest, and the feather under his palm was the only warm thing for a hundred paces in any direction. Every few steps he whispered something, too low for the grey to steal.

*The sand remembers. The sand remembers.*

There were Husks in there. Standing between the grey houses like fence posts, faces turned up toward a sky with no Embers in it. As we passed, their heads came round, slowly, one after another, following the warmth of the mark. They smiled their wrong smiles and murmured their last sentences into the dead air. One of them was a boy no older than Tobin. He was saying, *"I'm not tired, I'm not tired, I'm not tired."*

Zayn didn't look at him. I was proud of that, and sick about it.

We found Bram on his knees at the end of the lane.

Tobin was sitting in front of him in the sand, cross-legged, the way small children sit when they've been told to wait and haven't been told for how long. Four Husks stood around him in a neat square, one at each corner, facing inward. Keeping him. The way you'd keep a lamb in a pen.

He wasn't crying any more. That was what frightened me most. His face had gone the colour of old ash, and his eyes were open and calm and terribly white.

Bram had him by the shoulders, shaking him gently, desperately.

"Tobin. Tobin, it's me. Look at me. *Look* at me."

Tobin looked at him. Politely, the way you'd look at a stranger who had stopped you in a market.

"Who are you?" he said.

And around him, the four Husks said it too, in four different voices, at exactly the same moment. *"Who are you?"*

Bram made a sound I've heard many times and still have no word for. It's the sound of something tearing that isn't cloth.

---

They got him out of the grey between them. Bram carried him and Zayn walked behind, his glowing palms held up like lanterns, and the grey drew back from them a little. The four Husks didn't follow. They stayed exactly where they were, standing around the empty square of sand, facing inward at nothing. I looked back once. They were still guarding it.

Hadric the well-keeper was waiting at the edge with a lamp. He took one look at his younger son's face and sat down in the sand as if his legs had been cut from under him. Behind him people were gathering: Oszkar, Garrem with a cudgel, neighbours clutching shawls.

Bram laid his brother on the hard ground outside the house.

Then he turned and looked at Zayn.

It wasn't hatred. It was the look of someone at the bottom of a deep pit, seeing a rope and hating that it was this particular rope.

"You did something," Bram said hoarsely. "Before. You did something with the light and that woman's eyes went *brown*. I saw it."

Zayn said nothing.

"Do it again."

"I don't know how."

It was true. My tail glowed with it, soft gold in the dark. Bram didn't know what that meant yet, but he saw it, and something in his face crumpled.

"Please," said Bram.

I don't think he'd ever said that word to Zayn before. It came out of him rusty and broken, like a gate that hadn't been opened in years.

"Please. He's my brother. *Please.*"

Zayn knelt.

---

I want to tell you he laid his hands on that child like the heroes in Hesset's stories. He didn't. His hands were shaking so badly that when he set them on either side of Tobin's face, the boy's head rocked. He was thinking of the Husks, and the grey, and forty people watching him from behind their lamps. He was thinking about what they'd say. He was thinking about himself.

So the light came grey.

It leaked out of his palms thin and sickly, the colour of the thing he was trying to fight. It lay over Tobin's face like dirty water. And Tobin, under it, smiled. Slowly. Too wide. The Husk smile, on a seven-year-old's mouth.

Zayn snatched his hands back as if he'd been bitten.

"I can't," he whispered. "I can't, I'm making it worse, it won't—"

"Stop looking at them," I said, right by his knee, low. "Stop thinking about what they see. They're not the ones who need you."

"I don't know how to—"

"Look at *him*."

He looked.

And I watched it happen for the second time that night. I watched him stop being a frightened boy surrounded by people who hated him, and become a boy looking at a child who had forgotten his own name. A child who was, in that moment, exactly as alone as Zayn had been every day of his life.

The light went gold.

---

It came up out of him gently this time, softer and warmer than the blaze in the lane. It flowed from his chest down his arms and into Tobin's face like honey poured into water, and the grey began to lift.

And Zayn saw.

He saw a baby born in the hot season, red-faced and furious, while a boy of eight pressed his nose to the doorframe. He saw that same boy carrying the baby on his shoulders to the top of the dune behind the well, pointing at the Embers and making up names for them. *That one's the Goat. That one's the Fat Man. That one's you, see, the little one, right next to me.* He saw Bram sneaking him extra bread, and sitting alone behind the house one night after their father had shouted, crying silently into his knees where nobody could see.

Nobody but the small boy watching from the window.

Then the pictures turned and ran forward. Tobin taller, with his brother's shoulders, laughing across a fire. Tobin's hands, older, holding a lantern. Blurred, like a painting left in the rain. The future always is. But warm. Full of colour.

And then, behind all of it, the other vision.

It didn't wait its turn this time. It came up through Tobin's future like a hand coming up through water and took hold of him.

The Ash, enormous above him, so close it filled half the sky, pale and pitted like a skull. Sand beneath his own bare feet, higher than any dune he'd ever climbed, falling away on every side into the dark. His own arms, opening; he couldn't stop them opening. Light running out of the feather on his chest, cracking him from the inside, and this time he felt it. Not a beautiful heat. A real one. His skin tightening across his ribs like a drum. His lips splitting. The wet in his eyes boiling, and the world going white and staying white. The smell of honey, and hot metal, and hair, and now, under it, the smell of meat.

He tried to scream and found that the fire had already taken that.

Somewhere below, someone was shouting.

He couldn't hear what. He thought, wildly, through it, that it might be his name. Then he thought: *which one?*

---

Tobin gasped.

A huge, ragged breath, the breath of someone held underwater who has just broken the surface. The colour flooded back into his face, brown and pink and alive. His eyes cleared, white to grey to the ordinary brown of a well-keeper's son.

He blinked up at the sky.

Then he saw Zayn.

I understand why he screamed. Imagine you're seven, and you've just come back from the place where there is nothing, not even yourself. The first thing you see is a face bent over yours, and the face is burning. Golden cracks running up its neck and across its cheeks like a pot about to shatter. Light pouring out of its eyes like tears. Its mouth open in a scream with no sound in it. And a fox beside it, staring at you with one amber eye and one silver.

Tobin screamed for both of them. He twisted out from under Zayn's hands and scrambled backward across the sand until he hit his brother's legs, and he clung to them and kept screaming.

Zayn's light snapped out like a blown lamp.

He knelt there with his hands still raised, slapping at his own arms, his own face, putting out a fire that had already gone. Tears were running down his cheeks. Some were his and some belonged to the vision, and he couldn't have told you which.

And across Tobin's cheeks, where Zayn's fingers had been, lay two thin golden lines, faintly shining.

Bram saw them.

He looked down at his brother, clinging and sobbing, with gold scored across his face like a burn. He looked at Zayn, cracked and glowing and crying light. He looked at the talking fox.

He was fifteen. His brother had forgotten him and been given back, and the price was written in gold across that small face, and Bram understood none of it. He'd been taught his whole life to fear the strange thing at the edge of the village. That's what fear does, in the end. It reaches for the oldest word it knows.

"*Monster!*"

It cracked out of him like a whip.

"Get away from him! You *monster*, get away from my brother, get *away*—"

---

It went into Zayn the way it always did. Like a hook.

He flinched. Just a flinch, as if he'd been slapped. Then he said, quietly, the way he'd said it at the well, the way he'd said it his whole life:

"I'm not a monster."

My tail burned bright. Nobody was looking at it.

They were looking at the light still flickering in the cracks of his skin. And somewhere out in the dark, at the edge of hearing, something howled. A long thin sound that rose and broke like a child crying itself out, and was answered by another.

"He's calling them," said Garrem. His cudgel trembled in his fist. "You hear that? He's *calling* them."

"The grey came when he did," said a woman behind him. "Two nights ago. I saw him. Up on the dune. He was saying something to the wind."

A murmur ran through the crowd: the ugly sound of frightened people discovering they all agreed.

Zayn's hand went to his chest.

Because she was right. He had whispered his name to the wind, and the wind had whispered it back, and the grey had come. Every denial that came to his tongue, the feather burned before it reached his lips.

So he said nothing. And to the people of Harrow's End, his silence sounded exactly like guilt.

"Oszkar," said Garrem.

The headman looked at Zayn a long moment. I'll say this for Oszkar: he didn't enjoy it. He had the hopeless face of a man who has decided to feed one thing to the dark so the dark will leave the rest alone. Villages have been making that bargain forever. It has never once worked.

"Get out, Sandborn," he said. "Get out of Harrow's End, and don't come back."

The first stone came from somewhere at the back of the crowd.

---

It hit Zayn's shoulder. Not hard. A test.

He didn't move. He was still on his knees, looking at Tobin, who had stopped screaming and was staring at him over his brother's leg with huge wet eyes.

The second stone hit his ribs, and the third his cheek, and after that I lost count.

"*Run*," I snapped. "Up, now, you stubborn sack of bones, *run*! I'm not burying you here. The soil's terrible."

He got up. He didn't run. He walked, backward at first, then turned and walked away through the crowd with stones coming at him from every side. The crowd parted, then closed behind him, the way a fist closes. A rock struck his back just below the left shoulder blade. I heard the sound it made. It hit within a finger's width of the old scar, the one he'd carried since he was six.

He staggered. He kept walking.

He never raised his hands. Not once. I want you to know that.

I bit a man's ankle. To the bone. I'm not ashamed of that.

---

At the edge of the village, where the last houses gave way to hard ground, there was a small clay house with an oven behind it.

The door opened as Zayn passed.

Auntie Sabba came out. She was very small, with a face folded like a dried fig. She didn't look at the crowd.

She walked straight up to Zayn and pressed something into his hands.

Flatbread, still warm from the stones, wrapped in a square of faded blue cloth knotted at the top.

He knew that cloth. He stared down at it, then up at her, and I watched him understand.

"Eat, child," said Auntie Sabba. Her voice was dry and thin and fierce. "The sand remembers kindness too."

Then she went back inside and shut the door. I imagine she sat at her table and shook for a long time. I imagine she checked the names above her door, too, and counted them twice.

Zayn stood there holding the bread.

It was the first time in his life anyone in Harrow's End had touched him gently. He stood at the edge of the desert, bleeding from a dozen places, holding that bread against his chest as if it were made of gold.

"Come on," I said, as softly as I've ever said anything. "We can't stay."

He walked out into the dunes. Behind us the lamps of Harrow's End went out one by one, as if the village had closed its eyes. Or as if something were walking from house to house, pinching the wicks.

---

The Hounds found us before midnight.

I heard them first. A low sound under the wind, like breath drawn through teeth. Then a howl, close, from behind the dune to our left. Then another, ahead. Then one directly behind us, so near I felt it in the fur of my tail.

"Fox," Zayn said.

"Yes. I know. Keep walking. Don't run. Running makes them happy, and you don't want to see them happy."

They came over the dune like smoke poured from a jug.

Hollow Hounds. Six of them, shaped like wolves, if a wolf were made of the darkness between the Embers. Long and low, their bodies fraying into black mist at the legs and tail, too many ribs showing through the dark of their sides. Their eyes were coals, the only colour in them. They spread out in a slow circle around us, and the sand didn't dent under their feet, and the air inside the circle went cold enough that Zayn's breath came out white.

They track the Light. Every time a bearer heals, it rings out across the desert like a bell, and the Hounds lift their heads and follow. Zayn had healed a child three hours ago. He might as well have lit a bonfire on a hill and sung.

"What do they want?" he whispered.

"You," I said. My tail glowed. I wished it hadn't.

He pulled his rusty knife. His other hand went to his chest, and he tried, I watched him try, to call the light.

It came grey. A sputter in the cracks of his palms, and then nothing. He was too frightened, too full of stones and the word *monster*. He'd just watched himself burn. Nobody reaches eagerly for the thing that's going to kill them.

The Hounds saw it falter, and their coal-eyes brightened.

The first one sprang.

Zayn slashed at it. The knife passed through its shoulder like a hand through smoke, and the Hound hit him full in the chest and knocked him flat. Its jaws opened above his face. There was no mouth inside. No teeth, no tongue, no throat. Only more dark, going down and down, much farther than the Hound was long, and a cold breathing up out of it like the air from the bottom of a dry well where something has been lying a long time.

And at the bottom of that dark, very small and far away, I heard a voice. A child's voice. *"Who am I?"*

I went for its throat. I'm small. I'm not stupid. My teeth closed on something cold and slippery, and the Hound shook me off with a twist of its head and sent me tumbling down the slope.

Rolling through the sand, I felt something stir in the silver half of me. Something huge and hungry uncoiling, stretching, tasting the cold in the air the way you'd taste a familiar meal. It liked the Hounds. It recognised them. I didn't let it out. Not that night. That's a story for later, and you won't like it, and I like it even less.

As it turned out, I didn't need to.

Because that was when the spear came out of the dark.

---

One moment the Hound was crouched over Zayn's chest with its jaws spread. The next, there was a long shaft of pale wood standing straight up out of its back, and the Hound was coming apart.

It didn't bleed. Shadow things don't. It crumbled into a drift of black ash that fell over Zayn's face and chest like soot, and it was cold, and it smelled faintly of a burned-out lamp.

Then the hoofbeats.

They came from the north, a heavy rolling drumming I felt through my paws before I heard it. A camel came over the crest of the dune at a full run. It was pale, almost white in the Ember-light, like something carved from bone. On its back rode a figure wrapped from head to foot in grey cloth. There was no face to see. Only wrappings, and a veil, and a dark slit where the eyes should be.

The rider leaned from the saddle, pulled the spear from the sand in one smooth movement, and drove it through the second Hound without slowing. The third leapt at the camel's flank. The rider took it out of the air.

The last three ran, their coal-eyes winking out one by one in the dark.

---

The pale camel halted at the top of the dune perhaps twenty paces away.

The rider looked down at us.

Zayn was sitting up in the sand, black ash all over his chest, knife still clutched in his fist. In the dark, the golden cracks along his jaw were faintly glowing.

The rider didn't move or speak. But I could feel the weight of that look from where I lay, and it wasn't a hunter's look. It went on a long time. Longer than it should have. The spear-hand, I noticed, was not quite steady.

"Who are you?" Zayn called.

The wind answered him. Nothing else did.

Then the rider turned the camel and rode away north into the dark, the long spear held upright like a banner. Soon there was nothing left but three drifts of cold black ash and a set of tracks the dunes were already filling.

"Who was that?" said Zayn.

I licked the ash off my paw and said nothing.

Who the rider was is a story for its proper time. If I tell it now you'll skip ahead, and then where will we be? Be patient. He was. Fifteen years, he was patient. Patience is just fear that has learned to sit still.

---

Zayn sat in the sand a long time after that, holding Auntie Sabba's bread in his lap.

The golden cracks on his face and arms were fading slowly, the way colour goes out of a coal. His back was bleeding where the stone had hit. Every so often he turned his hands over, checking they were still skin.

He looked back toward Harrow's End. At its eastern edge the grey lay over the houses like a sheet over a dead man. Only one light still burned in that whole black huddle of clay: a single, stubborn lamp at the far edge of the village, glowing through the walls of a tent hung with herbs and bells.

Hesset was awake.

Zayn looked at that lamp for a long time.

"I saw it," he said at last. He wasn't talking to me. He was saying it to the dark. "When I healed him. I saw myself. On a dune. I was burning, and I couldn't stop it, and I couldn't scream." His voice didn't shake. That was the worst of it. "All those stories. The Phoenix-Born. He *knew*."

I didn't answer that either. But my tail, lying in the sand beside him, glowed a steady, quiet gold.

Zayn stood up. He tucked the bread inside his shirt, against the feather mark, where it was warm. He picked up his knife.

Then he started walking back toward the light. Toward it. They always do. That's the whole trouble with them.

---

*End of Chapter Three*


---

# Chapter Four
## The Bell and the Feather

> *The bell rings for the liar, and the liar is often you. It does not ring for silence. Nothing does.*
> — words stamped around the rim of an old healer's bell

---

His tent was gone.

Not burned. The people of Harrow's End were too frightened that night even for fire. Fire makes light, and light, they had just learned, brought things out of the dark.

So they had torn it down. Somebody had pulled up the stakes and slashed the patched walls in nine colours and trampled the whole thing flat into the sand, the way you'd stamp on a scorpion you weren't brave enough to pick up. His blanket and water skin were gone. His jar of tokens had been smashed, and the tokens taken. Fear doesn't stop people from being practical.

And on the flattened cloth, someone had drawn a circle in ash, with a line through it. The sign the grandmothers scratch over the doors of houses where someone has died of the coughing sickness. *Keep out. Whatever was here is catching.*

Zayn stood looking at it for a while in the dark.

"Well," I said. "At least you won't have to pack. And look, they've labelled it for you. Very thoughtful."

He didn't laugh. He knelt in the wreckage and began to dig through it with his hands, quietly, methodically, the way he dug through the ruins. A scavenger to the last.

He found the twist of waxed paper first. Four dates rolled in honey, the ones he'd been saving. Somebody had stepped on them. They were squashed flat and full of sand. He brushed them off carefully and put them inside his shirt with Auntie Sabba's bread.

Then he found the cloth.

It was buried under the collapsed tent pole: a square of grey fabric, softer than anything else he owned, woven with small strange marks no weaver in Harrow's End had ever been able to name. The desert had wrapped him in it fifteen years ago. He'd slept with it under his head every night of his life.

He held it against his face. Then he went still.

"It smells of smoke," he said.

It always had. He'd simply never noticed, the way you never notice the smell of your own house. Burned cloth, very old, very faint. I didn't tell him what kind of smoke. I didn't tell him that I'd smelled it on the night the desert left him, rolling across the dunes from the place where his family had been, along with another smell, the one that clings to the robes of men who burn their own names.

He folded the cloth small and tucked it away with the rest.

"That's everything," he said.

A knife, a cloth, some bread, four ruined dates. Fifteen years, and that was everything.

---

Hesset was waiting for us.

Of course he was. The lamp was still burning when we reached his tent, the bells turning slowly on their threads. There was tea already poured, two cups. Hesset sat cross-legged on his worn rug, his milky eyes fixed on nothing, his white beard falling over his folded hands.

"Sit," he said, before Zayn had even ducked through the doorway. "You're bleeding on my herbs."

Zayn didn't sit.

He stood in the doorway with the lamplight on his face. The golden cracks along his jaw had faded to a faint shimmer. His shirt was torn at the back and stiff with blood. He was shaking. Not from cold.

He was looking at the lamp. At the little flame in it. He couldn't seem to stop looking at it, and he couldn't seem to bear it, and every time it fluttered, his hand jerked toward his chest.

"You knew," he said.

Hesset turned his face toward the sound. For a long moment he said nothing.

Then he said, "Sit down, child. Let me see to your back. And then you can shout at me, and I promise to listen to every word."

The bells were silent. Every word of it was true.

---

He sat, in the end. I think he was too tired not to.

Hesset's hands were old and dry and very gentle. He peeled the torn shirt away and washed the wound with water from a clay bowl, then packed it with a paste that smelled of bitterleaf and smoke. Zayn hissed through his teeth. He didn't cry out.

"Close to the old one," Hesset murmured, his fingers moving over the pale scar under Zayn's left shoulder blade. "The sand does like to write in the same place twice."

He turned his blind face toward the corner where I sat with my tail curled over my paws.

"And you," he said. "You've finally decided to talk, I hear."

"News travels fast," I said.

"Garrem screams very loudly." Something moved at the corner of his mouth. "It's been a long time, fox."

"Not long enough."

Zayn twisted round to stare at us. "You *know* each other?"

Hesset and I said nothing at the same moment, which was its own kind of answer.

I'll tell you this much, since you're listening and he wasn't: yes. We knew each other. We had known each other for a very long time, longer than the boy could have guessed. The last time Hesset and I had stood in the same place, he'd had eyes, and he'd been younger than Zayn, and he'd been running. He ran so fast that when he finally turned around, the last thing he ever saw was the place he was running from, white and enormous under a white Ash. Then the dark came down over him and never lifted.

That's Hesset's story, and he never told it to the boy. So I won't tell the rest of it to you. Not yet.

"Drink your tea," said Hesset, and pressed the cup into Zayn's hands.

Zayn drank it. Smoke and mint, bitter as always. He hated it as always. It was the first thing all night that felt like home.

Remember that. I asked you to, before.

---

"What am I?" he said at last.

The tea was finished. The lamp had burned lower. Outside, the wind was whispering along the walls of the tent, testing the seams.

"You know the story," said Hesset.

"The Phoenix-Born." Zayn's voice was flat. "The Weaver puts a piece of its light inside a child. The child carries it to the highest dune in the world and sets it free and drives the darkness away."

"Yes."

"That's a story. It's just a story you tell."

Hesset said nothing.

"I'm not one of them," said Zayn. "I'm not—"

He stopped. His face twisted. His hand went to the feather mark and pressed hard, and I saw him bite down on the inside of his cheek until it bled.

It was burning.

He'd been about to say *I'm not one of the Phoenix-Born.* He'd wanted it to be true so badly he'd nearly said it. And the mark had caught the words before they reached his lips and set them alight, and for a moment the smell of scorched skin filled the little tent, sharp and unmistakable, and the bells all trembled on their threads without ringing.

He sat with his eyes squeezed shut, breathing through his nose, waiting for the pain to fade. When he opened them, there was a thin brown mark on his shirt, over his heart, where the fabric had singed.

"Ah," said Hesset softly. "Yes. That's the trouble with you, isn't it? You can't even lie to yourself."

"I saw it," Zayn said. "When I healed Tobin. I saw myself on a dune. Burning." He swallowed. "Is that the story too?"

Hesset's hands went very still in his lap.

"What did you feel?" he asked. Not *what did you see*. *What did you feel.*

"Everything," said Zayn.

The old man didn't answer. He turned his blind face away toward the wall, and for a moment I thought he looked like someone listening to a sound very far off, a sound he had spent three hundred years trying not to hear.

---

Hesset rose. It took him a while. His knees cracked like dry sticks. He felt his way past the hanging bitterleaf to an old wooden chest in the far corner. Zayn had never once seen it open.

Hesset opened it now. I caught a glimpse inside before his body blocked it. Other bundles. Small ones, wrapped in old cloth, laid in a row like loaves. A child's sandal, burned black along one side. I looked away. Some things I've had a lifetime to stop looking at, and I'm still not good at it.

Hesset brought out a bundle wrapped in oiled leather.

"When the desert left you on the edge of this village," he said, "you were wrapped in that grey cloth you carry. And tucked inside it, against your chest, was this. Oszkar's father would have sold it. So I took it before anyone saw, and I've kept it for fifteen years. Open it."

Zayn opened it.

It was a feather.

Gold, or something like gold, as long as his hand and so fine you could see the lamplight through its edges. Every barb was perfect. But it was broken, snapped clean across the middle, the two halves clinging together by a few bent barbs. Along one side the barbs were twisted and crushed, as if a hand had closed on it and tried to wring it in half. At the break, the gold had gone dark. Not tarnish. Grey, the dead flat grey of the lane outside Bram's house.

And it was warm.

Zayn held it in both palms and felt the warmth soak into his skin. It was the same warmth as the mark on his chest. The same shape. The same fine lines. And as he held it, very faintly, it beat. Once. Twice. Out of time with his heart, as if it were keeping time with something else.

"It's broken," he said finally.

"Yes," said Hesset.

"Can it be fixed?"

"Some things mend themselves," the old man said, "when they're ready."

True. The bells didn't stir. My tail stayed bright.

I'll tell you now, because it doesn't spoil anything: it did mend. Slowly. One barb at a time. Every time the boy learned something true about himself, something hard and painful, the feather grew a little more whole. I watched it the whole way. I've watched eleven of them, and they all mend, and they're always whole on the same night, under the same Ash.

A feather is mended so it can fly. That's all I'll say. Every time it healed, I hated it a little more.

---

There was a second gift.

Hesset reached up without looking and unhooked the smallest bell from its thread, no bigger than the tip of Zayn's thumb, bronze worn smooth with age, strung on braided leather.

"This one," Hesset said, "is for you."

He tied it around Zayn's neck himself, old fingers fumbling at the knot.

"You cannot lie. You know that by now. But the rest of the world can, and the rest of the world will. This bell will ring when someone lies to you. Not loudly. Not always in time. But it will ring."

Zayn touched it. It lay against his chest just above the feather mark, cold against the heat of his skin.

"How do I know it works?"

Hesset lifted his cup and smiled. "My tea," he said, "is the finest in the whole desert."

The bell rang.

A small, clear sound, like a single drop of water falling into a silver cup. And at the same moment my tail dimmed, the glow going out of it like a breath blown across a lamp, then coming slowly back.

Zayn looked at the bell. Then at my tail. Then at me.

"Your tail," he said slowly. "When you lie, it…"

"Yes, yes. Glows for the truth, goes dark for a lie. Anybody's lie, not just mine. Very embarrassing. Imagine trying to play cards." I curled the offending thing tighter. "Now you've got two ways of knowing when somebody's lying to you. Most people don't get one."

"And when *you* lie?"

"I don't," I said.

My tail glowed.

He was still staring at it. I could see him going back over everything I'd said that night, word by word, trying to remember whether it had glowed.

It had. All of it.

Later, that would matter more than either of us knew. A fox who never lies only has to do it once.

---

"Would you die for them?" Hesset asked.

He said it quietly. He was turning his empty cup slowly between his palms. He might have been asking whether Zayn wanted more tea.

Zayn looked up. "What?"

"The people of this village. Oszkar, who called you Sandborn. Garrem, who cheated you every week of your life. The children who threw stones when you were six, and the men who threw them tonight. Bram." A pause. "Would you die for them?"

The tent was very quiet. Even the wind outside had stopped, as though it, too, were waiting for the answer.

Zayn opened his mouth.

I'll always see it. He opened his mouth to say *yes*, because it was what a hero would say, what the Phoenix-Born said in every story Hesset had ever told him. The feather burned before the word was out. He flinched, and the singed mark on his shirt darkened.

He closed his mouth, and opened it again, to say *no*. Because they had stoned him. Because they had called him monster and drawn the death-sign on his tent. Because he owed them nothing at all.

That burned too.

And between the two burnings, for just an instant, the lamp flame filled his eyes, and I saw him flinch from it as if it had reached for him. The vision came uninvited. Only a flicker. The white Ash. The heat in his mouth. He made a small sound, like a child woken from a nightmare who doesn't yet know he's awake.

He sat with his hand pressed hard against his chest, eyes wet, mouth half open. Neither word would come.

"I don't know," he whispered finally.

My tail glowed. The bell was silent.

Hesset nodded slowly, as if that were the answer he'd expected, and dreaded.

"Good," he said. "That's honest. Hold on to it. It won't be your answer forever."

"Why are you asking me that?"

Hesset didn't answer. He turned the empty cup in his hands, round and round, round and round, the way the dunes turn, the way the Ash turns. The way everything out here comes back to where it started.

---

It was almost dawn when Zayn asked the question that mattered.

"Where do I go?"

"The City of Glass," said Hesset.

Zayn blinked. "That's a story too."

"Most things are, until you walk into them. It lies deep in the desert, farther than anyone from Harrow's End has ever gone. It's the place where the first bearer said no to the Light, and the sand around him melted into glass." His blind eyes moved, as though following something a long way off. "The walls there remember. Everything you need to know about what you are is written on them. The faces of the ones who came before you. Older than me. Older than the fox, almost."

"Steady on," I said.

"How do I find it?"

"You don't. You walk, and the dunes find it for you. They'll open a way if you're meant to reach it."

Zayn turned the broken feather over in his hands. "Will you come with me?"

Something passed across the old man's face. Quick, gone almost before I saw it. But I've known Hesset a long time, and I knew what it was. Shame. And under the shame, older and worse, fear.

"I don't leave the edge of the desert," he said. "I can't. The desert and I have an agreement. I made it a long time ago, and I'm still paying for it."

True. The bell didn't stir.

Zayn was quiet. Then he asked it. The same question he'd asked two nights ago, when his tent had still been standing.

"What happens to them? The Phoenix-Born. When they get to the dune and set the light free. What happens to them, after?"

I held my breath.

And Hesset, who had sat among his truth bells for more years than the boy could have guessed and never told a lie that mattered, looked at him with his blind eyes and said:

"Go to the City of Glass. The answers there are older than mine, and truer. You'll understand it better when you see it written."

The bell didn't ring.

Of course it didn't. Every word was true. My tail stayed gold. There was nothing in it for either of us to catch.

But I looked at Hesset across that tent, and Hesset, who could not see me, turned his face toward me anyway. And we both knew.

He had not lied to the boy. He had done something worse. He'd stood beside the truth with a cloth over it and let the boy walk past. He'd sent a child who can't read to a city made of writing, and he knew exactly what was written there.

That night, in that tent, an old man I had once loved like a son pushed a fifteen-year-old boy into the gap between *true* and *the whole truth*, and wished him well.

I didn't say anything either. Remember that, when you're judging him. I was sitting right there, and I could have said it, and I didn't. I never have. Eleven times, I never have.

---

"May the sand remember you," said Hesset at the tent flap.

The Embers were fading. The eastern sky was turning the deep, bruised blue that comes just before the Fire. Beyond the clay houses of Harrow's End the grey lay low and flat along the edge of the world. It hadn't moved in the night. It hadn't needed to. Along its edge, quite still, stood a line of small grey shapes. From that distance you might have taken them for posts. They were all facing the tent.

"Will it follow me?" Zayn asked. "If I go. Will it leave them alone?"

"I don't know," said Hesset.

The bell was silent. He truly didn't.

Zayn nodded. Then he did something I'd never seen him do in fifteen years. He stepped forward and put his arms around the old man, quickly and clumsily, like someone who's never been taught how. Hesset went very still. Then his dry hands came up and rested on the boy's back, either side of the new wound, and held there a moment too long. The way you hold someone at a graveside.

"Go," Hesset said roughly. "Before I make you drink more tea."

We went.

---

We walked north into the deep desert as the dunes were finishing their night's work.

If you have never seen the Destiny Desert move, I'm sorry for you, and also not. It's the most beautiful thing in the world, and one of the most frightening. In that last grey hour before dawn the dunes were settling into their new places. Whole hills slid slowly sideways with a sound like a vast animal breathing in its sleep. Ridges folded. Hollows opened. Paths that had been there yesterday were gone, and new ones lay smooth and fresh between the dunes, untouched by any foot.

One of those new paths opened right in front of us.

It ran straight out from the edge of Harrow's End, between two great golden slopes, smooth as a swept floor. It hadn't been there an hour ago. Zayn stopped at the start of it and stared.

"It's for me," he said.

"Apparently," I said.

It was very straight. Desert paths are never straight. This one went north like a line drawn by a ruler, like a road laid down to a single place by someone who did not intend you to wander. Like a throat.

He looked back once.

Harrow's End sat behind us in the blue light, small and dark and huddled around its well. He could make out the flattened patch where his tent had been. At the far edge, a thread of smoke rose from Hesset's tent straight up into the still air.

And along the eastern side of the village, grey.

He touched the feather mark. He touched the bell. He touched the folded grey cloth inside his shirt, where the broken feather now lay wrapped against Auntie Sabba's bread, beating faintly, out of time.

"The sand remembers," he whispered.
