Then he turned his back on the only home he'd ever had and walked out into the desert, and the dunes closed softly behind us. I listened for it, and I heard it: a long, slow, satisfied sound, like a mouth closing.

---

About an hour later, as the Fire came up red over the eastern dunes, I found a scorpion.

It was a fat one, the colour of old copper, scuttling out from under a stone to warm itself in the first light. I caught it neatly behind the stinger and sat down in the middle of the path to eat it.

I took my time. I chewed with my mouth open. The shell made a wonderful noise, a wet, splintering crunch that echoed off the dunes, and I made sure to crunch every single leg individually.

Zayn stopped and turned around.

"Do you have to?" he said.

"I do, actually," I said, with my mouth full. "It's very nutritious. You need building up. You'll want your strength for the end."

"That's disgusting."

"*You're* disgusting. You smell like bitterleaf and old blood. At least my breakfast was alive this morning."

He stared at me. And for a moment, just a moment, something happened at the very corner of his mouth. It wasn't a smile, not yet. He wouldn't truly laugh for a long time, not until he met someone who could make him. But it was the place a smile would go, one day, if things went well.

Things never go well. But they go, and sometimes there are smiles along the way, and I've learned to take those where I find them, like water under a stone.

He turned and kept walking. I finished my scorpion, and licked my whiskers, and trotted after him.

My tail was glowing. I'll let you work out why. And I'll tell you that I didn't say the other thing, the thing I wanted to say, that this time would be different. I knew what my tail would do.

---

*End of Chapter Four*


---

# PART TWO: THE DESERT THAT WRITES


---

# Chapter Five
## Where the Dunes Speak

> *The sand writes; the wise read; the rest are buried. The sand writes on them too.*
> — a proverb of the old caravan scribes

---

The desert doesn't care about you. That's the first thing you learn out there, and the last thing most people forget.

It isn't cruel. Cruelty takes attention. The desert simply goes on being what it is, and if you happen to die in it, it covers you up with the same patience it covers everything else. Then, a few hundred years later, it uncovers you again, just to see. I've seen faces come up after the night's shifting, dry and perfectly kept, mouths open, as if they'd been calling for help the whole time.

Zayn knew the desert. It was the one advantage he had.

He knew to walk in the cool hours and lie up through the white glare of noon in the shadow of a dune's steep side, scarf over his face. He knew that under a tuft of thornweed there was sometimes damp sand, and if you dug and waited, a little brown water would seep into the hole. And he knew to lick the dew from the undersides of flat stones at dawn.

"That's revolting," I said, the first morning I saw him do it.

He wiped his mouth. "You eat scorpions."

"Scorpions are a *meal*. That is a *rock*."

"It's water."

I had no answer to that.

---

We walked for six days.

The straight path lasted only the first morning. After that, every dawn the way ahead looked different. Zayn would stand on the highest ridge he could find and turn slowly in a circle until something told him which way to go. A tug in the feather mark. Some mornings he stood there a long time, and I knew he was wondering whether the thing pulling him was the Weaver or something else, and whether there was a difference.

He asked me questions. Not many. He'd never had anyone to ask before.

"How old are you?"

"Older than you."

My tail glowed. He frowned at it.

"How much older?"

"Much."

It glowed again. He gave up on that one.

"Do you have a name?"

Nobody had asked me that in a very long time. The Boy of Salt had asked, once, and said it over and over on the way up the dune, as if it would help. "Kiri," I said.

He tried it under his breath. *Kiri.* He didn't say it to the wind. He'd learned that much. When the wind came round from the north that evening, he flinched at every gust, waiting for it to say his own name back. It didn't. The waiting was worse.

"Why did you watch me? All those years. In the village."

"Because somebody had to," I said.

That one glowed brightest of all, and he didn't ask anything else for the rest of the day.

He touched the bell a lot, and the feather mark, and at night the folded cloth where the broken feather lay, beating its slow wrong beat.

He didn't sleep much. When he did, he woke with his hands slapping at his chest, putting out a fire. Once he sat up and said, perfectly clearly, "It's so high up," and didn't remember it in the morning. I did.

On the third night he finished Auntie Sabba's bread. He ate it very slowly. The four squashed dates he left where they were. He wasn't there yet. He knew harder days were coming.

He was right about that, at least.

---

The dunes spoke on the fifth night.

We'd stopped at the foot of a long curving ridge, and Zayn had scooped a hollow in the sand to sleep in. The Ash had risen late and lopsided. I lay with my nose on my paws and watched him not sleep.

Then the sand began to move.

Something small. Close. On the long smooth face of the dune in front of us, the sand was rippling, the way the skin of your arm ripples when something moves under it. Fine lines were drawing themselves across the slope. Curves and hooks and straight strokes, crossing and joining, and they weren't being drawn on the sand. They were being pushed up from beneath it, from inside the dune, as if by a single fingertip moving very slowly just under the surface. As they formed, they began to glow.

Gold.

Zayn sat up so fast he sprayed sand everywhere.

The marks went on writing themselves. A line, then a second line beneath it, glowing across the dark face of the dune like Embers fallen out of the sky. They had the shape of meaning, the way a voice in the next room has the shape of words even when you can't make them out.

I could hear it, too. A faint dry whisper as the strokes moved, like a quill on old paper, like many quills, as if a great many hands were writing at once under the sand, all of them patient, all of them in the dark.

Zayn got up and walked toward it. He looked for a long, long time.

Then he said, very quietly, "I can't read."

Reading was for rich people. Nobody had ever thought to teach a nameless scavenger his letters, and it had never mattered before.

It mattered now. I could see it go into him like the word *monster* did.

"Kiri," he said. "What does it say?"

I didn't answer.

He turned. "You can read it. Can't you."

"Yes."

My tail glowed. There was no point lying about that.

"Then tell me what it says."

"No."

In the light from the dune his face was very young and very angry. "*Why not?*"

"Because it isn't written for me," I said. "The Weaver writes the sands, and the bearer reads them. Never the fox. If I read it to you, it's just a fox telling you things."

"It's written for me," he said, "in a way I can't understand. So it's written for nobody."

He said it flatly. That was worse.

"The Weaver forgot me in the sand when I was a baby," he went on. "And now it writes me letters I can't read. That's very funny. That's the funniest thing I've ever heard."

He turned his back on the dune, walked to his hollow and lay down in it, facing away from the light.

The writing glowed on behind him for a long time. The whispering went on with it. Then, slowly, the sand smoothed itself over, and the gold faded, and the sound stopped. Except that just before it stopped, the strokes under the sand went still all at once, every one of them, the way a room goes quiet when someone it was talking about walks in.

---

I'll tell you something now that I didn't tell him.

I read it. Of course I did. I've been reading the sands since before there were cities in this desert.

The bottom line was short. A single word, written large.

I'm not going to tell you what it was. It wasn't mine to read, and it isn't mine to tell. But I'll tell you this: I'd seen that line before. Not that word. But that line, in that place on the dune, at the bottom, written large. Eleven times I had seen it, over eleven different children, and each time the word was different, and each time it meant the same thing.

I lay awake the rest of that night with my nose on my paws, watching the place where it had been, and I don't think I've ever hated the Weaver quite so much. It writes like someone who loves you. It writes like that every time.

If he'd been able to read it that night, I wonder whether anything would have been different.

No. I know better. Nothing would have been different. It never is. That's not despair, by the way. That's arithmetic.

---

The Sandwraith came the next afternoon.

We were crossing a wide flat basin between two lines of dunes, the kind of place where the sand lies smooth and pale as spilled flour. It was the hottest hour. There was no shade in that basin, and Zayn had decided to push across.

I felt it first. A tremor under my paws. Small. Then again, stronger.

Then a sound, coming up through the sand. *Tonk. Tonk. Tonk.*

A camel bell.

There were no camels in that basin. There was nothing in that basin but us.

"Don't move," I said.

He stopped.

The sand ahead of us bulged.

It rose in a long low ridge perhaps forty paces long, like a blanket over something moving. It sank. It rose again, closer, and the sand on top of it darkened, the pale gold going black as if something were soaking up through it. The bell rang inside it. *Tonk. Tonk.*

"What is that?" Zayn whispered.

"Run," I said. He ran.

The ground behind us *erupted*.

It came up out of the basin like a fountain of black sand, rearing, impossibly long, a serpent made of darkness and dust. No scales. No eyes. Its body was the black sand itself, endlessly pouring and re-forming, hissing as it moved, and where its head should have been there was a wide blunt shape that opened in the middle like a split seam.

And through the pouring black of its body, as it rose, I saw what it had eaten.

A camel's ribcage, turning slowly inside it, the bell still on its bridle. A man's hand, brown and dried, the fingers still curled around the strap of a water skin. A face, pressed against the inside of the sand as it poured past, the mouth open in exactly the shape of the faces the desert sometimes gives back. They went round and round inside it like things in a slow river, and none of them would ever come out.

It was taller than any house in Harrow's End. It was still coming up out of the ground.

Sandwraith. They swim beneath the dunes the way fish swim beneath water, and when they find something warm, they come up and swallow it, and keep it, and go back down.

Zayn had something warm inside him. Something that, to every creature of the Hollow, burned like a beacon.

It came down at us like a falling tower.

Zayn threw himself sideways. The wraith struck where he'd been and went straight into the ground, and the basin heaved and knocked him flat. He came up spitting, knife in hand.

"Keep *running*!" I shrieked.

It burst up in front of him, a black wall of hissing dust with the bell clanking somewhere inside, and he skidded, fell, scrambled back. It was herding him. The way a cat herds a mouse into a corner. It knew this basin. He didn't.

The feather mark was burning through his shirt. He held out his hand, and the light came.

Grey.

He was terrified. A thin sputtering light flickering in his palm like a candle in a draught. The wraith didn't even pause. It reared up over him, blotting out the Fire, the split in its head opening wide, and the air that came out of it was cold and wet and smelled of the bottom of an old well, and of the people in it.

And that was when I did something stupid.

---

I went for it.

I'm not a fighter. I've survived this long by being somewhere else when the teeth come out. But I didn't think. I ran straight up the loose sand at the base of its body and bit it.

It tasted of nothing. Dust and cold. And, very faintly, of the people inside.

The wraith twisted. A coil of black sand whipped round and caught me, pouring and squeezing at once. I couldn't breathe. I felt myself being pulled down, toward the darkness under the dunes where the thing lived, and something brushed against my side in the black, and it was a hand. It was dry and light and it closed, gently, on my fur, the way you'd hold on to someone who'd just arrived. Welcoming me in.

I heard Zayn shout my name.

Through a gap in the black sand, I saw him get to his feet.

And I saw the light change.

It's the same every time. One moment it was grey, the light of a boy afraid for himself. The next, it wasn't, because he wasn't afraid for himself any more. He was looking at a small rude fox being dragged into the sand, and he forgot his own fear the way you forget a splinter when the house is on fire.

The light went gold.

It roared up out of his chest and out of his hands, and he didn't throw it, exactly. He *reached* for me, and the light reached with him.

It hit the Sandwraith square in the body.

Where the gold touched the black sand, the sand stopped pouring. It turned glassy and dark and then, with a sound like a thousand tiny bells breaking, it shattered. The coil around me burst apart, and the dry hand let go of me last. I fell, landed hard, rolled. Above me, the light raced up the wraith's body the way fire races up a dry palm. The whole vast shape shrieked, high and thin, and in that shriek, I swear to you, there were voices. Many. Then it collapsed in a slithering rain of black glass and ash that pattered over the basin for a long time.

What it had been keeping fell with it. Bones. The bell, rolling to a stop at Zayn's feet, ringing once, then quiet. The dried hand, still holding its water skin.

When it was over, Zayn was on his knees.

Golden cracks ran up his forearms and down his neck. And for a moment his eyes went far away and white, and his mouth opened, and I knew he was seeing it again, the Ash, the height, the heat. It had come without a healing, this time. Just from using the Light. It was learning the way in.

He came back. He looked at me.

"Are you hurt?" he said.

I spat out a mouthful of sand. "*Am I hurt.* I've just been half eaten by a sand worm and cuddled by a dead man, and you want to know if I'm *hurt*."

"Are you?"

"No," I said.

My tail glowed. He let out a long breath and sat back on his heels.

"Good," he said.

And I had to look away from him at the black glass scattered across the sand, because there was something in my eye. Sand, probably. There was a great deal of sand.

---

He didn't hear the humming until it was very close. Neither did I. I'll never forgive myself for that.

Light and noise bring things. We should have moved. Instead we sat among the black glass, catching our breath, and the first I knew of it was a shadow falling across Zayn's shoulders, and a smell. Burned cloth. And under it something sharper and stranger, like hair singed in a candle, like ink boiled off a page. I've smelled it in a great many places. It's the smell of a name being burned.

He looked up.

A figure stood on the ridge above us, against the white glare of the Fire.

Grey robes, wrapped close, the hems stirring in the hot wind. A hood. And under the hood, where a face should have been, a mask. Smooth and white and perfectly blank. Two narrow slits for the eyes, and behind them, darkness. Where the edge of the mask met the skin of his neck, the skin was raw and red and puckered, as though the mask had been worn so long it had begun to grow there, and every time someone had tried to lift it the face had come with it.

In each hand the figure held a blade, long and thin and slightly curved, and grey. Not the grey of steel. The grey of the Hollow, dull and dead. They didn't shine in the Fire at all. Looking at them was like trying to remember a dream.

The Unnamed. That's what they call themselves, the men in white masks. They burn their own names in a fire and take numbers instead, and they worship forgetting the way other people worship the Weaver. They hunt the bearer the way the Hounds do. But the Hounds are animals. The Unnamed know exactly what they're doing, and the worst of them do it kindly.

This one, we'd learn later, they called Brother Seven.

He came down the slope.

---

He moved like water running downhill. No wasted step, no sound. Zayn scrambled up with his rusty knife out before the first blade came.

He blocked it. More luck than skill. Steel met grey with a dull sound, like a stone dropped on sand. The second blade was already coming low, at his ribs. He twisted. It missed his side by a breath and opened his sleeve from wrist to elbow.

It didn't touch his skin. It didn't need to.

Zayn staggered. For an instant his face went blank and puzzled, as if he'd walked into a room and forgotten why. I saw him reach for something in his mind and close his hand on nothing.

That's what the grey blades do. They don't cut flesh so much as memory. Even a whisper of one, passing close, takes something with it, and it doesn't leave a hole you can feel. It leaves smooth sand.

He swung at the masked figure, wild and desperate. Brother Seven stepped inside the swing, hooked his ankle, and put him on his back. One grey blade came down and rested against his throat.

Zayn froze.

The feather mark was blazing under his shirt, and there was light in his hands, but it was grey again, flickering, useless. He'd spent everything on the wraith. And grey blades, I should tell you, are the only thing in the world that can cut the Light.

I flew at the figure's legs. A knee caught me in the ribs and sent me tumbling. When I got up, nothing had changed. Zayn on his back. The white mask looking down. The blade against his throat, and a thin line of grey spreading on the skin beneath it, like frost on a window.

Brother Seven didn't move.

---

He didn't press down. He didn't draw back. He stood there with one blade at the boy's throat and the other loose at his side, looking at Zayn through the slits of that blank white mask. I couldn't see his eyes. I don't know what was in them. I'm not sure there was anything left in them to see.

It went on long enough for the hot wind to drop.

And then, under the mask, very softly, he began to hum.

You could barely hear it over the hiss of settling sand. A few notes, low and slow, climbing. A pause. The same notes again, a little higher, like something lifting off the ground. Like wings. It came out of a mask with no mouth, as if from somewhere very far back in him, a room he'd been locked out of.

Zayn's eyes went wide. I don't think he knew why. But something in his face changed, the way the grey woman's eyes had changed in Harrow's End. Something long buried turning over in its sleep.

I knew that song.

I'm not going to tell you who used to sing it, or to whom. Not yet. I sat in the sand with my fur standing up along my spine, and I said nothing. Not then, and not for a long, long time.

Add it to the list. It's getting long, I know. Everything I don't say is a stone in my belly.

The humming stopped.

Brother Seven took the blade away from Zayn's throat.

He stepped back. One step, then another. The blank white mask tilted slightly to one side, as if he were asking himself a question and had forgotten what questions were for.

His hand came up to the edge of the mask. Touched the raw skin there. Pulled, a little, the way you'd worry at a scab.

Then he let go, and turned, and walked away up the ridge, and over it, and was gone.

---

Zayn lay on his back in the sand a long time, staring at the sky.

"He let me go," he said finally.

"Yes."

"*Why?*"

"I don't know," I said.

My tail glowed. True, as far as it went. I knew a great many things about that masked figure. But why he lowered his blade that day, and would again and again, I truly didn't know.

Zayn turned his head and looked at me. He was learning to listen to the shape of what I said.

"Do you know who he is?"

I got up and shook the sand out of my fur, very thoroughly, for a long time.

"It's going to be dark soon," I said. "We should find somewhere to sleep that isn't the middle of a wraith's larder."

He looked at me a moment longer. I saw him decide not to push.

He got up. His sleeve flapped open where the grey blade had cut it. There was a pale mark on his throat where the other had rested, like a line of old frost. He picked up his knife, and started walking.

That night, by a fire of dry thornweed, he sat for a long time with his knees drawn up, frowning into the flames.

"Kiri," he said. "There's something missing."

"Your sense, mostly. Your tent."

"No. In my head." He pressed the heel of his hand against his forehead. "There's a place where something was. I keep going back to it. I don't know what it was. I don't know if it was important." He looked at me, and his eyes were frightened in a way the wraith hadn't made them. "How can I miss something if I don't know what it is?"

I knew what it was. It was the taste of Hesset's tea. Smoke and mint. He'd hated it and loved it every night for years, and now it was gone, and he would never know it had been there. They always start with small things.

"Go to sleep," I said.

He didn't. He lay on his side with his back to the fire, and after a while, very softly, almost without meaning to, he began to hum. A few notes, low and slow, climbing.

He got it wrong. He didn't know the rest.

I lay beside him and didn't correct him, and out in the dark, beyond the edge of the firelight, the sand shifted very slightly, as if something under it had turned over to listen.

---

*End of Chapter Five*


---

# Chapter Six
## The Valley of Echoes

> *Shout your name in the valley, and it will shout back your fear. Whisper it, and it will learn it.*
> — a warning scratched on a cairn at the valley's mouth, in a hand no one living can read

---

The dunes ended on the ninth day, and the ground fell open like a wound.

One moment we were walking a long golden ridge. The next, the sand simply stopped, the way skin stops at the lip of a cut, and below us was rock. Red rock, the colour of old blood that has been left to dry in a bowl, split down the middle by a canyon so deep the Fire couldn't reach the bottom of it.

It ran north as far as I could see. The walls were sheer and banded carved by some long-dead river into ledges and hollows and curved shapes like the insides of ears. A valley made of ears.

At its mouth, half buried in drifted sand, stood a cairn of piled stones with something scratched into the topmost one. Around the base of the cairn lay a ring of small things. A sandal. A wooden spoon. A child's clay whistle with the end bitten off. Offerings, or things dropped by people running. The desert doesn't label its exhibits.

Zayn looked at the scratches for a while. Then he looked away. He'd stopped asking me to read things for him since the night the dunes wrote at him and he'd stood there like a cow in front of a sermon.

"We have to go through," he said. "Don't we."

It wasn't a question. The feather mark was tugging north, straight down into that red throat of stone. And on every other side, the dunes had closed in during the night, steep and soft and very patient, the way a crowd closes in at a hanging.

"The desert does like to make its point," I said. "Usually with someone's head."

---

"Listen to me," I told him, at the top of the path that wound down into the canyon. "Listen properly, for once in your short life. This is the Valley of Echoes."

"Another story?"

"Every story is a story until it eats you. Listen. Whatever you do down there, don't talk. Don't shout. Don't call out. If you trip, swear inside your head. If you see something *else*, keep that to yourself even harder."

"Why?"

"Because the valley answers," I said. "Not with your voice. With what's underneath it. The things you're most afraid of. The things you'd never say out loud." I paused. "And it remembers what it hears. If you say your name down there, little scavenger, the valley keeps it. It'll be calling you by it long after you've gone. People have heard their own names coming up out of this canyon fifty years after the owners died."

He looked at me. My tail was glowing in the shade of the canyon's lip, steady and bright.

"Have you been here before?" he asked.

"Yes."

"What did it say to you?"

I started down the path without answering. After a moment, I heard him follow.

---

It was cold at the bottom.

Not cool. *Cold*, the damp, creeping cold of a cellar, which is wrong in a desert the way a smile is wrong on a corpse. The air smelled of stone and standing water and, underneath that, something sweetish and old that I decided not to name for him. Somewhere ahead, something dripped, *tik, tik, tik*, and the sound came back from everywhere at once, like a great many small fingers tapping on the stone.

We walked in silence.

The walls rose higher as we went. The sky narrowed to a ribbon, like the light at the top of a well you've fallen down. Our footsteps echoed, every one, sent back to us faintly from the stone. They came back a little *late*. And there were a few more of them than we'd made.

Zayn noticed. He stopped once, and the footsteps went on for three more paces without him, and then they stopped too, as if whoever was making them had also paused to listen.

He looked at me. I looked at the path. We kept walking.

In the hollows of the walls, as the morning wore on, I began to see the marks. Handprints, some of them. Long, five-fingered streaks in the soft red stone, as if someone had walked along this ledge with their hand on the wall, slowly sinking. And here and there, scratched into the rock, the same short word over and over, in letters neither of us could read. I didn't need to read it. I'd seen people write it before. It's what people write when they're trying to keep hold of their name.

For most of the morning, the silence held.

Then, around midday, the path narrowed to a ledge along the canyon wall, with a long drop to a dry streambed on one side. There were small bones down there. And a loose stone turned under Zayn's foot.

He caught himself. He didn't fall. But he gasped, the small sharp intake of breath anyone would make, and with it, before he could stop himself, came a sound.

"*Ah*—"

It went up the canyon wall like a bird let out of a box.

We both froze.

The echo came back. It should have come back as *ah*. That's what echoes do. They return what you give them.

This one didn't.

"*Thrown away*," said the valley.

---

It wasn't loud. That was almost the worst of it. It was soft, like someone whispering an inch behind his ear. And it was in his own voice. Zayn's voice, but younger. The voice of a very small child who has been crying for a long time and has stopped expecting anyone to come.

"*Thrown away*," it said again, from farther up the canyon, and again, fainter, from somewhere below us, down among the little bones. "*Thrown away. Thrown away.*"

Zayn's hand went to his chest.

I could see him trying not to breathe. He was pressing so hard on the feather mark that his knuckles had gone white as the bones below.

"*Mistake*," said the valley.

This voice was different. Older. Deeper. A man's voice, and I didn't recognise it. But it said the word the way you'd say it about a pot that had come out of the kiln cracked. Without anger. A thing not worth the water it would cost to keep.

"*Mistake. Mistake. Should have left him in the sand. Should have left him for the dogs.*"

"Keep walking," I breathed. As quietly as a fox can breathe, which is very quietly indeed. "Don't answer it. *Walk.*"

He walked. One foot in front of the other, with the voices coming from everywhere. Something moved in a hollow in the rock ahead of us. A shape the size of a crouching man, pressed back into the dark of the hollow, with its face turned to the wall. As we passed, it didn't move. But I heard it whisper, under the other whispers, in a voice that was nobody's at all: "*…don't answer it, walk, don't answer it, walk…*"

My words. Already. The valley had taken them and was wearing them.

"*No name*," said the valley. "*No name. Nobody's. Nothing.*"

Then came a voice I *did* recognise. Everyone in Harrow's End would have recognised it. Sharp-tongued and cracking, a voice Zayn had heard at the well every day of his life.

"*Monster.*"

Zayn stopped walking.

---

"*Monster*," said Bram's voice, high up on the canyon wall. And then other voices joined it, dozens of them, from every ledge and ear-shaped hollow and crack in the stone. The whole village of Harrow's End, shouting together the way they had shouted with the stones in their hands. Oszkar. Garrem. Hadric at his well. Little children who had learned the word before their letters.

"*Monster. Monster. Monster. Monster.*"

And under it, very faintly, the thud of stones. Over and over. Landing on something soft.

He turned to face it.

I knew what was going to happen. I'd known since the top of the path. Some things you can warn a person against all you like. They'll do them anyway, because some people would rather die than let the thing stand unanswered. In my experience, they usually get the chance.

"*I'm not!*" Zayn shouted.

It tore out of him, loud and raw. The valley caught it in its red mouth and turned it inside out like a glove and threw it back.

"*NOT A HERO*," the valley roared. "*NOT A HERO. NOT A HERO. NOBODY. NOBODY. FORGOTTEN.*"

It was his own voice again. Not a child's now. His own, exactly as it was, and that was the cruellest thing of all. Because none of the others had been true. Not *thrown away*, not *mistake*, not *monster*. Those were other people's words. But this one was *his*. It was the fear he'd kept in the deepest cellar in him, under the dreams of being a hero, under everything.

That he'd do it all, every terrible thing, and in the end it wouldn't matter. Nobody would remember him at all.

"*FORGOTTEN*," howled the valley. "*FORGOTTEN. FORGOTTEN. FORGOTTEN.*"

And then it did something I had never heard it do, in all the times I'd passed through. It said his name.

Not *Sandborn*. Not *the Nameless*. The name he'd given himself at seven, sitting on the lip of a well. The name he had never once said to the wind.

"*Zayn*," the valley whispered, almost tenderly, from every wall at once. "*Zayn. Zayn. Zayyyn.*" Tasting it, the way a dog learns a scent. "*Who was Zayn? Nobody. Nobody remembers Zayn.*"

He went down on his knees on the ledge, both hands over his ears. It didn't help. It was coming from inside his hands.

---

I bit him.

Not hard. On the wrist, just enough to sting. He yelped, and the yelp went up the canyon wall too, and the valley sent back something wordless and wet, like someone crying with their face pressed into the dirt.

"Up," I said. I said it very low, almost no voice at all. "Get up and walk, or I'll bite you somewhere that'll make it hard to sit for a week, and then you'll have something *real* to cry about."

He got up. He walked.

And I'll tell you something I'm not proud of. When I'd said *up*, I'd said it a fraction louder than I meant to.

The valley heard me.

"*Again*," it whispered.

It was my voice. Old and thin and very, very tired.

"*Again. Again. Again.*" And then, so faint I've sometimes told myself I imagined it: "*Too late. Too late. You're always too late.*"

Zayn turned his head and looked at me.

I didn't look back at him. I kept my eyes on the path and my mouth shut, and I walked, and after a while he walked too.

Behind us, as we went, I heard the valley practising. Trying out the two names it had learned that day, side by side, the way a child practises new words. *Zayn. Kiri. Zayn. Kiri.* Over and over. Learning them, for later.

He never asked me about *again*. I've always been grateful for that. I'm not sure what I'd have told him.

---

The valley let us go a little before sunset.

The walls opened out slowly, the way a fist unclenches when its owner has finally died. The canyon spilled us out onto a wide stony plain where a few thorn trees grew crooked and black against the sky, all bent leaning away from the valley's mouth. There was a pool under an overhang of rock. Real water, brown and cold and tasting of iron, like a split lip. Zayn drank until he was sick, then drank again, more carefully.

Neither of us said anything for a long time.

The Fire went down. The Embers came out. The Ash rose late again, a little thinner than it had been the night before, a pale curl of white low over the canyon's mouth like a fingernail paring.

Zayn sat with his back against a rock and the grey cloth from Harrow's End wrapped round his shoulders. Very quietly, he said his own name under his breath. Once. Testing. As if checking it was still there.

From the mouth of the valley, faint across the stony plain, something said it back.

He didn't say it again.

"Is it true?" he said at last, barely above a whisper.

"Is what true?"

"What it said. That I'll be—"

He stopped. He couldn't say the word. Not because it would burn, but because he was afraid it wouldn't.

"The valley doesn't tell the truth," I said. "It tells you what you're afraid of. That isn't the same thing."

My tail glowed. He looked at it for a long time, as if he were deciding whether to believe it. My tail glows for things that are *true*. It has never once glowed for things that are *enough*.

Then he lay down on the stony ground, pulled the cloth up over his ears, and slept.

---

He dreamed.

He was standing on a plain, and the plain was grey.

Not the grey of the Hollow at the edge of Harrow's End, not exactly. Softer. A flat, endless grey that stretched to every horizon. No sky. No sound. Not even his heartbeat. He put his hand to his chest to feel for it, and found nothing there to feel, and the hand went on sinking a little way into himself, as if he were made of ash, before he snatched it back.

And there were sheep.

They moved slowly across the plain around him, dozens of them, grazing on nothing. They were made of grey mist. When they turned their heads he could see through them. They had no eyes, but their mouths kept chewing, and when one came close he heard that it was chewing words. Tiny, mumbled, half-swallowed words. *Mother. Water. Home. My name is—* and then the grinding of teeth, and the word was gone.

Among them, leaning on a long crook, stood a boy.

He was small. Smaller than Zayn. Twelve or thirteen, thin as a stick, in a patched brown robe that was the only colour in that whole grey world. His feet were bare. His face was turned away.

"They always come back," the boy said. "Have you noticed? No matter how far they wander. You just stand still, and wait, and they come back to you. Everything comes back to the Hollow in the end."

His voice was soft and ordinary. A little hoarse, as if he hadn't used it in a very long time.

Zayn tried to speak, and found he could.

"Who are you?"

The boy thought about it. He frowned, the way you'd frown trying to remember where you'd put something down.

"I don't remember," he said at last. He didn't sound upset. "I used to. I'm almost sure I used to." He shrugged his thin shoulders. "It doesn't hurt, after a while. Not having one. It's like taking off a sandal that's been rubbing."

He turned his head.

His face was a boy's face, thin and brown and dusty, with a narrow chin and a crooked front tooth. All except his eyes. Where his eyes should have been, there were two small round holes, very neat, as if someone had pressed them out with a thumb. Through them Zayn could see the grey. The endless, empty grey, going on forever inside his head.

"I heard you in the valley," the boy said gently. "*Forgotten.* You're right to be afraid of it. You should be. I'm sorry. I wish someone had been honest with me, when I was where you are."

"I don't know what you mean."

"Don't you?" The boy smiled. It was a kind smile. That was the terrible thing. The kindest smile anyone had given Zayn in his whole life, except perhaps Auntie Sabba's at the edge of the village. "They've told you the stories. The Phoenix-Born. They rise like the fire-bird, they save everyone, they're remembered forever." He shook his head slowly. "No one remembers the bearers."

The grey sheep had stopped chewing. They had all turned their eyeless faces toward Zayn.

"They light lanterns for them once a year, out in the towns," the boy said. "On the Night of Embers. A whole sky full of little lights. And not one person holding a lantern could tell you a single bearer's name. Not their faces. Not whether they screamed at the end." He tilted his head. "They did, you know. Some of them. You'll want to know that, later, when it's your turn. You won't be the first to scream."

"That isn't true."

The words came out of Zayn, and as they did, the feather mark gave a single sharp throb. Not quite a burn. Not quite. A warning.

The boy saw it. His hollow eyes went to the mark, and something in the grey behind them stirred, like a fish turning in deep water.

"Isn't it?" he said softly. "You can't even say it without it hurting."

He came closer. He walked without leaving footprints, and stopped an arm's length away and looked up at Zayn with those two holes full of nothing. Up close, he smelled of wool and smoke and something burnt. Hair, maybe.

"Ask the fox," he whispered. "Ask the fox how many. Ask him what he isn't telling you."

And he reached out one small, dusty hand toward Zayn's chest, toward the feather, and where his fingers came near, the light under Zayn's skin began to go grey. Not dim. *Grey*. And the boy's fingers, where the light touched them, grew a little more solid. A little more real. As if he were drinking.

"There," said the boy, with his eyes closed. "Oh, there. I remember warm."

---

Zayn woke with a shout.

He was sitting bolt upright on the stony ground with the grey cloth tangled round his legs, and his hands were glowing. Not gold. Grey. A dim, cold, sickly light, like the light on that endless plain. It flickered between his fingers and then went out.

The Embers were fading. Dawn was close.

And there, in the dust of the stony ground beside where his head had lain, were marks. Small, careful marks, like hoofprints. Sheep's hoofprints, in a ring all round him, as if something had stood over him in the night and grazed.

I was standing over him with every hair on my body on end. I'd already looked at the prints. I'd already decided not to look at them again.

"What did you see?" I said.

He told me. I could see that he didn't want to, but he couldn't help it. The grey plain. The sheep chewing words. The boy with eyes like thumbprints. *No one remembers the bearers.* *Ask the fox how many.*

I listened to all of it without a word.

When he'd finished, I was quiet for a moment. Then I said:

"Don't listen to him."

My voice came out strange. Low and rough, as if something had got caught in my throat and was still alive in there.

"Kiri—"

"Don't listen to him," I said again. "Whatever he says to you. However kind he sounds. However sad. *Especially* when he sounds kind. Don't you *dare* listen to him." The fur along my spine stood straight up. My silver eye burned like a coal. "I knew him. He's a liar."

My tail blazed. Brighter than he'd ever seen it, brighter than the lamp in Hesset's tent, so bright it threw my shadow out long and black across the stones and over the ring of little hoofprints.

Zayn stared at it. Then at me.

"You *knew* him?" he said. "Who is he? He said he didn't remember his own name. He said to ask you *how many*. How many what?"

"That's all," I said.

"But—"

"That's *all*."

I turned my back on him and walked away across the stony plain, up onto a tall flat rock at the edge of the canyon, and I sat there with my tail wrapped round my feet.

He didn't follow. He'd learned that much about me, too.

---

I sat on that rock until the Fire came up.

I looked at the sky the whole time. The last few Embers were going out one by one, like eyes closing. I don't know whether the Weaver lives up there. It's where I look when I need to be angry at something that never answers.

*You let him in,* I thought at it. *You let him walk right into the boy's sleep. Nine days out of Harrow's End, and you've already let him in. Did you open the door yourself? Do you two still talk?*

The sky said nothing. It never does. That's how you know it's listening.

I'll tell you what I didn't tell Zayn, since you're listening too.

His name was Malek.

He was a shepherd. A small, poor, ordinary shepherd boy, a long time ago, before the City of Glass was glass. He had a crooked front tooth and a patched brown robe that had been his father's, and he was frightened of the dark, and he talked to his sheep because nobody else would listen.

Nobody except me.

He was the first. The very first child who ever carried the Light. And I was his friend. His only friend. I loved him the way you love the first of anything, before you've learned what loving things is going to cost you.

He called me a liar once, too. At the end. Just before. He stood on a hill with the light pouring out of his chest and his eyes still brown and still his, and he said, *You knew. You knew and you walked me here anyway.*

He wasn't wrong. That's the part the valley found.

I've told you the valley says what you're afraid of. On that ledge, when it whispered *again* in my own voice, it had found my fear and said it perfectly.

That I would walk beside one more child to that dune. That I would watch it happen again. And again. That I'd be the one who brought them, every time, the patient fox at their heel, like a dog bringing the lamb.

Behind me, I heard Zayn get up. I heard him come and stand at the foot of my rock, quietly, not saying anything. I noticed he was careful not to step in the hoofprints.

After a while I climbed down.

"North," I said. My voice was almost normal. "There's a caravan road out past those thorn trees, unless the dunes have eaten it. You need water, and food, and to be somewhere with other people for a while, so you'll stop having conversations with things in your head."

He looked at me for a long moment.

"Kiri," he said. "Was he really a liar?"

"Yes," I said.

My tail glowed. Every liar I've ever known has told the truth sometimes. That's how they get you to keep listening.

He nodded slowly, and we walked out across the stony plain toward the thorn trees, with the Fire rising red on our right and the Valley of Echoes silent at our backs.

It was silent. I listened very carefully. But I could have sworn, just as we passed out of earshot, that somewhere deep in the red stone, in a child's voice, sweet and patient and in no hurry at all, something was calling his name.

*Zayn. Zayn. Come back, Zayn. We'll wait.*

---

*End of Chapter Six*


---

# Chapter Seven
## The Caravan and the Beast

> *When the sky goes dark at noon, pray to the Fire or run. If you hear your mother calling from inside the dark, do not turn around.*
> — a caravan saying, older than any caravan still on the road

---

You smell a caravan long before you see it.

Camel, mostly. Then dung smoke, and spice, and sixty people who haven't washed in a month because water is money. It's the smell of the living. After a valley full of ghosts, it smelled like hope.

To Zayn, it smelled like a city. He'd never seen one.

We found the road in the middle of the morning, a line of hard-packed ground marked with stone cairns, running north and west between the dunes. And on it, crawling along like a great brown caterpillar, was the caravan of Master Dagan.

Forty camels, roped nose to tail, groaning under bales and water jars. A dozen wagons on wide wooden wheels. Six sand lizards scaled the colour of dust, ranging along the flanks like dogs around a flock. Hooded falcons on perches above the lead wagon.

Zayn stood on the ridge above the road and watched it pass with his mouth slightly open.

"Close your mouth," I said. "You'll swallow a fly. Or a camel."

---

Master Dagan was a broad, bald, sunburnt man with a beard dyed orange with henna and a voice that could have cracked rock. He looked Zayn up and down the way he'd look at a camel he suspected of being lame.

"Name?"

Zayn hesitated. Then he said the only true thing he could. "In my village they called me Sandborn."

"That's not a name, that's a weather report." Dagan grunted. "Doesn't matter. One of my boys ran off at the last well and I'm short a pair of hands. You can load, you can dig, you can shovel dung?"

"Yes."

"You'll work for water and food and a place by the fire. No tokens. You don't like it, the desert's that way." He jerked a thumb at the horizon, then squinted down at me. "What's that?"

"A fox," said Zayn.

"It's *my* fox," I said, before I could stop myself. "And it has a name, thank you."

There was a long pause.

"Huh," said Master Dagan at last. "Talking fox. Saw a two-headed goat in the salt towns once." He turned away, already bellowing at someone about a loose rope. "Keep it out of the food stores. And *circle the wagons* at dusk, the lot of you, or so help me I'll feed you to it myself."

*Circle the wagons.* He said it at every halt, like a prayer. You'll see why I remember.

---

Zayn worked.

He was good at it. He loaded bales and dug out wheels and shovelled dung, and didn't complain. He kept his scarf wound high around his throat and his shirt tied close across his chest, so nobody would see the faint glow of the feather mark after dark.

He was happy. I don't think he knew it. But I knew what his face looked like when nobody was throwing anything at it, and I tried very hard to memorise it, the way you'd press a flower in a book you knew was going to burn.

On the second night, a boy came and sat beside him at the fire.

His name was Pell. He was the youngest lizard rider in the caravan, sixteen or so, with a gap in his front teeth. His lizard was a big female called Dustmother, and he talked about her the way some people talk about their grandmothers.

He'd brought dates. Plain ones, not honeyed, but he held out the cloth without being asked, and after a moment Zayn took one.

"You're from the edge villages," Pell said. "I can tell. You walk like the sand's going to bite you."

"It does, sometimes."

Pell laughed. It was an easy laugh, loud and unworried.

They talked late. Or Pell talked, and Zayn listened, mostly to the rumours along the road. There was a lot of grey out east this season, Pell said. Whole villages gone quiet. A well-keeper's family found sitting round a cold hearth with their bowls in their laps, smiling at nothing, saying *pass the salt, pass the salt, pass the salt*. And up in Veimore, the great red city, the old king had thrown his own eldest son out into the desert for dark magic. Hiding a Hollow-touched boy in the palace, people said, and trying to cure him.

"Cure him!" Pell shook his head. "You can't cure the grey. Everybody knows that. You give them to the dunes, quick, before they start smiling at you."

Zayn said nothing. His hand had gone to his chest.

I lay by the fire with my eyes half closed and said nothing either. My tail stayed very still.

---

On the third day, at noon, the falcons wouldn't fly.

Dagan's falconer threw the lead bird into the air, and it went up three wingbeats and came straight back to its perch and sat there with its feathers flattened, shivering, its hooded head turned east. He tried another. The same.

The camels knelt, one by one, without being told, and lowered their long necks and laid their heads flat on the sand and closed their eyes, the way camels do for the butcher.

"Kiri," Zayn said quietly.

"I know," I said.

The wind had stopped.

Out here the wind never stops completely. Now there was nothing. The air hung heavy and hot and dead. The Fire was high and white above us, and the sky was clear blue in every direction.

Except to the east.

To the east, low on the horizon, there was a line of black.

At first it looked like a sandstorm, and the caravan men relaxed a little. Dagan was already bellowing: circle the wagons.

But it wasn't moving like a storm.

Storms run *with* the wind. This was coming against it, out of the dead air, rising higher as it came, and it wasn't brown or yellow like sand. It was black, like the inside of a closed eyelid. And it was silent. The silence came ahead of it like a smell.

And it was getting dark.

Noon, the Fire at its height, and the light was going out of the world. The colours went first: the red of the wagons, the orange of Dagan's beard. Everything went the colour of old bone. Then the shadows went. As if something enormous had put its mouth to the sky and was drinking the light out of it like milk from a bowl.

"When the sky goes dark at noon," said Pell beside us, very softly. All the laughter had gone out of him. "Pray to the Fire or run."

"Run," I said. "Run now. All of you. Leave the wagons, cut the camels loose, *run*—"

Nobody listened. Nobody ever does.

---

It hit us like the end of the world.

The wind came first, a wall of it, so hard it knocked Zayn flat. Sand came screaming sideways.

Then the darkness came down over the caravan like a lid on a pot.

And inside it, something moved.

I've seen most of the creatures of the Hollow. I've seen nothing like the Veilbeast. Nobody who's seen it all at once has kept their name.

It was the storm, and it was something *inside* the storm, and you couldn't tell where one stopped and the other began. Its body was the black wind itself, coiling and folding on itself like a great gut, and in the folds of it were shapes. Limbs, perhaps. The suggestion of limbs, huge and slow and jointed in too many places, feeling across the sand the way a blind man feels for a dropped coin. One passed over us. It was *dense*, the way grief is dense, and where it brushed the top of an overturned wagon the wood went grey and soft and sagged like wet bread.

High up there were lights in the dark. Red ones. Dozens of them, scattered across the black, blinking slowly, not all together. Some were large as a well-mouth. Some were small and set in pairs, at about the height of a man's head, and there were a great many of them.

Eyes. All the eyes of everyone it had ever swallowed, still open in there, still looking out.

And beneath them, in the very heart of the storm, a hole.

Not a mouth. A mouth has edges. This was a great round silent place where even the darkness was darker, where the roaring wind went in and didn't come out. Sand flowed toward it in long hissing streams, like water to a drain. My ears popped. Something warm ran out of my nose. And the hole made a sound, at last, the only sound I ever heard it make.

It *breathed in*.

---

The front of the caravan went first, Dagan's own camel kneeling with its eyes closed. The dark closed over them, and when it lifted there was smooth sand, raked as neat as a temple floor.

Then the camels behind, roped nose to tail. The dark took the end of the rope and pulled, and they went in one after another like beads off a string, not a sound out of any of them.

The people screamed. And then the screams changed.

They didn't stop the way screams stop when someone dies. They *frayed*. A man would be shrieking for his brother, and halfway through the brother's name the scream would forget what it was for. It would trail off into a puzzled little note, then a hum, then nothing.

That's what the Veilbeast does. It doesn't eat you. Eating would be kind. It swallows you, and inside it is the Hollow, all of it at once, like being drowned in a well of everyone's forgetting. Whatever comes out the other side, if anything does, walks on your legs and doesn't know your face.

Zayn was on his feet. I don't know how. His scarf had torn loose, and the feather mark was blazing through his shirt, so bright it lit a circle of sand around him in the dark.

And the Veilbeast's red eyes, high up in the storm, turned toward it.

All of them. At once. The big ones and the small ones in their pairs.

"*Down*," I screamed at him. "Get *down*, it can see you, it can *see* you—"

The light in his hands came grey.

It came grey and thin and terrified, a dead candle in a dead wind. I watched the fear go all the way down into him, like a stone dropped down a well that never hits the bottom.

And then the voices started.

Soft and close, the way the valley had spoken, but these weren't echoes. These were the people it had swallowed, and it was *wearing* them.

"*Circle the wagons*," said Master Dagan's voice, very calmly, from the black. "*Circle the wagons. Circle the wagons. Circle the wagons.*"

"*Where's my*—" said a woman's voice. "*Where's my*— *Where's my*—"

And a child's. A little girl from the water-sellers. I never knew her name. "*Mama, it's dark. Mama, it's dark. Mama, it's dark.*"

Last words. That's all that's left of you, inside it, playing over and over in the black like a bell that won't stop swinging.

Then, through the sand, I saw Pell.

Dustmother was down. She lay on her side kicking, and Pell was pinned beneath her, one leg trapped under her weight. He was screaming. Not Zayn's name. He didn't know Zayn's name. *Sandborn.* It was the only one he had.

And the edge of the dark was coming toward him, slow and deliberate, sliding over the sand like a tide that knows exactly where you've buried your feet.

Zayn ran.

He ran straight into the wind, toward the boy who'd shared dates with him, and somewhere in those few steps the light changed, grey to gold, like a coal catching. He threw himself down over Pell and the struggling lizard with his arms spread wide, and the light came out of him in a dome. A shell of gold, closing over the three of them like cupped hands over a moth.

The darkness came down on top of it.

---

I was outside the light. I want you to know that. I wasn't brave. I'd gone flat in the lee of an overturned wagon, with my silver eye screaming at me to let the other half out and fight, and I stayed small.

I saw a small gold glow in the middle of a world gone black. The Veilbeast settled over it like a hen on an egg. The gold bent and went thin as a soap bubble, and the eyes crowded in close above it, hungry.

What Zayn saw was the underside of it.

He told me, much later, that from inside the dome the Veilbeast wasn't black at all. It was *grey*, close up. Grey and soft and folded, like the inside of a lung. And pressed against the outside of the gold, flattened like faces against a tent wall, were faces. Hundreds. Master Dagan, his orange beard gone the colour of ash. The falconer. The little girl from the water-sellers, her small hands pressed flat on the gold right in front of his face, saying *Mama, it's dark* with no sound, over and over, smiling, and not knowing what a smile was for.

They were trying to get in. Not to hurt him. They were cold, he said. They wanted the warm.

He held the dome. He held it with the gold cracking open across his skin like the glaze on a dropped pot. He could feel each face where it pressed. He said it was like being leaned on by every lonely person who'd ever lived.

And then, slowly, like a tide going out, the darkness lifted.

The black wall of the storm went rolling away west across the dunes, against the wind, its red eyes winking out into the distance one pair at a time. It was still noon. It had all taken less time than it takes to eat a meal.

I got up.

The caravan of Master Dagan was gone.

Sixty people. Forty camels. The road was swept clean. No bodies. No blood. A single wagon wheel on its side, still turning slowly, creaking, *creak, creak, creak*, in the dead air.

Far off across the dunes, small grey figures were walking away, slow and aimless, the way Husks walk. Their arms didn't swing. One of them had a beard dyed orange with henna. As he went, faint on what little wind there was, I heard him calling, cheerful and flat: "*Circle the wagons. Circle the wagons.*" He didn't look back.

In the middle of that great swept emptiness, a small golden glow went out.

---

Zayn was lying in a drift of sand with his arms still spread. The golden cracks were everywhere, even across his eyelids. He was shaking so hard his teeth clattered. There were marks on the sand all round the place where the dome had been. Small hand-shaped hollows, hundreds of them, pressed into the sand in a ring, where they had leaned.

Of Pell, there was only a hand sticking up out of the sand.

The storm had buried him, him and the lizard both. Zayn dug. He had nothing left, and he dug anyway, with his bare cracked hands, until he found a shoulder, and a face, and dragged the boy out by the collar.

Dustmother was dead beneath him. Her open eyes were grey all the way through.

Pell was alive. Mostly.

His leg was broken. And the left side of him, arm and shoulder and face, had gone grey where the edge of the dark had licked him before the gold closed over. His left eye was white as a boiled egg. His mouth smiled on that side only, slack and sweet, and he was mumbling names, half finished, trailing off. "*Dust*— *Dusty*— *Mo*—"

"Don't," I said. "Look at you. You've nothing left. You do this now and it'll bring every Hound for a day's ride. And it'll bring *that* back."

He laid his hands on Pell's face anyway. Of course he did.

---

It came gold this time without being asked. Warmth, flowing out of him and into the boy, and the grey began to lift, peeling back from Pell's cheek the way frost peels from a stone.

He saw Pell's life. A small boy tossing scraps to baby lizards, refusing to cry about his first bite. Then forward: Pell older, grey at the temples, telling children a story by a fire, his hands describing something huge. The children weren't delighted. They were frightened.

And then the other vision.

Longer now. Much longer. It didn't flash. It *took* him.

The full Ash above him, so vast and white it seemed to be falling. The Crown Dune under his bare feet. He knew its name now, though nobody had ever told him. He felt his arms rise. And then the fire started in his chest, and this time it didn't stop at a flash, and this time he *felt* it.

Not light. Fire. Real fire. He felt the skin of his chest tighten and split. He felt the heat go into his ribs, and climb his throat, and fill his mouth so that he couldn't scream, and come out through his eyes. He smelled his own hair burning, and under it, something worse, something like meat.

Below him, someone was shouting a name. Over and over. A man's voice, cracked down the middle.

Then it was gone, and he was on his knees on the caravan road, retching, with his hands still on Pell's face and the smell of burnt hair still real in his nose. It was his own hair. The fringe had crisped where the light came out of his eyes.

---

Pell opened his eyes. Both of them brown.

The grey was gone from his face and arm. His leg was straight. He took a breath, a huge shuddering one, and looked up.

At Zayn, cracked all over with glowing gold, light still leaking from the corners of his eyes, his hair smoking. At the swept, empty desert where an hour before there had been everyone he knew.

I watched him put it together. I watched him do the sum, the way frightened people always do, quick and wrong.

"It looked at you," Pell whispered. "I saw it. All those eyes. It came, and it looked straight at *you*."

Zayn didn't answer.

"It came for you." Pell was scrambling backward through the sand, away from him, his newly healed leg kicking. "Didn't it? You brought it. You're what it was looking for. Everyone's gone, Dagan's gone, they're all *gone*, and you—"

His face twisted.

"*Monster.*"

Zayn flinched. Like a slap.

"I'm not a monster," he said.

My tail glowed. It glowed bright and steady and true. But I watched it closely, all the while Pell was talking, and here's the thing I've never been able to forgive.

When Pell said *it came for you*, my tail didn't dim.

Not even a flicker.

Pell got to his feet, swaying on the leg Zayn had mended. Then he turned and ran, stumbling, out across the empty sand, and he didn't look back. Not once. I hope he's the old man by the fire. I hope he tells it wrong.

Zayn knelt where he'd left him and watched him go.

---

The ground began to move under us.

The sand began to sink and turn like water going down a drain. Where the caravan had been, a hollow was opening, and from the bottom of it, very faintly, came the creak of a wagon wheel, still turning, somewhere underneath.

Far off to the west, at the edge of the sky, the black storm had stopped moving.

Its red eyes had turned back toward us. All of them.

And very faintly, on no wind at all, a little girl's voice: "*Mama, it's dark.*"

"*Up*," I said. "Zayn. Up. *Now*."

He tried. He'd given everything to the dome and the rest to Pell, and there was nothing left. He got halfway up and the sand slid out from under him, pouring now, pulling him toward the centre of the hollow, toward the creaking.

I grabbed his collar in my teeth and pulled. I felt the silver half of me straining, wanting to come out, wanting to *grow*. *Let me,* it whispered. *I could hold the whole desert.* I almost let it.

The sand was at his waist. At his chest.

Then came the hoofbeats.

---

The pale camel came over the edge of the hollow at a dead run. A wrapped hand closed on Zayn's arm. A heave, a grunt, and Zayn came up out of the sand like a cork from a bottle and was thrown across the saddle face down.

"The *fox*," Zayn gasped. "Kiri—"

The rider didn't hesitate. He hooked me up by the scruff with the butt of the long spear and dropped me on top of the boy, and the camel was already turning, already running, out of the hollow and away from the storm.

Behind us, the Veilbeast was coming back.

I saw it over the rider's shoulder, rising black against the sky, faster now, eager. And on the ridge between us and it, a grey robe, flapping. A smooth white mask with no face.

Brother Seven was standing in our path.

He drew one grey blade as the camel thundered toward him and swung it at the boy slung across the saddle. A clean, fast, perfect stroke. It went past Zayn's throat a finger's width away and cut nothing but air.

Brother Seven doesn't miss by accident. He stood on the ridge and watched us go, the blade hanging at his side, and the storm came down over the place where he stood and he didn't move. He stood with his white mask tipped up toward all those eyes, as if they were old friends.

Just before the dark took him, I heard it, very faint, under the wind. Four notes, low and slow, climbing.

Then I lost sight of him in the sand.

---

The rider didn't stop until the Fire was going down.

By then the storm was a smudge on the horizon behind us. The rider slid Zayn off onto firm ground and set me down beside him. He crouched over the boy without touching him and looked at the golden cracks on the boy's face, and the singed fringe, for a long, long time. His wrapped hands were shaking. I filed that away with all the other things I wasn't saying.

Then he stood and pointed with the long spear. North and west.

"Wait," Zayn croaked. "Who are you? Why do you keep—"

The rider mounted, and in a few moments was gone into the dusk the way he'd gone the first time, without a word.

"That's twice," Zayn whispered.

"Yes," I said.

"Kiri, who *is* he?"

I didn't answer that. You know by now that I didn't.

---

We walked the way the spear had pointed. We had nothing else.

Almost nothing. Where the rider had crouched, he'd left a water skin, half full, without a word, the way Auntie Sabba used to leave her bundles.

Zayn made it last. By the next afternoon the skin hung flat against his hip. The golden lines on his skin faded slowly to a dull shimmer. Near dusk he ate one of Auntie Sabba's squashed honey dates. Just one. He held it in his mouth a long time, like a child holding a hand.

He didn't say anything about Pell, or my tail when Pell said *it came for you*. He'd seen it. I know he had.

Once he stopped and sniffed the back of his own wrist. He could still smell burnt hair on himself. I could too. I'm not sure he ever knew, after that, which fires were real.

The Ash came up late that night. It was thin, thinner than I'd seen it since we left Harrow's End, a pale scratch of white low over the dunes. It hardly gave any light at all.

And somewhere ahead of us, so faint only a fox could feel it, something was humming.

I felt it first in my teeth. Then in the pads of my feet. A deep, low, steady note rising out of the dark to the north and west, exactly where the spear had pointed, like a mother humming a child to sleep. Or a well, waiting.

I knew that sound, and what it did to people who drank from it. And I knew the rider had not pointed us at it by accident.

I didn't tell Zayn. He was walking with his head down and his hand pressed to the feather mark, and he hadn't heard it yet. In a little while he would.

After eleven bearers, you'd think I'd know what waits beyond every dune. I don't. That night I didn't know the desert had saved one more thing for him by the singing water. Not a monster, or a vision, or a stranger in grey.

Something worse, in the end. Something he could love.

If I had known, I'd have made him walk faster. Or slower. I've never decided which.

---

*End of Chapter Seven*


---

# Chapter Eight
## The Singing Wells

> *Drink from the wells, and the past will drink from you. It is always thirstier than you are.*
> — a saying of the water-finders, older than any town that uses it

---

The Ash was thin the night we found the Singing Wells.

Thin as the smile of a trader who has just cheated you and would like you to come back tomorrow. It hung low over the western dunes, a pale scratch on the dark, and gave almost no light at all.

That suited Zayn. He'd had enough of being seen.

He had barely spoken since the beast came down out of the black sky and the caravan was simply *gone*. Since he'd dug one boy out of the sand and poured the Light into him, and the boy had opened his eyes and said the word.

The cracks were still fading on his forearms, thin lines of dull honey under the skin. Every so often he'd lift his wrist to his nose and sniff it, and I'd pretend not to see.

"You can talk to me, you know," I said. "I'm told I'm an excellent listener."

"Who told you that?"

"No one. Everyone I've listened to is dead. I was hoping you'd be the first to say it out loud."

The corner of his mouth moved. It wasn't a smile, but I've worked with less. I've worked with skulls.

His water skin had been empty since the afternoon before. Once, in the dark, I heard him say very softly, "*Mama, it's dark*," and then stop, appalled, as if someone else had used his mouth.

Then he stopped walking.

"Do you hear that?" he said.

I did. I'd been hearing it for some time and hoping he wouldn't.

It was a hum. Low and steady and very large, as if the whole desert were humming to itself with its mouth closed. You could feel it come up through your feet and into the bones of your skull, until it was hard to tell whether the hum was outside you or whether you'd always been humming and had only just noticed.

"Water," Zayn whispered.

"Unfortunately," I said.

---

You'll want to know why a fox would be unhappy about water in the middle of the Destiny Desert.

The Singing Wells are older than Veimore. Seven stone well-mouths sit in a ring among date palms, and the water in them hums. The water-finders say it's the ghosts of everyone who ever drowned in them, still singing on the way down.

What I know is this. The water is sweet and cold, and it remembers. Drink it, and it will hand you back something you had forgotten so completely you didn't know there was a hole where it used to be.

People have never had a hole filled in with something they'd buried there on purpose. I've seen a man drink from the third well and remember, all in one swallow, what he'd done to his brother when they were boys. He walked into the dunes that night and didn't take any water with him. The wells don't give you your past. They give your past *you*.

"Zayn," I said. "Before you go running in there with your tongue out—"

But he'd stopped again, and this time it wasn't the hum.

From somewhere ahead, beyond the dark shapes of the palms, came a snarl like tearing cloth. Then a clang of steel. Then a sound I had not expected to hear anywhere within three days' walk of the Hollow.

Someone was laughing.

---

We came over the last low dune and looked down into the grove.

The seven well-mouths glowed faintly between the palms, silver light rising off the water like breath. And in the middle of all that quiet, beautiful wrongness, a young man was fighting five Hollow Hounds by himself.

I've described the Husks to you. Let me tell you about the Hounds.

They are wolves, or the memory of wolves, made of shadow the way smoke is made of fire: solid in the middle and fraying at the edges, and you feel the cold of them before you see them. Their eyes are embers. The angry red coals you'd find under the ash of a house where somebody didn't get out. They make one sound only, that low, wet snarl. And when they bite you, they don't take flesh. They take whatever you were thinking about at the time.

They hunt the Light. That's what they're for.

The young man didn't seem to care.

He was tall, a few years older than Zayn, with dark hair tied back and a jaw that had been badly cut once and healed into a pale seam. But he held his sword the way I've only ever seen kings' guards hold them, high and easy, the point never wandering. Or the half of the point he had left. The sword was broken. Snapped a hand's width and a little more above the guard, so that what remained was barely longer than a long knife, with a jagged end like a broken tooth.

He moved like water poured from a jug.

"No, not you," he was saying, sidestepping a lunge. "You, the ugly one. Yes. You know who you are."

He spun. The broken blade went through the nearest Hound's throat. It didn't bleed. It burst, the way a dropped sack of flour bursts, into a cloud of fine black ash that drifted down onto the sand. Inside the cloud, for half a breath, I heard a small surprised voice. Somebody's last thought, let go.

"Four," said the young man cheerfully. "Who's next? Don't all volunteer at once."

---

Zayn had dropped flat on his belly behind the crest of the dune. His hand was on his knife.

"Not our business," I said.

"He's alone."

"He seems to be enjoying it."

That was true. My tail glowed with it. But the young man was also bleeding from a long scratch on his left shoulder and one of the four remaining Hounds had begun, very quietly, to circle behind him while the others kept him busy.

"They're not here for him, you know," I said. I said it before I could stop myself. "Hounds hunt the Light. They were waiting here for you. He just got in the way."

Zayn turned and looked at me.

I wish I hadn't said it. But it was true, and my tail was glowing so bright it lit the sand between us. Everyone who stands near him gets in the way, sooner or later.

He got up.

---

By then, Zayn didn't trust the Light. Worse, he'd started to be afraid of it.

Every time it saved someone, the people it saved had looked at him afterwards with the same face, and that face had a word in it. And every time, it had shown him the dune, and the dune was getting closer, and now it came with the smell.

So when he ran down that slope toward the Hounds, the first thing he felt wasn't courage. It was a cold, sick little voice in his head, a voice with a shepherd's patience in it, saying: *He'll see. He'll see what you are, and then he'll look at you like they all do. And then he'll be gone too. Everyone near you goes.*

The light came, and it came grey.

It flickered out of his fists like dishwater, and the Hound circling behind the young man stopped. It turned its ember eyes toward Zayn. It lowered its head and grinned, the way only shadows can, a grin that went too far back along the jaw.

Fear does that. I've told you. It turns the Light into something the dark can chew.

The Hound sprang. Zayn threw himself sideways and felt its teeth snap shut beside his ear, and for a moment, just a moment, he couldn't remember where he was. The palms, the wells, the reason he'd come down the dune: gone, bitten clean out, a gap like a pulled tooth. Then it came back, in a rush. Most of it. I've never been sure it was all of it.

Over the Hound's smoking back he saw the young man stagger as a second Hound caught his wounded shoulder and dragged him half off his feet.

The young man didn't cry out. He set his teeth and swung the broken sword one-handed. He kept standing up.

And Zayn thought, with no words at all, *Not him too.*

The light went gold.

---

It came up out of his chest warm and steady, like the Fire climbing over a dune, and it ran down his arm and into the rusted blade of his scavenged knife until the metal shone like honey.

The Hound in front of him flinched.

Zayn lunged, and for the first time in his life his knife went *into* a creature of the Hollow instead of sliding off. The Hound burst into ash. Zayn slammed his shoulder into the Hound that had the young man's arm, and the light went through it like lamp-oil going up.

Then there was a back against his back.

A warm, solid weight, someone else's shoulder blades pressed against his own. He felt the other boy's breathing. The last two Hounds paced in a slow circle, snarling their tearing-cloth snarl, and the young man shifted his feet so the two of them could turn together, like the hands of a water clock.

The young man glanced back over his shoulder. He looked at Zayn's face, and Zayn's arms, and the gold pouring out of Zayn's skin and lighting up the palms, the wells and the falling ash.

"You glow," he said.

A pause, while a Hound feinted and drew back.

"That's weird."

"I know," said Zayn.

"I didn't say it was *bad*." The young man turned forward again. "Duck."

Zayn ducked. The broken sword whistled through the air where his head had been and took the leaping Hound clean across the chest. Ash rained down on them, warm, like the ash after a pyre. The last Hound seemed to do some quick arithmetic, and turned to run.

Zayn's light reached it first.

---

The grove went quiet. Only the wells hummed, patient, waiting for someone to get thirsty.

The gold drained back into Zayn's chest and left him swaying, with new cracks running up both forearms, brighter than the old ones. And then it came: the thing that always came. It didn't wait any more for him to close his eyes.

The full Ash, white and enormous.

The Crown Dune, so high its top was lost in the sky.

Heat. Not light: heat, real heat, climbing up through the soles of his feet as if the sand itself had become a cooking stone. His own arms, opening, and the skin of his palms blistering as he watched. The smell again.

And something new. A sound, somewhere below him on the dune. Someone shouting. Someone shouting a *name*, over and over, in a voice cracked down the middle like a dropped bowl, and he couldn't hear what the name was. He leaned toward it with his hair already burning, and couldn't hear it.

"Hey. *Hey.*"

A hand on his shoulder. Zayn blinked, and the desert came back: palms, wells, Embers, the thin scratch of Ash in the west. He was sitting in the sand. He didn't remember sitting down. His palms were stinging. There was nothing on them. The young man was crouched in front of him, his broken sword across his knees, watching him with frank, dark-eyed interest.

"You went somewhere," the young man said. "Your eyes did a thing. And you were saying *it's hot, it's hot*. It's freezing."

Zayn looked up at the sky, at the Ash. It was thin. It was so very thin. He let out a breath he hadn't known he was holding.

I didn't tell him how fast an Ash fills up. It seemed unkind. Kindness and cowardice wear the same robe in the dark.

"Does it hurt?" the young man asked.

Zayn stared at him.

He nodded at the golden cracks on Zayn's arms. As if it were a perfectly normal question to ask someone who'd just turned into a lamp in front of you.

In fifteen years of being Zayn, no one had ever asked him that.

"Yes," said Zayn.

My tail glowed. The young man glanced at it, then back at Zayn, and nodded slowly.

"Thought so," he said. "Your face looked like mine does when I've been punched." He stood and held out a hand, and when Zayn took it, he pulled him to his feet in one easy motion. "Right. Excuse me a moment. I need to collect my wages."

---

He took a clay jar from a pack by the nearest palm, knelt in the sand, and began to scoop up Hound-ash with a flat wooden spoon.

Zayn watched him for a long time.

"What," he said finally, "are you doing?"

"Making a living." The young man didn't look up. "Glassblowers in the free towns pay a water token a pinch for this. Makes black glass that won't break. They say if you hold a cup of it to your ear you can hear the Hound still growling in there." He tapped the jar. "A good Hound's worth a week of water. You just doubled my income, glowing stranger. I'd thank you, but I'm going to have to give you half, and I'm still sad about it."

"You can keep it."

"Absolutely not. I have principles." He considered. "One. It's that one."

He sealed the jar and sat back on his heels. In the silver light from the wells, I saw the ring on his right hand for the first time. Too heavy and too gold for a young man who scraped up monster-ash for a living. It was carved with a crest I'd seen before, on banners and on great red city gates, hung with lanterns.

I kept that to myself. I keep a lot of things to myself. You may have noticed.

"Soren," said the young man, and pointed at his own chest.

He said it out loud. Right into the open night, with the wind moving in the palm fronds over his head.

Zayn flinched as if he'd dropped a lamp. He looked round, wildly, at the dark between the palms, as if something would come out of it. "You shouldn't— The wind— It *learns* them. There's a valley, it—"

"The wind and I have an understanding," said Soren. "It's never done me a single favour, and I've never asked it for one." He raised his eyebrows. "You've got one too, I assume. A name. Or do I keep calling you glowing stranger?"

Zayn's hand went to his chest. He looked at the wind moving in the palms for a long time. I knew what he was hearing. Red stone, and a child's voice, tasting him. *Zayn. Zayn. We'll wait.*

Then he leaned in, close to Soren's ear, and said it so quietly I only just heard it. "Zayn."

"Zayn," Soren repeated, at a perfectly normal volume. "Good. Easy to shout when you're about to be eaten." He looked down. "And who's this?"

That was me. I'd trotted down from the dune and was sitting by the nearest well, washing ash off my front paw with considerable dignity.

"Kiri," I said. "Yes, I talk. No, it isn't a trick. Yes, I've heard every joke. No, I'm not possessed. Not entirely."

Soren looked at me, then at Zayn.

"Your fox talks," he said to Zayn.

"He's not my fox," said Zayn.

"I'm not his fox," I said, at exactly the same time.

Soren took this in. Then he shrugged. "Fine," he said. "I've had a strange year."

He didn't scream. He just went back to tying up his shoulder with a strip of cloth, one end held in his teeth.

I decided I liked him. I didn't say so. I have standards. Also, I know what happens to the people I like. It was already too late for him. It usually is.

---
