Zayn's knees chose that moment to remember he hadn't had water in a day and a half. He sat down rather suddenly beside the nearest well.

The water inside was black and still, and the hum was so loud this close that the stone lip shivered under his hand. For a moment he could have sworn the face looking up at him from the water was smaller. Younger. Not his.

"Careful," said Soren. He'd stopped tying his bandage. "You know what these are?"

"The Singing Wells," said Zayn. "Kiri says they remember."

"Kiri's right." Soren crouched beside him. For the first time his voice wasn't joking. "First drink's the worst." He looked down into the black water. "I drank once. My first night out here. I saw my mother's face. She died when I was three. I didn't know I remembered her at all." He was quiet a moment. "I saw her dying. That's the part they don't tell you. You don't get to choose which bit you get back." Then, lighter: "Now I strain it and boil it. It still hums a bit. It doesn't sing."

Zayn looked at the water.

I knew what he was thinking. A boy found alone in the sand had nothing back there to remember. Whatever the well gave him, it couldn't be worse than the emptiness he already had.

He was wrong about that. Emptiness is the kindest thing the desert gives anyone.

"Zayn," I said.

He looked at me.

I could have stopped him. One word would have done it.

I didn't say it. I've thought about why, many times, and I'll let you decide whether it was kindness.

He cupped his hands, dipped them into the Singing Well, and drank.

---

It was cold enough to ache, and sweeter than any water he'd ever bought. He swallowed, and the humming came inside him, filling his chest and his skull until it wasn't humming anymore.

It was singing.

And the desert went away.

He was small. Smaller than he had ever been, wrapped tight in something soft, warm all the way through for the first time in his life. He couldn't see faces, only the flicker of a fire. And beyond the fire, outside, very far off, a sound he didn't have a word for yet. Many voices, chanting. No words in it. Just breath, rising and falling, rising and falling, coming closer.

But he could feel a hand.

It was a child's hand. Small, a little sticky, very warm, and it had hold of his entire fist. It wasn't letting go. It squeezed, the way you'd squeeze something to make sure it was real. Someone was holding on to him. Someone small had decided he was worth holding on to, and was not going to let go, whatever came through the door.

And above him, somewhere in the warm dark, a man was singing.

His voice was low and rough and a little out of tune. It was a song about fire, and about wings, and about something that falls into the ashes and rises up out of them again. The words slid away. But the tune went into him and stayed, the way a fish-hook stays.

The chanting outside got louder. The man's voice shook, and kept singing.

The small hand squeezed harder. And then—

Then it was gone. Not let go. *Pulled*. Torn out of his fist, the fingers dragging across his palm, and there was a scream, a child's scream, high and furious, getting farther away. And the singing stopped.

He knew that tune.

He'd heard it hummed, out in the dunes, behind a smooth white mask, by a man with twin grey blades who had cornered him in the dark and then, for no reason at all, let him go.

---

Zayn came up out of the water gasping.

He was on his knees by the well, his face wet, and not all of it was well-water. His right fist was clenched so hard his nails had cut half-moons in his palm. He couldn't open it. He sat there trying, and it wouldn't. As if it were still holding on to something that had been taken out of it fifteen years ago.

Soren didn't say anything.

That was the second thing he did that night that no one had ever done for Zayn. He didn't ask questions. He sat down in the sand beside him, close enough that their shoulders nearly touched, and looked out at the palms, and waited. After a while, without a word, he reached over and began gently prying Zayn's fingers open, one at a time, the way you'd help an old man with a stiff hand.

When the last finger came loose, Soren said, "Good one or bad one?"

Zayn wiped his face with the heel of his hand.

"I don't know," he said.

My tail glowed. It was the truest thing he could have said.

Later, when Soren had built a fire between two of the wells, Zayn came and sat by me. He didn't look at me. He kept his eyes on the flames.

"Kiri," he said softly. "That song. Do you know it?"

The hum of the wells filled up the silence.

"Lots of people sing about fire," I said at last. "It's the desert. There isn't much else to sing about."

My tail glowed, because every word of that was true.

That's the trouble with the truth. You can hide a whole child in the spaces between it, and no tail in the world will ever give you away.

---

Soren shared his flatbread, hard as roof tiles, and the boiled, strained water from his tin pot. It only hummed a little. Zayn drank it with his eyes shut, braced, and nothing came.

"You can stay, if you like," Soren said, poking the fire with the end of his broken sword. He said it lightly. It mattered a great deal. "Fire's big enough for two." He glanced at me. "Three."

"I eat scorpions," I said. "I won't be touching your bread."

"Then you're cheap company. Even better."

Zayn lay down on the far side of the fire with his patched cloak pulled over him. And I watched him do something I had never once seen him do in fifteen years. He fell asleep with his knife lying in the sand beside him, instead of in his hand. His right hand was curled loosely on the sand instead, palm up, fingers half closed, as if waiting for someone small to take it.

Soren sat up a while longer, turning the gold ring and looking north. Then he lay down too.

I stayed awake. Someone has to.

The wells hummed. Around midnight, the hum changed key, the way it does sometimes, and for an hour it sounded almost exactly like breathing. I sat with my back to the fire and watched the black water and did not let either of the boys go near it.

The Embers turned. And low over the western dunes, the thin Ash hung like a white thread, like the first stitch in a shroud that would take exactly one turn of the sky to finish sewing.

I didn't count the nights that first night. I should have. If I'd started from the beginning, I'd have known exactly how many they had, and I could have told you every one.

I started the next night instead.

I've never been able to stop. Some nights I still count them, and every time, there's one less than I remember.

---

*End of Chapter Eight*


---

# PART THREE: ONE TURN OF THE ASH


---

# Chapter Nine
## A Prince with a Broken Sword

> *A broken blade still cuts, if the hand is true. So does a broken promise.*
> — words cut above the door of an old swordsmith's forge, the rest of the line chiselled away

---

Night one. I counted it at dawn, which is cheating, but I'm the one telling this story and I'll count how I like. You'll find that the one telling the story always does.

Zayn woke to the sound of someone fighting nobody.

He came up out of his cloak with his knife already in his hand, the way he'd woken every morning of his life, and found Soren in the open sand between the wells, moving through the grey light before the Fire. His broken sword flashed and cut the air in long, looping patterns, as if the whole thing were a dance he'd been taught before he could walk.

Which, as it turned out, it was.

Zayn sat and watched him for a long time. He'd learned to fight from bullies and falling ceilings. He'd never seen fighting that was *beautiful*. He hadn't known it could be.

"You're staring," Soren said, without stopping.

"Sorry."

"Don't be. I'm magnificent." He finished the pattern with the broken point an inch from a palm trunk, held it there a moment, then lowered it. "Taught by the finest swordmaster in the city, and then by every guard who wanted to knock a prince on his backside and call it a lesson. I've never lost a fight."

From somewhere inside Zayn's pack, muffled by the cloth around it, came a small, clear *ting*. He'd kept the bell wrapped up since the caravan, where nobody could see it glint and ask.

Soren stopped. He looked at the pack. He looked at Zayn.

"It's a truth bell," said Zayn. "It rings when someone lies to me."

I glanced down at my tail. It had gone as dim as an old wick.

"Ah," said Soren. He sheathed his sword with great dignity. "Twice. I've lost twice. Once to a palace guard when I was nine, and once to a camel."

No bell. My tail glowed. Zayn's mouth twitched at one corner.

"A *camel*?"

"It was a very large camel, and I don't want to talk about it."

---

Something was wrong with that sword, and Zayn noticed it while Soren was cleaning sand out of the guard.

The blade was snapped, of course. But the hilt was strange too. It was wrapped in old dark leather, and the pommel at the end was a round cap of bronze that didn't quite match the rest, as though it had been made for a different sword. Or a different hand.

"Why don't you fix it?" he asked.

Soren didn't look up. "Some things you don't fix."

My tail stayed bright. It was true; it just wasn't an answer.

"The old smiths of my city made their hilts hollow," Soren said after a moment, as if he'd decided to give Zayn a different answer instead. He twisted the bronze pommel, and it turned and came off in his hand. Inside the grip was a narrow, empty space about the length of a finger. "You're supposed to keep a prayer in here, written on a scrap of paper. So the Weaver rides with you into battle. In Veimore we bury everything with everybody. We're very thorough."

"What's in yours?"

Soren tipped the hilt toward him. Empty. Dark in there, like a little well.

"I was never much good at praying," he said, and screwed the pommel back on.

Zayn nodded slowly. "Me neither."

It was a small thing. Two boys admitting a small thing to each other beside a well. But I've learned to watch small things. They're usually where the big ones are hiding.

---

He told his story that night, by the fire, with the wells humming around them like a crowd that had come to listen.

Zayn didn't ask. That's important. Asking meant they'd ask him back. But somewhere in the long hot day, something had loosened between them. When the Embers came out, Soren just started talking.

"I'm from Veimore," he said.

Everyone in the desert knows that name. Mothers in Harrow's End used it to frighten children: *Behave, or the red city will come for your name.*

"The City of the Dead and Darkness," Soren said, and smiled crookedly at the fire. "That's what the free towns call it. The walls are blood red, and very high. There are lanterns on every wall, thousands of them, burning day and night, and they must never all go out. There are men whose whole lives are just walking the walls with oil jars. You can always tell a lamp-man by his eyes." He poked the fire. "And under the city there are catacombs, deeper than wells. On quiet nights, if you lie with your ear on the palace floor, you can hear them. Not voices. Just a sort of knocking. Very slow. The priests say it's the stone settling."

"Is it?"

"I was a prince. Nobody told me the truth about anything." He shrugged. "And on the walls of every street, someone's painted the names of the dead. Back to the beginning. The painters go round touching them up every year, because the names fade." He paused. "They fade faster than they used to. That's what the old painters say. Some of them fade in a single night. You go to bed and your grandfather's on the wall, and you wake up and there's just a sort of pale patch where he was. Nobody knows why."

"It sounds…" Zayn searched for a word.

"Grim. Yes. It's lovely, though, in its own way. A city that refuses to forget anybody." He was quiet. "Mostly."

He turned the heavy gold ring on his finger.

"My father is King Absalon," he said. "I'm his eldest son. Or I was. It's complicated."

My tail glowed like a lamp. Zayn looked at it, and then at the young man who'd spent last night scraping Hound-ash into a jar with a wooden spoon, and his mouth fell slightly open.

"You're a *prince*."

"Was. Probably still am, technically. Nobody's quite sure. I think it depends who you ask, and whether they're holding a knife at the time." Soren shrugged. "Close your mouth. You'll get sand in it."

---

There was a boy called Nim.

He worked in the palace stables, Soren said, looking after the riding lizards and the king's white camels. He was maybe twelve, with ears like jug handles and a laugh like a donkey with hiccups, and he knew the name of every animal in the stables and told them good morning each one, every day.

"I used to hide out there," Soren said. "When lessons got too much. When my father got too much. Nim never told anyone. He'd just hand me a brush and point at a lizard."

One night Nim was sent outside the walls on an errand, past the last lantern. Nobody would ever say who sent him. He came back grey.

"Not all the way," Soren said. "Not a Husk. But half of him was gone. He didn't know the lizards' names any more. He'd just stand there, turning his hands over as if they belonged to someone else." He was staring into the fire now, and his voice had gone flat and careful, like someone walking on a roof that might give. "And he'd smile, sometimes. That was the worst. Not his smile. A different one. Like someone had shown him what a smile was once and he'd copied it wrong. And he'd say *good morning*. Just that. *Good morning, good morning*, at midnight, to the wall."

Zayn's hand had gone to his chest. Behind my eyes, I heard a man with an orange beard saying *circle the wagons* across an empty desert.

"The law says the emptied are to be taken outside the walls and given to the dunes. Before they spread it. So I hid him."

"Where?"

"An old storeroom over the stables. I brought him food. I sat with him every night. I read him the names off the walls, all the old names, thinking maybe if he heard enough names he'd find his own again." Soren swallowed. "Once, after about a month, he almost smiled. His own smile. I swear he did. *Almost*."

The fire cracked. Nobody said anything.

"The Vizier found out," Soren said. "Orrin. My father's advisor. He's—" He stopped. "He's very soft-spoken. He's never once raised his voice in my hearing. He has grey eyes, pale grey, like someone left them out in the Fire too long. He sees everything. He just doesn't seem to *keep* any of it." He picked at the bandage on his shoulder. "And he stood in front of my father's throne and said, very gently, that the crown prince of Veimore had been hiding a creature of the Hollow inside the palace walls. Practising dark magic on it. *Treason*, he called it. He said it so kindly, Zayn. Like he was sorry he had to."

Grey eyes. Soft voice. I knew a few men with eyes like that. None of them had ever been sorry for anything.

"Was it true?" Zayn asked.

"I was hiding him," said Soren. "The rest was a lie."

My tail glowed for both halves of that.

"They sent guards that night. Six of them. I stood in the storeroom door." He touched the hilt of the broken sword lying beside him. "One of them had an iron shield, and I hit it wrong. I was trying to get past it to the one who'd grabbed Nim's arm. The blade went in half. I kept fighting with what was left." He let out a breath that was almost a laugh. "I'm very good with what's left. I've had practice."

"And Nim?"

The wells hummed.

"Orrin had him taken away," Soren said. "That's all anyone would tell me. *Taken away.* Nobody knows where. Nobody even remembers him properly. That's the thing that frightens me, Zayn. I go back over it, and I ask the stable hands, and they say *what boy?* Like he was never there." He looked down at his hands. "I've been looking for most of a year. Sometimes I wake up and I can't remember his face either. Just the ears. And the *good morning*."

"And your father—"

"Wouldn't look at me." Soren's voice didn't change. That was the worst part. "Orrin did all the talking. My father just sat there with his hand on his face. And then I was outside the gates with a broken sword and a bag of flatbread, and that was that."

He picked up a stick and drew in the sand with it. A small figure. A stick-boy.

"My brother Idan ran after me," he said. "He's ten. He got all the way to the outer gate in his bare feet, crying so hard he couldn't talk. He just grabbed my robe and wouldn't let go." His mouth twisted. "They had to carry him back. Orrin carried him. Very gently. Idan stopped crying the moment Orrin picked him up. Just stopped, all at once, like a lamp going out." He rubbed the stick-boy away with his palm. "I can still hear him. I can still hear him *stop*."

Zayn looked at the smeared place in the sand for a long time.

Then, very quietly, because a story was owed for a story and Zayn always paid what he owed, he told Soren about Harrow's End.

He told him about the stones. The scar under his shoulder blade. Bram, and the lane full of Husks, and the light, and Bram's little brother, and the faces afterwards. The valley. The caravan, and the faces on the gold. He told him about the word. Every time somebody had used it.

When he finished, Soren didn't say anything for a while.

"They'll call you a monster, Zayn," he said at last. "They called me one too." He looked up from the fire, and the crooked smile came back, a little shaky at the edges. "Let's be monsters together, then."

My tail lit up the whole grove.

And somewhere out past the palms, beyond the light of the fire, I heard something move in the sand. Not a Hound. Something slower. Something that had been listening to two boys say their secrets aloud in the dark, and had liked it.

I went and sat between them and the dark for the rest of the night. Neither of them asked why.

---

Night four. That was the night of the scorpions.

I want to say first that it was not my fault.

I had caught a fine fat one under a flat stone at the edge of the grove, about the length of Soren's hand, and crunched it by the fire with great enthusiasm, because I've found over the centuries that nothing ruins a bearer's appetite faster, and a bearer with no appetite is a bearer who isn't thinking about anything worse. Zayn turned away and made a small sick noise, which was what I'd been going for.

Soren, however, leaned in.

"What does it taste like?"

"Like victory," I said. "And a little like old boots. With a finish of regret."

"Can I try one?"

Zayn turned back round. "*No.*"

"Why not? He eats them every day. He looks fine."

"I am older than your city, and half of me is—" I stopped. "I have a very strong stomach."

Soren had already got up. He came back a short while later with his own scorpion pinned under the flat of his broken sword, looking deeply pleased with himself. He pulled the sting off the way he'd watched me do it. Its legs were still going.

"Soren," said Zayn.

"Don't be a coward, Zayn. Princes eat all sorts of things. I once ate a roasted lizard eye at a feast. It looked back at me the entire time."

He put the scorpion in his mouth, and crunched.

His face did several interesting things in a row. Then he swallowed, nodded slowly, and said, "Huh. Actually, that's not so—"

He was sick for a full day.

---

I've watched a great many people be ill in my time. Plague. The bitter cough off the salt flats that takes whole towns in a season. Soren being sick from a scorpion was not the worst of it. But it was by far the loudest.

He lay in the shade of the palms with his arms flung out like a man dropped from a great height. He turned interesting shades of green. Every so often he rolled over and was sick into the sand. Then he rolled back and announced, to the sky in general, that he was dying.

"Tell Idan," he croaked, "that I died bravely. In battle. Against a beast of terrible size."

"It was the length of your hand," I said.

"Tell him it was *this big*." He tried to spread his arms and gave up halfway. "Zayn. Zayn. Are you there?"

"I'm here."

"I'm leaving you my sword. It's broken. Look after it. Don't let the fox near it. He'll eat it."

When he said that, something cold went down my back, like a door opening in an empty house. The desert has a terrible sense of humour, and it never forgets a line.

And Zayn laughed.

It wasn't a big laugh. It came out of him in a short, surprised burst, like a bird startled out of a bush. Then another, and then he had his hand over his mouth and his shoulders were shaking, and he couldn't stop.

Soren lifted his head off the sand. His face was green and his hair was full of grit.

"Is that—" he said. "Kiri. Is he *laughing*? I'm dying and he's *laughing*."

"It would appear so," I said.

I'll tell you something now, and you can do what you like with it.

I had not heard Zayn laugh in eight years. Not since he was seven, and gave himself a name, and sat on the edge of the well in Harrow's End saying it over and over under his breath until it made him giggle. After that, the village took the laughing out of him the way they took everything else. One stone at a time.

And here was this green, groaning, ridiculous prince, flat on his back in the sand at the Singing Wells, and he had given it back without even trying.

I want you to hold on to that. Because I've told you how this ends, and there aren't many sounds like that one coming.

Zayn offered to help. He held out his hands, with the faint gold already stirring under the skin.

"Absolutely not," Soren moaned. "Every Hound for three days would come running, and I'd have to fight them lying down. And you'd go all strange in the eyes again. I'll be sick the honest way, thank you."

So Zayn sat with him. He brought him water, strained and boiled, and wet a cloth for his forehead. All through the long hot afternoon, every so often, he'd look over at Soren groaning on the sand and start laughing again.

I didn't eat any scorpions in front of either of them for three days after that. I'd like that noted. It was a sacrifice. I know about those.

---

The lessons started on the sixth night.

It was Soren's idea. He'd been watching Zayn try to make sense of some marks scratched into the stone lip of the oldest well, centuries ago, and finally he said, "You can't read, can you?"

Zayn's whole body went stiff. His hand went to his chest.

"No."

He said it to the fire. In Harrow's End hardly anyone could read. Only the rich could, people like Soren. And somewhere under his ribs Zayn had already started to dread the moment Soren would realise how far below him he was, and look at him differently, and go.

But Soren just smoothed a patch of sand flat with his palm and picked up a stick.

"Right," he said. "Come here."

He drew a shape. A sharp line, a slant, another line, like a lightning flash lying on its side.

"That's a *zed*," he said. "It makes a *zzz* sound. Like a fly. Or like the start of you." Beside it he drew three more shapes. "*Ah*. *Yuh*. *Nnn*."

He sat back.

"That's your name," he said.

Zayn stared at it.

Four shapes in the sand, wobbly and crooked, because Soren wrote like a prince who'd always had someone else to write for him. *ZAYN*. The only thing in the world that had ever been his, sitting there outside of him, where anybody could see it. Where anything could.

"Is it—" Zayn's voice cracked. "Is that like saying it? To the wind?"

"The wind can't read," said Soren.

"You'd be surprised," I said.

They both looked at me. I looked at the fire. My tail glowed, steady and calm. I'd seen the dunes write. I'd seen what they wrote back.

Zayn copied the four shapes, slowly, with his tongue between his teeth. Then again. Then again. He was still doing it long after Soren had fallen asleep, by the fire and then by the Embers, until the patch of sand in front of him was covered edge to edge with his own name.

In the morning, the wind had smoothed it all away.

Nearly all.

I found it while the boys were still asleep. At the lip of the oldest well, a long way from the fire, where the stone was soft enough to scratch. *ZAYN*. Scratched deep, over and over, in a ring all the way round the well-mouth, in his crooked new hand. The scratches were fresh. There was blood in some of them, from a split fingernail.

When he woke, I looked at his hands. The nail on his right forefinger was torn to the quick. He looked at it, puzzled, and sucked it, and didn't remember. I watched him not remember. I kicked sand into the letters until they were filled, and told no one, and sat a long time afterwards with my nose pointed at the black water, wondering which of us the well had been talking to in the night.

He wrote it again, that evening, by the fire. Awake.

---

It went both ways. That's what I want you to understand about the two of them. Nobody was rescuing anybody. Every night Soren taught Zayn a few more letters, and every day Zayn taught Soren how not to die.

He showed him how to stretch a cloth over a hole in the sand before dawn so the dew would gather and drip into a cup. Which plants had water in their roots and which had poison. How to smell a sandstorm a day off, like an old coin. He taught him to listen for the change in the sand's hiss that meant the wind was turning, and, more quietly, the other change, the one where the hiss stops altogether, which means something under the sand is listening too.

"And when the dunes move at night," Zayn said, "you don't follow the sand. The sand lies. You follow the Embers. They never move." He pointed up. "That bright one. And those three in a line. Keep them on your left and you're walking east."

Soren looked up at the sky for a long time.

"Nobody ever taught me that," he said. "I had six tutors. Not one of them taught me anything that would keep me alive."

"Nobody ever taught me either," said Zayn. "I just didn't die."

They looked at each other over the fire. Then Soren started laughing, and after a moment, so did Zayn.

*Yet*, I thought. *You just didn't die yet.* I kept it behind my teeth, with all the rest.

---

On the tenth night, Soren reached into his pack and pulled out a small clay pot with a wax seal, and two handfuls of dates he'd shaken down from the palms.

"Honey," he said, cracking the wax. "From a beekeeper in the salt towns. Cost me two whole Hounds. Don't tell anyone, it ruins my reputation as a dangerous exile."

Zayn went very still.

He told Soren then about the cloth bundles outside his tent, and the dates rolled in honey, and how he always saved them. He kept them for the hardest days. As if sweetness was something you could store up like water against a drought.

Soren listened. Then he dipped a date in the pot and held it out.

"Then let's have it on an easy day," he said. "For once. See what that's like."

Zayn took it.

They ate the whole pot. They licked their fingers and argued about which of the Embers was brightest, and I sat between them in the warm, and my tail glowed so steadily all night that I gave up trying to hide it. It was the best night. I've measured every night since against it.

I counted that one. I counted all of them. The thin Ash had gone and left the sky dark for a few nights, and now it was coming back, a little fuller every evening, like a cup being filled. Like a pyre being built, one stick at a time.

When Zayn told Soren that Old Hesset had sent him to find the City of Glass, Soren didn't even pretend to think about it.

"I'll come," he said. "I know roughly where it is. And frankly, you need someone with you who can read the signs."

"I can read *some* letters now."

"You can read your own name, Kiri's name and the word for *camel*." Soren grinned. "I'm coming."

My tail stayed bright. He meant it entirely.

I looked up at the Ash, filling. I didn't say anything. Out past the palms, in the dark, the thing that liked to listen shifted in the sand, and settled, and was patient.

---

*End of Chapter Nine*


---

# Chapter Ten
## The City of Glass

> *Here the first one said no, and the sand wept glass. Here the rest said yes, and no one wept at all.*
> — scratched into a glass wall, in a hand no one living can read

---

Night fourteen. We saw the City of Glass at sunset, which is the only proper way to see it, and the worst.

It rose out of the dunes like something the desert had coughed up and couldn't swallow back. Towers and broken arches and whole streets of glass: green as a bottle, black as oil, amber as honey, and here and there a cloudy red, like a bruise seen through skin. When the Fire went down behind it, the whole city burned from the inside, every tower lit like a lantern. Like a great many small pyres, standing in rows.

Soren stopped walking.

"Weaver's teeth," he said softly.

Zayn didn't say anything at all. His hand was on his chest. The mark under his shirt was hot, throbbing in time with his heartbeat. He told me later that it felt like something on the other side of the mark was pressing a hand against it, from inside, the way you'd press your palm to a door to feel whether the room beyond was on fire.

I sat down in the sand and did not look at it.

---

It had taken us four days to walk there from the Singing Wells. Zayn led us by the Embers, and Soren read us the old waymarkers. They were good days. I want you to know that. Every evening they sat by a small fire while Soren drew letters in the sand and Zayn copied them.

He'd learned a good many by then. His own name, and Soren's, and mine. *Water*, *fire*, and *camel*, which Soren insisted was the most useful word in any language. And on the twelfth night, sitting under the Embers, he'd asked Soren to show him the sky-words.

"Fire," Soren had said, drawing. "Embers. And Ash. That one's easy. Three letters. *Ah. Sss. Huh.*"

Zayn had copied it ten times. He didn't know yet why it mattered. Neither did Soren.

I did. I could have stopped the lesson. I didn't. I'm a great one for not stopping things.

---

"Here the first one said no," I said, when they'd both stared at the city long enough. "That's what the old stories say. The first bearer came here, a long time ago. There was an ordinary city here then, with markets and dogs and people quarrelling about the price of oil. He stood on that hill there," I nodded toward a glassy rise at its heart, "and the Weaver asked him to give the Light away, and he said *no*. And the Light went the wrong way. It went *inward*. It burned so hot the sand around him melted. The whole city. Everyone in it." I watched a cloud of glass-dust blow across the dunes. "The sand wept glass."

My tail was glowing. Every word of it was true.

"Everyone?" said Soren.

"Look at the walls when we're inside," I said. "Not too closely."

"How do you know all that?" Zayn asked.

"Stories," I said.

My tail stayed bright, because I'd *been* the story. But Zayn didn't know that, and I didn't tell him.

I'd stood on that hill. I'd watched a small, poor, furious boy with a shepherd's crook close his hands around the Light instead of opening them. I'd called his name over and over, and he'd looked straight at me and hadn't known who I was. Then the whole city below had screamed, all at once, and stopped, all at once, the way a scream stops when there's suddenly no air.

I hadn't been back in a very long time.

"Kiri?" said Zayn.

"I'm fine," I said. "Glass makes my paws cold. Let's get inside before the Hounds smell us."

My tail flickered. That was only half true, and Zayn saw it, and for once he was kind enough not to say so.

---

You have to walk through the streets to get to the great hall. There's no other way.

I tried not to look, and so I'll tell you what Soren saw, because he looked. He couldn't help it.

The walls of the City of Glass don't have names on them. They have *people* in them.

Shadows, mostly. Dark smudges inside the glass, where the sand had set around them before they could fall. A man with his arm thrown over his face. A woman curled over something small. Two shapes in a doorway, holding each other. Hundreds, along every street, like flies in honey. The last light threw their shadows onto the street, long and reaching, so that we had to walk on them.

Soren stopped in front of one. A child, near the ground, small enough to have been four or five. Face pressed to the inside of the glass. Mouth open.

"Don't," said Zayn quietly, and took his arm, and pulled him on.

Soren went. But he kept looking back. I heard him whispering, under his breath, the way he'd told us he whispered to Nim. He was just saying *I'm sorry, I'm sorry, I'm sorry*, to each one as we passed, all the way down the street.

That was Soren. I've never forgotten it.

---

The murals were in the great hall at the centre of the city.

The roof was gone, and the walls were glass all the way up, smooth and dark, and the evening came down through the open top in long violet bars. And in the walls, *inside* the walls, deep in the glass like the shadows in the streets, there were pictures.

Nobody had painted them. Nobody had carved them. They were simply *there*, floating in the glass the way your reflection floats in water. The glass had remembered them. Memory, set hard.

There were eleven.

They ran in a long curve around the hall, each one taller than a man. Under each one, set into the glass in pale gold, ran a line of letters.

Soren had gone to find the old cistern. "Don't touch anything," he said as he left. His voice was too bright. "Especially you, fox."

So it was only Zayn and me in the great hall when he started to read.

---

He went slowly. Eight days before, he hadn't known a single letter. Now he stood in front of each mural with his lips moving, sounding out the gold shapes one by one, the way a child sounds them out. *Thuh. Eh.* A long pause. His finger traced the letters on the glass without quite touching them. The glass breathed cold onto his hand, like a cellar door.

He couldn't read most of it. But he could pick out letters he knew, and every so often a whole word came clear for him like a coin turning up in the sand.

"*Smith*," he said, in front of the third mural. "The… Smith."

And then he stopped, and frowned, and looked at the picture properly for the first time.

It showed a smith's boy. Not a man. Broad in the shoulders from the bellows, but a boy. Thirteen, perhaps. He held a hammer in both hands, and the hammer was too big for him. His feet were bare.

Zayn moved to the next. *Twin*, he found, and a word he didn't know. Two figures, almost the same, both children, one reaching out a hand toward the other, who was turning away.

In the fifth he found *king*. A boy again. A crown on his head too large for it, slipping down over one ear.

In the seventh, *salt*. A boy with white crystals in his hair and on his eyelashes, like frost, smaller than any of them. Nine, maybe. He was smiling. The kind of smile children put on for grown-ups who are watching them be brave.

In the eighth, a falcon lifting from a wrist so slim the bird's talons wrapped all the way round it.

"Kiri," Zayn said. His voice had gone thin. "They're all—"

"Keep reading," I said.

He stopped in front of the first one for a long time.

It showed a small boy holding a crook. The boy was turning away, out of the picture, his face hidden. The glass all around him was black and cracked, as if something had struck it from inside. I didn't look at it. I looked at the floor.

"Is that—" Zayn said. "That's him. The boy with the sheep."

"Keep reading," I said.

He did.

The ninth mural showed a boy walking away from a dune with his hands pressed over his eyes. Not an old man. A boy. But something in the way his head tilted, as if listening for something no one else could hear. Zayn looked at it for a long while, and something moved across his face that he didn't put into words. I didn't help him. I'll tell you about that one later. I promise.

The eleventh was clouded, as if the glass had breathed on itself. You could just make out a figure running. No taller than Zayn. Nothing else.

And then he went back to the beginning and looked at them all again, together. That was when he saw it.

---

It wasn't the words that told him first. It was the pictures.

In the first, the boy with the crook was turning away. In the ninth, the boy with his hands over his eyes was walking away. In the eleventh, whoever it was was running. But in the other eight, the picture was the same.

A dune. A very high dune, so high that its top went up out of the picture into the dark. A full white Ash hanging above it, enormous, filling half the sky. And on the top of that dune, a child with its arms open, and fire rising up out of it like wings.

Eight times. Eight different children. A girl with long hair, the oldest of them, maybe fifteen. The smith's boy with his hammer dropped at his feet. The little king with his crown fallen off. The boy of salt, so small that the fire coming off him was taller than he was. Each one on that same dune, under that same Ash, with their arms open, and burning.

And the glass had remembered their faces. That was the thing he couldn't look away from. In the pictures where the children turned away, you couldn't see their faces. In the eight that burned, you could. The glass had kept exactly how each of them looked, in that moment, with the fire coming up through them.

Not one of them looked like a hero in a story.

Every one of them looked like a child who has just found out that it hurts.

Zayn had seen that picture before.

He'd seen it every time he used the Light. With Bram's little brother in his arms. On the caravan road, when he'd smelled his own hair burn. At the Singing Wells, with his palms blistering. Every time, a little longer, a little clearer, a little hotter.

He went back to the first of the eight, the girl with long hair. His finger moved along the gold letters underneath, past all the words he didn't know. Right to the very end, where there was one short word on its own.

Three letters.

*Ah. Sss. Huh.*

"*Ash*," Zayn whispered.

He went to the next mural, the smith's boy. The last word was *ash*.

The next. *Ash*.

The next. *Ash*. *Ash*. *Ash*. Eight times, eight murals, eight children, the same word at the end of each line. Soren had told him it was an easy one.

He stood in the middle of the great hall of the City of Glass with the night coming down on him through the broken roof, and he understood.

It wasn't a picture of something that *might* happen.

The Phoenix-Born didn't save everyone and go home. They didn't grow old. They were children, every one, and they climbed the Crown Dune under the full Ash and opened their arms and burned, every one of them who'd been brave enough, and the grey went away for a while, and they were *ash*.

*They are remembered*, Hesset had said, in his tent full of herbs and bells, when Zayn asked what happened after.

No bell had rung. It was true, after all. The glass remembered them. Perfectly. Forever. Their faces at the worst moment of their lives, set hard for anyone to look at.

---

He didn't cry. I'd half expected him to.

He just sat down on the glass floor, slowly, the way a very old man sits, and pulled his knees up to his chest. His hand was pressed flat over the feather mark. He was quiet for a long time. Through the glass floor beneath him, faintly, I could see more shadows, very deep down.

Then he said, without looking at me, "You knew."

"Yes."

My tail glowed. I couldn't have lied about that if I'd wanted to. I'm not even sure I wanted to.

"Hesset knew."

"Yes."

"They're all children," he said. "Kiri. They're all *children*. Why are they all children?"

I didn't answer. I looked up through the broken roof at the sky, where the first Embers were coming out, and I felt something rise in me that I'd been swallowing for a very long time. Eleven times I had walked a child toward that dune, and every time I'd told myself the Light simply settled in the young, the way frost settles on low ground.

I've had a long time to think about that, since. I'll tell you what I think at the end. You won't like it.

"Ask *it*," I said at last, and my voice came out so savage that he flinched. I didn't mean it for him. I was looking at the sky. "Go on. Ask it why. It's never once answered me."

He looked up at the eight burning children in the glass. In the dark, the gold in them shone faintly, like coals under ash.

And then he asked me.

He asked it in a very small voice. It was the voice of a seven-year-old sitting on the edge of a well, saying his own name over and over so he wouldn't forget it.

"Kiri," he said. "Is there a way I don't have to die?"

---

I want to tell you about that moment properly. I've told you I can't stand liars. I've watched my own tail go dark for them over and over, and every time, I've thought a little less of them.

I have walked beside eleven bearers. I'd never lied to one. Not once. Not even Malek, when he begged me to.

And I looked at that boy on the glass floor, with the word *ash* written eight times around him under eight small burning faces. I thought: *He's fifteen. He's only just learned to laugh. He has a friend for the first time in his life, and that friend is somewhere in this city right now apologising to the dead in the walls, and he'll come back soon, and they'll eat together and argue about the Embers.*

I thought: *Let him have that. Just a little while. Let him have a little longer before his face looks like theirs.*

So I said, "Yes."

---

My tail went dark.

Not dim. *Dark*. It went out all at once, so that the only light left was the faint gold of the burning children in the glass.

And somewhere in Zayn's pack, muffled in its cloth, Hesset's little bronze bell gave one small, clear note.

*Ting.*

It went up into the dark of the hall and came back from the glass, again and again, smaller each time. *Ting. Ting. Ting.* As if all eleven of them were ringing it.

Zayn was looking at my tail. He'd looked at it before he'd even finished asking. Of course he had.

He didn't say anything.

That was the worst part. I'd been ready for anything else. For him to shout, or call me a liar. But he just looked at my dark tail for a long moment, and then he looked away, up at the eight burning children, and he nodded slightly, as if I'd confirmed something.

Which, of course, I had.

"Okay," he said quietly.

That was all.

I've told a great many hard truths in my life. I've told mothers what happened to their sons. None of it ever hurt as much as that one small lie, and that one small word he gave me back.

I'll be honest with you now, because I owe somebody the truth about it. I don't know whether I lied to protect him, or to protect myself from watching his face when I said *no*.

Both, probably. People are rarely only one thing. Not even foxes. Especially not foxes who are only half of anything.

---

Soren came back with a full water skin and glass-dust in his hair, talking before he was through the doorway.

"There's a cistern under the east side, it's full, it's disgusting, there's something living in it, I *think* it's only a frog, it had the right number of legs. And there are more of them down there. In the walls. Zayn, there's a whole room of them, all sitting in rows, like they were waiting for someone to—"

He stopped.

He looked at Zayn on the floor. He looked at me, with my tail still dark as a dead coal. He looked up, slowly, at the eight burning children in the glass.

"What happened?"

Zayn told him.

He couldn't have done anything else. Lying wasn't open to him. He told him all of it: the dune, the Ash, the word *ash* eight times, the children, and the vision he'd been having since Harrow's End. That it had smell now, and heat. He told it in a flat, quiet voice, staring at the floor.

He didn't tell him what I'd said. He left that out. I've never forgotten that he left that out.

When he'd finished, Soren set down the water skin very carefully. Then he walked to the first of the eight murals and read it. Properly, out loud, every word. His voice came back off the glass, quieter, as if the hall were reading along.

"*The Weaver's Daughter*," he read, "*who in her fifteenth year climbed the Crown Dune under the full Ash and opened her hands, and the grey went away. And of her there remained only*—" His voice caught. "*Ash.*"

He went to the next. *In his fourteenth year.* And the next. *In his twelfth year.* The boy of salt: *In his ninth year.* His voice got quieter and harder with each one. He read all eight. Then he read the first, the ninth and the eleventh. *Refused.* *Refused.* *Refused.*

Then he stood in the middle of the hall with his fists clenched at his sides.

"*Ninth year*," he said. "That one was nine. Zayn. That one was younger than Idan."

"Soren—"

"*No*." He swung round. His eyes were too bright in the dark. "No. Look at them. Look at them, they're *babies*. Three of them said no. Three of them *lived*."

"The first one turned into the Hollow," Zayn said quietly. "Kiri told me. This whole city is what happened when he said no. The people in the walls."

"Then he said no *wrong*." Soren's voice rang off the glass. "Eight burned and three ran. That's two ways. Who says there are only two? Who *decided* that? Who decided it had to be *children*? Some sand? A wall?"

"The Weaver."

"Then the Weaver can explain itself to me in person." He jabbed his broken sword at the open roof, at the dark sky and the swelling Ash. "I mean it. I've already had one boy *taken away* from me because someone decided there was only one way things could go. I'm not standing here and letting it happen again. I'm *not*."

My tail flared back to life for him, all at once, so bright it hurt my own eyes. He meant every word.

I'll tell you something. When he jabbed that sword at the sky and shouted *who decided*, I loved him for it. It was the first time in all my years that anyone had shouted it with me.

It didn't help. It never does.

Zayn looked at him for a long time.

And there, on the floor of the City of Glass, something happened I'll remember until the Embers go out. Zayn smiled. Small and tired and sad. But real.

"Okay," he said. "A third way."

He didn't believe it. I could see that. But he'd seen how much Soren needed him to say it, and he'd said the only true thing he could find. He *would* look for one. That was true. My tail glowed faintly gold for him.

---

They slept in the great hall that night, because it was the only place in the city out of the wind. Neither of them would sleep in the streets, with the shadows.

Soren lay with his back to the wall and his broken sword across his chest, facing the doorway. He didn't say much else. Once, in the dark, I heard him say, "Idan would like this place," to nobody. Then, a long while later, so quietly I almost missed it: "No. He wouldn't." Then he was quiet.

Zayn didn't sleep for a long time.

He sat cross-legged on the glass with his pack open in front of him. He took out the broken golden feather and turned it over. In the dim light of the murals, it seemed to me that the break across its middle had closed. Only a little. Only the width of a hair. As if facing one hard truth had mended something in it. I've wondered since what it was mending itself *for*.

He didn't notice. He was looking at the twelfth place on the wall.

There was one. I'd known there would be. At the end of the long curve, after the clouded figure of the Runner, there was one more stretch of smooth, dark glass. Empty. Nothing in it at all.

Not yet.

And past it, if you looked, the wall didn't end. The glass went on, smooth and dark and empty, curving round the hall and away into the shadows by the door. Room for a thirteenth. Room for a great many more. Whoever had made this hall had not been planning for twelve.

Zayn looked at the twelfth place for a long time. Then he lay down beside Soren and closed his eyes.

I sat in the doorway and kept watch, with my tail glowing faintly in the dark. Above the broken roof, the Ash hung half full. It was halfway to the dune.

Some time after midnight, he moved.

He didn't wake. But his right hand came out from under his cloak, and the forefinger, the one with the torn nail, reached out and began to write in the thin film of glass-dust on the floor. Slowly, carefully.

*A. S. H.*

Then again, beside it. *A. S. H.* And again. Moving in a slow ring around himself, the way he'd ringed the well, until he was lying in the middle of a little circle of the word.

And then, at the end, in a hand that wasn't quite his, one more word, very small. Letters Soren hadn't taught him. The same shapes the dunes had written at him, that night on the ridge, when he'd stood in front of them and couldn't read a single one.

He couldn't read it now, either. He was asleep. But his finger knew it.

I wiped it all away before dawn, with my tail, every letter. I didn't tell him. I didn't tell Soren. You'll learn what the dunes were spelling when he did, and not before. That much, at least, I'll let him have.

I counted the night. Night fourteen. I wished I had never learned to count. I wished, for the first time in a very long time, that I had never learned to read.

---

*End of Chapter Ten*


---

# Chapter Eleven
## Riders of the Storm

> *Ride with the storm, never against it. And if the wind says your name, do not answer.*
> — what every lizard-rider's father tells him, before the first storm and after the last

---

Night eighteen. The Ash was fat and white and getting fatter, and I'd stopped looking at it, the way you stop looking at a stranger who has been standing at the edge of your camp for too many nights without speaking.

We'd left the City of Glass three days before, walking east toward the free towns. Soren had decided that if the walls didn't know a third way, somebody alive might. "Everyone in this desert has a different version of every story," he said. "One of them has to be the one where the child lives."

Nobody answered. The walls had shown us eleven bearers set in glass, and all of them were children. You don't argue with a wall like that. You just stop sleeping well.

Zayn walked. He found water. He sat by the fire in the evenings and copied the letters Soren drew, and when Soren made jokes he laughed at them, a little too quickly, like someone paying a debt to a man who might come back with a knife.

I didn't say much. I'd told one lie in that glass hall and it had used up all my talking. Zayn hadn't mentioned it once. He still let me sleep against his side. Somehow that was worse.

On the morning of the eighteenth day, Zayn stopped on top of a dune and sniffed the air.

"Storm," he said.

Soren sniffed too. "I don't smell anything."

"Like an old coin. Like a coin someone's been holding in their mouth." Zayn was looking south, where the sky along the edge of the world had gone the yellow of an old bruise, the kind that never quite heals. "It's coming fast." He frowned. "And something else."

I'd smelled it for an hour and hoped I was wrong. Smoke without fire. Ash without heat. Burning cloth a long way off, and under it the sweet, flat smell of a room where somebody has been dead for days and nobody has opened the door.

The Hollow.

From the dunes behind us came a snarl like tearing cloth.

---

They came over the ridge to the west in a long grey line.

Hounds first: eight, ten, more, running low, with darkness streaming off their backs like smoke off a wet fire. They don't pant, Hounds. You notice that, after a while. They run and run and never breathe, because there is nothing inside them that needs to.

Behind the Hounds, on sand lizards, came men.

I'll describe the lizards, since you've never seen one. Long and low and dune-coloured, with a frilled crest that flares red when they're angry, which is always. Their eyes roll independently, one forward, one back, so a lizard is always watching where it's been. Faster than any camel. They bite, and they don't let go.

The men on them wore grey robes. Their faces were smooth white masks with nothing on them: no eyes, no mouth, no nose. A blank white oval, like an egg, like the inside of a skull scraped clean. They didn't turn their heads to look at us. They didn't need to. The masks were all pointed the same way, at the same boy, the way flowers point at the Fire.

Each one had a blade at his hip that didn't shine. It was grey, the flat, swallowed grey of a sky with nothing behind it.

The Unnamed.

Twelve of them. Zayn counted under his breath, and I watched the colour go out of him number by number.

They were humming. No words, no tune, rising and falling with the lizards' strides, and it came across the sand without getting any quieter, the way a sound does in a dream.

"Friends of yours?" said Soren.

"They hunt me."

"Of course they do." Soren drew his broken sword. "Twelve of them, ten Hounds, two of us and one small rude fox. I like those odds."

From somewhere in Zayn's pack, muffled, came a small *ting*.

"I don't like those odds," Soren admitted. "Run."

---

Nobody outruns a sand lizard on foot. By the time they'd stumbled down into the next hollow, the first rider was cresting the dune behind them, his lizard sliding down the slope in a hiss of sand with its crest blazing.

Soren turned, planted his feet, and waited.

The rider came at him with the grey blade swinging low. Soren stepped *toward* it, which is not what anyone sensible does. He ducked under the swing, caught the rider's sleeve, and *pulled*, and the masked man went off the side of the lizard into the sand. Soren went up onto its back in the same movement.

The man he'd thrown didn't get up straight away. He lay on his back in the sand with that blank white face turned to the sky, and from behind the mask, in a young man's voice, he said quite clearly:

"*Tell my mother I'll be back for supper.*"

Then he got up, like a puppet lifted by the back of its shirt, and walked toward Zayn.

I have heard a lot of things in my life. That one stayed with me. It was the voice of somebody who had said those words once, on an ordinary evening, and had never been allowed to say anything else.

"Zayn!" Soren wrenched the lizard round. "*Come on!*"

A second lizard had come down the slope. Its rider was turning to slash at Soren's back. Zayn ran at it, ducked the snapping jaws, grabbed the rider's ankle with both hands, and hauled. The masked man fell without a sound. Zayn scrambled up onto the scaly back in his place.

The lizard went mad underneath him, bucking and spinning, and Zayn grabbed two fistfuls of frill and clamped his knees. I leapt, landed half on his head, and clawed my way down into his lap, and he didn't even yell at me.

Soren was already racing away. Zayn's lizard saw its friend go and decided, as lizards do, that wherever that was looked better than here.

It ran.

---

The dunes went past in a blur. Behind us came the Unnamed, a grey tide pouring over every ridge, and under the thunder of claws, steady and unhurried, that humming. Ahead, the sky had gone black, and the storm was coming up to meet us like a wall falling over.

"*Left!*" Soren shouted. "We go left, around it—"

"*No!*" Zayn's voice cracked. "*With it! Ride with it!*"

"*What?*"

"*Wind's from the south! Turn across it and it'll knock you flat! Go where it's going!*"

I'll give Soren this. He'd spent most of a month learning from Zayn how not to die, and he didn't waste a breath. He hauled his lizard's head round, straight into the storm where the wind ran north. Zayn followed.

And the storm swallowed us.

---

The world went brown. Then it went dark.

Sand everywhere, roaring like the sea, so thick Zayn couldn't see his own hands. He pulled his cloak over his face and rode blind. I pressed myself flat against his stomach and felt his heart going like a trapped bird's.

And in the wind, there were voices.

There always are, in a big storm. The old riders say it's only the sand singing through the dunes. But I know the difference between the sand singing and the sand *talking*. Dozens of voices, faint and close at our ears, worn smooth with repeating:

*Is that you? Is someone there? I can't see. I can't see. Where did everyone go?*

And then, under all of them, close enough that I felt Zayn go rigid:

*Zayn.*

Not shouted. Said. Gently. The way someone says your name when they've found you after a long time looking.

"Don't answer it," I said into his stomach. My first words to him in three days. "Whatever it sounds like. *Don't answer it.*"

He didn't. I felt him bite down on it.

Behind us, the Unnamed tried to cut across.

I heard it happen. A lizard screamed as its rider turned it sideways into the wind, and the storm caught it like a sail and flung it away into the dark. Then another. Then a Hound howled, a howl like nothing I'd heard from them before, as the wind tore the shadow off it in strips and there turned out to be nothing underneath.

The riders didn't scream when they went. They just kept humming, right up until the storm took the sound away.

*Ride with the storm, never against it.*

Zayn had learned that from no one: from a lifetime of crouching alone in a patched tent, listening to which way the seams were straining while the voices in the wind said things outside. Most of the things that keep you alive don't seem useful until they do.

One rider was still with us, close behind, running in the shelter of our lizards. Zayn didn't see him until the grey blade came out of the brown, low and fast.

He twisted. It missed his leg and caught his forearm, a shallow cut along the old golden cracks.

It barely bled. That's the horror of them. They barely bleed.

But for one long heartbeat, Zayn couldn't remember what honey tasted like.

He knew he'd eaten it. There had been a pot, and dates, and someone laughing. But the sweetness itself was gone, cut out clean, and where it had been was a cold blank with smooth edges, like the place a tooth used to be. His tongue kept going back to it.

Then it seeped back, slowly. Mostly. He gasped.

I don't think it all came back. It never does. The grey blades don't cut flesh so much as they cut *you*, and you heal the way a dune heals over a footprint: smooth on top, and something missing underneath that you'll never be able to point to.

Soren's lizard came round out of the storm, and the broken sword came down, and the rider behind us was gone. His mask spun away into the dark, still humming.

---

We came out of the worst of it into a place of stone.

Ruins, older than anything Zayn had ever dug in. A long road ran between rows of carved pillars, half buried, half fallen, like the ribs of something that had died on its back. There were faces carved on the pillars, and every one had had its eyes chiselled out. Not weathered. Chiselled, carefully, all the way down the road.

The storm still howled overhead, but between the pillars the wind broke and swirled, and for a moment we could see.

The lizards didn't like it.

Soren's stopped so suddenly he went over its head into the sand. Zayn's reared, hissing, and threw us both, and by the time Zayn had rolled to his feet the lizards were gone, vanishing back into the brown with their crests blazing. They'd rather the storm. Animals are wise about places.

"Well," said Soren, spitting out sand. "That's gratitude."

Then the ground shook.

One of the great pillars beside him had been leaning for a thousand years. The storm had been leaning on it for an hour. Now, with a long groan like the wall in the Old Ruins on the morning this story began, it gave up.

"*Soren!*"

He threw himself sideways. He almost made it.

The pillar came down across his legs with a sound like the end of the world. Soren didn't scream. He made a short, punched-out noise, as if every bit of air had been knocked out of him, and then he lay very still, face-down from the knees under a tower of carved stone.

A blind stone face lay a hand's width from his cheek, staring at him with no eyes.

"I'm all right," he said into the sand. His voice was thin. "Nothing's broken. I think nothing's broken. It's just *very heavy*."

Zayn got his shoulder under the edge and heaved until the cracks on his arms flared, and the stone didn't so much as tremble. It weighed as much as a house. It weighed as much as a life.

From the storm behind us, out of the dark between the pillars, came a snarl like tearing cloth.

---

Six Hounds. Four masked riders on foot, lizards lost, grey blades drawn. They came out of the swirling brown slowly, because they could see there was no hurry. A boy who couldn't move a stone. A prince who couldn't move at all. A fox.

The riders stopped in a line. They tilted their blank white faces, all four of them, to exactly the same angle at exactly the same moment.

"*Is that you?*" said one, in an old woman's voice.

"*I'll be back for supper,*" said another.

"*Don't let it in,*" said the third. "*Don't let it in. Don't let it in.*"

The fourth said nothing. The fourth had a child's voice, I learned later, and had nothing left to say at all.

Zayn reached for the Light. It came grey and thin and sputtering, the way it had with the Hounds at the Wells, because he wasn't thinking about anyone else now. Terror filled him to the brim and left no room for anything gold.

The Hounds grinned their shadow grins. They have too many teeth when they grin. More than they have when their mouths are shut.

"Zayn," said Soren from the sand. "Go. *Go.* Run with the storm. You know how. Leave me—"

"*No.*"

The first Hound sprang.

And I stopped being a small fox.

---

I'll tell you how it feels, because nobody has ever asked me.

It feels like drowning in reverse. Something cold and enormous that has been folded small inside you for a very long time begins to *unfold*, and it doesn't care what it breaks on the way out.

My silver eye goes first. It doesn't burn; it *opens*, wider than an eye should, and behind it there's no fox at all. Then the dark comes up out of the place where half of me used to be. It isn't a part of me. It's a part of *it*, a piece of the Hollow I swallowed long ago, trying to drag someone out. It has always been patient.

I grew.

You should hear the sound it makes: bones that aren't bones lengthening like wet rope pulled tight, my jaw unhinging, and unhinging again. I grew until my head was level with the tops of the pillars. My fur went black, the black that was there before the Weaver lit anything, and it didn't lie on me; it *poured* upward off me, like smoke going back into a fire. My mouth filled with teeth, row behind row, the way the Hounds' do. My eyes burned like coals in a cave.

And I could hear them. The ones inside the dark. Every Husk the Hollow has ever emptied, all the last words of everyone who has ever been forgotten, all of it rushing through me like wind through a ruined house. *Is someone there. Where did everyone go. Don't let it in.*

One voice under the others, quiet and familiar, saying a name I won't repeat.

The Hound that had sprung at Zayn hit my chest and simply came apart, like fog walking into a wall.

I put my front paws, which were not paws anymore, under the fallen pillar. The one Zayn couldn't move. I lifted it the way you'd lift a stick. Soren dragged himself out on his elbows, and I heard him make a sound when he looked up and saw what was holding it. It was not a brave sound. I don't blame him.

And then, because I was angry, and because the Hollow part of me is always angry, I threw it.

It went end over end through the swirling sand and came down on the four masked men with a sound I won't describe to you. I'll only tell you that the humming stopped. All four voices at once, mid-note, the way a door cuts off a room.

The other Hounds turned to run. I caught two. I didn't need my teeth. I closed my mouth around them and they went into the dark inside me like water into sand, and the dark was glad. That's the part I've never told anyone. *It was glad.*

The rest melted back into the storm.

Then it was quiet, except for the wind overhead and my own breath, which was not a fox's breath. It came out of me in long, slow drafts, cold enough to frost the sand.

I turned round. A huge shadow-beast with ember eyes and the dark pouring up off my back, my head among the broken pillars, my long jaw still working on nothing. I looked down at the boy I'd spent fifteen years protecting.

---

I've hidden that form from every bearer I ever walked with. All eleven. Not one of them ever saw it.

I told myself it was to keep them from being afraid of me.

It was really to keep me from seeing their faces when they were.

Zayn's face, looking up at me in that storm, was the worst thing I've ever seen. And I have seen eleven children burn.

He didn't know it was me. All he saw was a monster of the Hollow standing over Soren with its mouth full of teeth, the biggest thing of the grey he'd seen since the Veilbeast. And somewhere in all the voices pouring out of me, one said his name. I didn't mean it to. I don't know whose voice it used. All he felt was fear, pure and total, filling him like water filling a well, all the way to the top and over.

"*Get away from him!*"

The Light came out of him like the end of the world.

It wasn't gold. It wasn't grey. It was both, twisted together, pouring out of his hands and his chest and his eyes in a raging white torrent, gold shot through with grey like smoke through fire. He didn't aim it. He couldn't have. It *burst* out of him, and it hit me in the chest.

Light and Hollow don't mix. Nothing in the world burns the dark the way the Light does.

I howled.

I had never howled before. It came out of me broken and many-throated, every voice inside me screaming at once, and went ringing off the pillars and up into the storm. The Light tore into the Hollow half of me like fire into dry grass, and for one moment I felt the dark in me *afraid*, which I had never felt and hope never to feel again. It burned my silver eye. It burned down into the cold place where the dark lived, looking for the bottom, and it *hurt*. Weaver help me. More than the Hollow did, the first time it took me.

I fell.

And as I fell, I got smaller. The dark ran out of me like water from a broken jar, and the voices went with it, the quiet familiar one last of all. The teeth went back to one small, ordinary row. By the time I hit the sand I was only a fox, scorched and smoking down one flank, my silver eye squeezed shut and weeping something that wasn't tears.

The light went out of Zayn all at once. He stood there swaying. His arms were cracked gold from wrist to shoulder.

"Kiri?" he whispered.

---

The vision came the moment the Light went out, the way it always did now, like a debt being collected. Longer this time. Clearer. Closer.

The full Ash, so near he could see the grey stains on its face like old fingerprints. The Crown Dune beneath his feet. The fire climbing his arms, and this time he could *feel* it: not pain yet, but heat, rising, the way the air rises off a cooking-stone just before you touch it. Somebody below him on the dune, shouting. And for the first time, the sand under his bare feet, cold as a grave, and how the cold and the heat met somewhere around his knees.

Then it let him go, and he was on his knees in the ruins, and he could still smell his own hair burning. He touched it. It wasn't. He touched it again anyway.

"Kiri," he said. "*Kiri.* I didn't know— I didn't— Kiri, I'm sorry, I'm so sorry—"

I didn't look at him. I couldn't.

"Don't look at me," I said.

That was the last thing I said to him for a long time.

---

The storm blew itself out by nightfall.

They made camp among the pillars, because Soren couldn't walk far; his legs were bruised black, but by some miracle nothing was broken. Zayn built a fire from a dead thornbush and turned the nearest stone face toward the dunes so its empty sockets wouldn't watch us. Then he gathered me into his lap, scorched side up, and curled himself around me.

He kept saying he was sorry into my fur until the words stopped sounding like words. Near the middle of the night he cried himself to sleep, and even asleep his hands kept checking that I was still there.

I lay in his arms with my good eye half open, and I watched Soren.

Soren didn't sleep either.

He waited until Zayn's breathing had gone slow, then got up, wincing, and limped toward the fallen pillar. I think he wanted to see if any of them were alive. Soren was like that, even with them.

One of them was. Barely.

He lay apart from the others, half under the stone. His mask had cracked down the middle, and Soren had to pull at it. It didn't want to come. It had grown to his face at the edges, the way bark grows over a nail. Under it was a face much younger than I'd expected, thin and grey and frightened, pale as a thing kept too long in the dark. Not much older than Soren.

The young man's eyes found Soren's. For a moment, they were his own eyes.

His lips moved. I couldn't hear. Soren bent down close. Whatever he said, it took all he had, and then his lips stopped, and his eyes stayed open on the Ash.

Soren knelt there a long while. Then he reached into the dead man's robe and drew something out.

A letter. Folded small and sealed with grey wax.

He held it up to the Ash, bright enough now to read by, and went still: the stillness of a man who sees a familiar face at a window where no one should be. He'd seen that seal before, at the bottom of the order that sent him out of Veimore with half a sword. The personal seal of the Vizier Orrin.

He broke it and read.

I'll tell you what it said, because you'll need to know, and because Zayn couldn't have read it then even if he'd seen it. It was signed *The Voice*. Soft, careful handwriting, every letter perfectly formed, like the hand of someone who has never once been in a hurry. It gave orders. On the Night of Embers, when every lantern in Veimore was lit for the lost bearers, King Absalon was to die.

Soren read it twice. His hand was shaking.

Then he folded it very small. He twisted the bronze pommel off the hilt of his broken sword and slid the letter into the hollow where the prayer was supposed to go. He screwed the pommel back on, tight.

He came back to the fire and sat beside us with the sword across his knees. He looked at Zayn asleep, the gold still glowing faintly up both arms. He looked at me, and I didn't pretend to be asleep, and he didn't pretend he hadn't seen what I'd been that afternoon. Neither of us said a word.

He didn't wake Zayn. He didn't tell him.

I think he meant to. I think he was waiting until he knew what to do with it. I think he wanted Zayn, for one more night, to carry only one terrible thing instead of two.

We were a fine pair, the prince and I. Two keepers of secrets, sitting up in the dark among blind stone faces, watching over the same sleeping boy.

Above us the Ash hung nearly full, white and patient, like an eye that has decided not to blink.

---

*End of Chapter Eleven*


---

# Chapter Twelve
## The Night of the Full Ash

> *Under the full Ash, every promise is weighed. And what is found wanting is taken.*
> — from the oath-sayings of the free towns, spoken over water at weddings and at graves

---

I didn't speak again before the full Ash.

I'll tell you about those days anyway. You can be silent all the way through a story and still have to describe every minute of it.

I limped behind the two of them with my fur scorched brown down one side and my silver eye swollen shut. The burn wept a thin black fluid that smoked when it touched the sand. I didn't eat much. At night I slept away from the fire, and every night Zayn looked over at me before he lay down, and every night I turned my face away.

He tried to say sorry. Sometimes out loud, sometimes by setting water beside me in his cupped hands. Once he left me the best piece of a roasted sand-hen and sat with his back to me so I could eat unwatched. I ate it after he'd fallen asleep. I want that on the record.

I wasn't angry with him. You need to understand that, because he didn't. I wasn't angry at all.

I was ashamed. He had seen what I was. He had heard the voices that live in me. And there is no word for how that feels, except that it's the opposite of being remembered.

---

Soren was different too, after the storm.

He still joked, still complained about his feet and the general unfairness of sand. He still drew letters every evening, and Zayn could read whole short lines now. One night Soren wrote ZAYN AND SOREN, MONSTERS in big letters beside the fire and made him read it aloud, and they both laughed, and for a moment the dark beyond the firelight felt a long way off.

Remember those letters. I'm sorry. I have to ask you to remember them.

But Soren had started touching the pommel of his sword, resting his thumb on the bronze cap the way Zayn touched his feather mark. And when he thought no one was looking, he'd stand on a dune and stare west toward Veimore, the way you stare at a house where you know someone is waiting for you in the dark.

"We're changing direction," he announced on the fourth morning. "West. I need to go home."

Zayn stared at him. "You were exiled."

"I know. I was there."

"They'll kill you."

"They'll *try*." Soren's grin came and went too fast, like a lantern swinging past a window. "I need to see Idan. I need to make sure he's all right."

My tail glowed. That was true. Every word.

But Zayn had learned to watch the spaces between words, too. He looked at Soren a long moment. He knew there was something more. And because Zayn had never in his life asked anyone for something they didn't want to give, he didn't ask.

I knew what it was. I'd watched it go into the hilt. I didn't say anything either.

Three of us walking west together, and every one of us carrying something we didn't say. You could have heard the silence from a dune away.

---

Every night, the Ash got fuller.

Zayn stopped looking at it. Then he started looking at it all the time. He'd lie on his back by the fire with his hand on his chest, staring up at that white face as it swelled a little more each evening, and I knew what he was seeing. The dune. The fire. The sound of somebody shouting a name at the bottom of the world.

He'd started waking in the night and touching his arms, his hair, his face, quickly, the way you pat out sparks. Checking. Once I saw him hold his hand an inch above the fire and keep it there, his face blank, as if he were trying to learn the feeling in advance. Soren knocked his wrist away and said nothing, and Zayn said nothing, and they didn't speak of it.

He knew the Crown Dune was nowhere near. But every full Ash would bring it one turn closer, and this was the first one since he'd read the word *ash* at the end of eight lines on a glass wall.

On the twenty-ninth night, it rose full.

It came up out of the eastern dunes huge and white and close, filling half the sky, exactly as in his vision. The desert went silver. You could see for miles, and nothing moved in any of them, not a beetle, not a breath of wind. The desert was holding still the way an animal does when something larger passes overhead.

It was the brightest night Zayn had ever seen, and he sat by the fire and couldn't stop shaking.

One turn of the Ash. Thin to full, and the dark between. That's how long they'd had.

I knew it. I'd counted. Neither of them knew.

---

It started, as the worst things do, with someone trying to help.

"Come to Veimore with me," Soren said.

He'd been quiet a long time, sitting across the fire turning his ring round and round on his finger. Now he leaned forward, and his face in the firelight was serious in a way Zayn had hardly ever seen it.

"I mean it. Under the city there are the catacombs, and in the catacombs there's the Archive. Every book anyone in Veimore ever wrote. Every story. Every name. Thousands of years of them, Zayn, rooms and rooms, all the way down into the dark. If there's a third way written anywhere in this desert, it's there. You can read now. Not fast. But we could look together."

"Soren—"

"I can get you in. I know a way through the kitchens. The cook would hide an army if I asked her nicely and brought back her honey jars."

"I can't go to Veimore."

"Why not?"

"Because it's not *there*." Zayn's voice cracked. "It's not in a book. You read them. In the glass. You read them out loud to me. Eight of them burned, and three of them ran, and the ones who ran—" He pointed east, at nothing, at the empty silver desert. "Look what happened. One of them *is* the Hollow. That's the third way, Soren. That's the only other way there is. You become the thing in the storm."

"You don't *know* that."

"I've *felt* it! Every time I use the Light I'm standing on that dune and I can feel the fire coming up my legs, I can *smell* it, I wake up and I can still smell it—" He stopped. He pressed his hand flat over his chest. "It isn't a story anymore. It's going to happen. It's *written*."

"Then we unwrite it."

"You can't unwrite the *sand*!"

The wells, the glass, the storm: none of it had been as loud as that. The words went out across the silver dunes and the desert swallowed them whole and gave nothing back, not even an echo.

They sat there breathing hard on either side of the fire.

Soren looked away first. He looked down at his hands. When he spoke again, his voice had gone quiet.

"Promise me something," he said.

Zayn didn't answer.

"Promise me you'll never give up." Soren looked up. His eyes were very bright. "Whatever happens. Whatever you see in your visions. Whatever the walls say. You keep going. You don't just *lie down* and let it take you. Promise me."

Zayn looked at him across the fire, under the full Ash.

He could not lie. You know that. So when he said it, he said it knowing that the feather on his chest would have burned like a coal if he hadn't meant it with everything he had.

"I promise," said Zayn.

It didn't burn.

My tail lit up the sand like a second fire.

And somewhere out in the silver dark, a long way off, something answered. Not a Hound. Not a voice. A single soft sound, like a heavy book being closed.

Neither of them heard it. I did. I've never known what it was. I've been afraid for a very long time that I do.

---

If they'd stopped there, this would be a different story.

They didn't stop there.

"Good," said Soren. He let out a long breath, almost a laugh. "Good. Then come to Veimore."

"That's not—"

"You just promised."

"I promised not to give up. Going to the dune isn't giving up." Zayn's voice was shaking. "Don't you see? It's the *opposite*. The ones who gave up are the ones who ran. If I keep going, Soren, *that's* where I'm going. That's what not giving up *is*, for me. It's walking into the fire with my eyes open."

Soren stood up so fast he nearly kicked the fire.

"No," he said. "No. I'm not doing this again. I'm not standing by while someone I—" He bit it off. "I'm not letting them take you. I'll drag you to Veimore by your ankles. I'll lock you in the catacombs. I'll find the third way myself and read it to you through the door. I am *not* losing anybody else."

"You can't save me."

"Watch me."

And then Zayn said it.

He didn't plan it. I know that, because I know him. He was frightened, so frightened, and tired past the point where tiredness has a bottom, and the Ash hung full and white above him like an eye that wouldn't close. Soren was standing over him promising things nobody could promise. And it hurt so much to be loved like that, when he knew exactly how it ended, that something in him reached for the sharpest true thing it could find, and threw it.
