"You can't save me," he said. "You couldn't even save Nim."

---

The fire cracked.

That was the only sound in the whole desert.

Soren didn't move. He didn't shout. His face didn't even change, at first. He stood there with the full Ash behind him, looking down at Zayn, and something went out of his eyes. It was like watching a lantern go out on a high red wall, one of the ones that must never all go out, and then the next one along, and the next.

My tail glowed. I wish it hadn't. But it was true, every word, and nothing in the world cuts like the truth said at the wrong moment for the wrong reason.

Zayn saw his face. He saw what he'd done.

He got up. He didn't say sorry. He couldn't make his mouth do it. He turned round and walked away from the fire, out into the silver dark.

"Zayn," said Soren behind him.

Just his name. Once. Not loud.

Zayn didn't turn round.

I've thought about that moment more than any other in this story. More than the dune, more than the fire. A boy walking away across moonlit sand, and another boy standing alone by a small fire, saying a name once into the wind.

You know what the rule is. *Never say your name to the wind.*

Nobody ever told Soren what happens when you say someone else's.

He meant to come back. You should know that. He meant to walk until he'd stopped shaking, then come back and make it right. There'd be time in the morning. That's the oldest lie there is, and the feather never burns for it, because nobody knows it's a lie until afterwards.

He walked away and left Soren alone.

It was the worst mistake he ever made. He'd tell you so himself.

---

And where was I?

I'll tell you the first part.

I'd smelled Hounds that evening, before the argument started. A long way off, but coming, drawn by the Light still glowing in the cracks on Zayn's arms. I'd been lying a little way from the fire with my scorched side to the cold sand, listening. When Zayn walked off into the dark, I got up and went the other way.

I went hunting.

I let the Hollow half out. It hurt worse than it ever had, and the voices inside were louder that night, as if the full Ash had pulled them up near the surface the way it pulls the sea. But there were Hounds in the dunes and the boy I loved was alone out there, and that is the only use I've ever found for the thing that I am.

I hunted them across the silver sand under the full Ash, for most of the night.

That's all I'm going to tell you about where I was. Don't ask me any more. Not yet.

I said, *don't ask*.

---

Zayn didn't go far. He climbed a high dune a long way off and sat at the top, knees pulled up to his chest, the Ash pouring white light down on him until he cast no shadow at all. He could still see the fire, very small, a single orange point down in the hollow where he'd left it.

He said sorry to it. Out loud, over and over, practising.

*I'm sorry. I didn't mean it. You were right to try. You were right to try to save him. You were right to try to save me.*

He went through it again and again, getting the words right, the way he'd copied his name in the sand until the letters were perfect.

Some time after the middle of the night, he looked down, and the little orange point had gone out.

He told me later he'd thought nothing of it. Fires burn down. He said it twice, and the second time his mark burned, faintly, and he had to start over. *I told myself it was nothing.* That one was true.

Near dawn, with the Ash sinking into the western dunes and the sky going the grey of old bone, he fell asleep sitting up.

He didn't dream. No visions, no warnings. He felt nothing at all. Not a whisper in the sand. Not a single bell.

The Weaver was very quiet that night. I've never forgiven it for that either. It's a long list, the things I haven't forgiven it for. Most of them are quiet nights.

---

He woke when the Fire touched his face. He scrambled up and ran down the dune, sliding and stumbling, the words ready in his mouth. *I'm sorry. You were right to try.*

He came over the last rise and stopped.

There were camels in the hollow. Five of them, kneeling, with blocks of salt strapped to their sides. And there were men, five desert travellers in dusty white, standing in a ring around the cold black circle of the fire.

They weren't talking. That was the first thing. Five grown men and not one of them talking. One had his hand over his mouth.

They were looking down at something on the sand.

Zayn walked toward them. He didn't feel his legs moving. The morning was very bright and very still, and the only sound was a falcon on one man's wrist, screaming and screaming in its hood, the way animals scream at something they can smell and can't see.

Soren lay on his back beside the dead fire, one arm flung out, his broken sword in the sand by his open hand.

He was grey.

Not Husk-grey. Not emptied. Something worse. His skin had gone the colour of old ash, every part of it, his lips, his eyelids, the beds of his nails. And running all through the grey, across his face and down his throat and along both arms, were fine bright lines of *gold*. Thin glowing cracks, like the glaze on a broken pot. They were still glowing. Faintly. Fading even as Zayn watched, one by one, the way the last sparks go in a fire nobody is feeding.

Like the cracks the Light left on Zayn's own skin.

His eyes were open, looking up at the morning sky. They had gone pale. Not white. Pale, like a window someone has breathed on.

His mouth was a little open too, as though he'd been about to say something. As though someone had come up to the fire in the dark, and he'd looked up, and started to say a name.

The sand around him was smooth. Perfectly smooth, the way the wind leaves it, for ten paces in every direction. No struggle. No tracks. As if the desert had tidied up after itself.

And beside the fire, untouched, not a grain out of place, the letters he'd written in the sand that evening, before the argument, and never rubbed out. Zayn hadn't noticed they were still there. He noticed now.

ZAYN AND SOREN, MONSTERS.

Except the first word had been smoothed away. Just that one. The rest were perfect.

---

One of the travellers saw Zayn first.

An older man with a beard gone yellow at the edges. He backed away a step, then another. The others turned and saw, and went still.

They looked at Zayn. They looked at his arms, at the golden cracks still glowing faintly from wrist to shoulder.

They looked at Soren, cracked with the same gold.

"That's him," said the young man with the falcon. His voice was high and thin. "The prince. The exiled one. The *king's son*. Look at the jaw, the scar. They said in the free towns he was out here selling ash—"

"And *that*," said the old man, staring at Zayn, "is the glowing boy."

Zayn didn't move.

"From the caravan," the old man said. "They talked about him at the last well. The one the beast didn't eat. A man came out of the sand alive and he was telling everyone." He was backing toward his camel. "*Monster*, he said. And here he is."

"I didn't," said Zayn.

It came out of him like something broken. He looked at Soren, and back at them.

"I didn't do this."

The feather mark didn't burn. He felt it not burn. It was true, every word. And it didn't matter at all, because these men had never heard of a boy who couldn't lie, and if they had, they wouldn't have believed it.

There were other true things, too. They came up in him one after another, like water rising in a well that's about to drown you.

*I left him.*

*I said the worst thing I could think of, and I left him alone in the dark, and I watched his fire go out and I told myself it was nothing.*

"I left him," said Zayn. "I walked away. I left him alone."

The feather didn't burn for that either.

The travellers stared at him. And then one of them, I never learned which, said it: the word that would follow Zayn across the whole desert, carried on falcons' legs and in traders' mouths, faster than any lizard could run.

"*Prince-Killer.*"

They ran.

They were on their camels and away in moments, salt-blocks swinging, the falcon still screaming. East, toward the free towns, toward the wells. Toward everyone. I could hear them shouting it to each other as they went, until the dunes swallowed the sound.

*Prince-Killer. Prince-Killer. The glowing boy killed the prince.*

By the next turn of the Ash there wouldn't be a town in the desert that hadn't heard it. They'd paint it on doors, I heard later. Not his name. He didn't have one. Just that.

---

Zayn knelt in the sand beside Soren.

He didn't touch him at first. He knelt there with his hands open on his knees and the gold fading in Soren's face, crack by crack, until there were only a few lines left glowing at the corners of his eyes, like the last of something trying to get out.

Then, very slowly, he reached out to close Soren's eyes.

They didn't want to close. The lids were stiff and cold as wax and slid back open, a little, both times. The third time he held his fingers there and counted, the way you count through a pain, and when he let go they stayed. Mostly. A thin pale line of eye still showed beneath each lid, watching nothing.

He wanted to heal him. Of course he did. His hands were already burning. He laid them on Soren's chest and called the Light, and it came, gold and desperate, more gold than it had ever come for anyone. It poured into Soren and went looking.

It found nothing. Not a dark place, not an emptiness like a Husk's, where something had been scooped out. *Nothing.* As if there had never been anyone in there. The Light went down and down and touched no bottom, and came back to him cold.

He'd never seen it fail before.

And then, because it always did now, the vision came. Not of Soren. There was no past or future in Soren to see. Only his own: the full Ash, the dune, the fire climbing. But this time, for one breath, he wasn't alone in it. Somebody was standing on the sand below him, just at the edge of the light, very still, looking up. He couldn't see the face.

Then it was gone, and he was kneeling in the morning with his hands on a cold chest.

He took Soren's hand. It was cold. The fingers were grey and cracked with gold, and on the right hand, on the finger where the heavy ring had always been, there was nothing. Only a band of paler skin where it had sat for years. The ring he never took off.

Zayn looked at that bare finger for a long time.

He looked at his own arms, at the cracks. Then at the cracks on Soren's face. The same gold. The same light. And a question came into his eyes that I could see as clearly as if it had been written in the sand. He'd been asleep. He'd been far away. He'd felt nothing. But the Light had burst out of him once already when he didn't mean it to, and nearly killed me.

He thought of the little orange point going out.

*Did I do this?*

He would carry that question for a long time. I'm sorry to tell you that. I could have taken it from him. I'm sorry to tell you that, too.

---

The broken sword was lying in the sand by Soren's open hand.

Zayn picked it up.

It was lighter than he'd expected. Half a blade, dark leather on the grip, a bronze pommel that didn't quite match. It was cold. Everything in that hollow was cold, though the Fire was well up by then. He held it across his knees the way Soren used to hold it by the fire at night.

He didn't know what was inside it. Not yet.

He sat there with the sword in his lap and his friend beside him while the Fire climbed and the shadows of the dunes went short. Then the wind came, and began, gently, the way it does, to move the sand. A little over Soren's hand first. A little into the open fold of his collar. The desert, doing what the desert does with everything it's given.

Zayn watched it happen and didn't stop it. He didn't cry. Not then. That came later.

He just sat, and held on.

---

I was already on the dune above the hollow when he came over the rise that morning. I'd been there before the travellers came.

Don't ask me how long.

I sat at the top of the slope, a long way off, with my scorched fur and my one good eye, and I didn't go any closer. My tail lay in the sand behind me. It wasn't glowing. It wasn't dark. It just *lay* there, the way a tail does when no one's saying anything at all.

Zayn didn't look up. I'm not sure he knew I was there.

I could have gone down to him. I could have sat in his lap and let him hold me instead of a cold broken sword. I could have said a great many things. One thing, really. One thing, with a face in it.

I didn't say anything.

I'm very good at that. I've told you before. It's becoming a theme.

It's the one I'm least proud of. It's the one that still wakes me, when I sleep, which isn't often. In the dream I'm always on that dune, and the fire is always going out, and I am always, always a moment too far away.

That's all. That's all you get. Turn the page.

---

*End of Chapter Twelve*


---

# PART FOUR: THE UNNAMED


---

# Chapter Thirteen
## Nameless Again

> *The loneliest grave is the one no one visits. The second loneliest is the one that visits you.*
> — a saying of the desert burial-keepers

---

For four days, he didn't say a word.

You'd think I'd be relieved. I'd have traded every scorpion in the desert to hear him tell me to shut up.

He walked. That was all. In the cold, in the hammering noon Fire, at night under an Ash already beginning to thin, as if something had taken a bite out of it. He let the dunes choose the direction. If the Weaver was writing the sand that week, it was writing for a boy who had stopped reading.

Soren's sword was tied across his back, wrapped in his scarf. It knocked against his spine with every step, and every time it did he flinched, and every time he flinched he kept walking. It had a rhythm to it after a while. Like a knock on a door. Like someone behind him, tapping his shoulder, wanting to be let back in.

He hadn't given Soren to the dunes. He'd sat until the wind began the work, and then walked away and left the sand to finish, with the travellers' word chasing him the way stones had once chased him out of Harrow's End.

*Prince-Killer.*

He'd been called a lot of things. Nameless. Sandborn. Monster. Now he had a new one, and the worst part, the part that sat in his chest like a swallowed stone, was that he couldn't be sure it was wrong.

I followed a dune behind. I'd have walked closer, but I wasn't walking well.

---

You remember what his light did to my Hollow half in the storm.

There was a patch on my left side, the silver side, where the fur had come away. What was underneath wasn't fox. It was dark and faintly smoking, like a coal dropped in water that hadn't decided whether to go out. Sometimes, at night, when it was very quiet, I could hear it whispering. Not to me. To itself. The same three words, over and over, in a voice I knew.

It hadn't healed. I'd stopped expecting it to.

The night of the full Ash had made it worse. I'll say that much and no more.

So I limped. And he walked. And neither of us talked about it.

---

He still did the things that kept him alive. Fifteen years of scraping by don't leave you just because your heart has.

On the second day he found water: a wet finger to the wind, a hollow where camel-thorn grew, digging until the sand went dark.

He'd taught Soren how to do that. I watched him remember it. He sat back on his heels with wet sand on his fingers and his face went completely still, the way a well goes still after something has dropped to the bottom of it and you're waiting to hear it land.

You never do hear it land, in a deep enough well.

He got up and kept walking.

At night he hardly slept. He sat at the top of whatever dune he'd stopped on, and with one finger he wrote in the sand.

The same word, every night. Five letters, slow and careful, the way you write a thing you've only just learned, tongue between his teeth. Soren had made him practise it fifty times by the fire, laughing at how he wrote the S backwards. *It's my name, Zayn. Have some respect.* He didn't write it backwards anymore.

S. O. R. E. N.

And every morning the dunes had moved in the night, and the word was gone.

That's the trouble with being given to the desert. There's no stone. The people who loved you have to carry the grave around inside them. After a while, the grave starts to carry you.

---

On the third morning, the word was still there.

Not where he'd written it. He'd written it on the crest, where he sat. This one was at the bottom of the slope, fifty paces down, in a smooth bowl of sand where the wind hadn't reached. Five letters. The S the right way round. Pressed in deep with one finger, the way he pressed.

His handwriting. I'd know it anywhere. So would he.

He stood over it a long time.

"Did you write this?" he said. Not to me, really. To the air.

I hadn't. I have paws. And I'd been beside him all night, and I'd have sworn he never moved.

But there were his own footprints, going down to it from where we'd slept, and none coming back.

He didn't say anything. He rubbed it out with his foot, carefully, the whole word, until the sand was smooth. Then he looked at his finger for a while, turning it over, as if it belonged to somebody else.

That night he didn't write anything at all.

---

On the third night he tried to lie.

He was sitting with his knees up and the sword across his lap, and he said, so quietly I nearly missed it, "It wasn't my fault."

His face twisted. His hand flew to his chest. The feather mark flared under his shirt, hot and sharp, and he doubled over and pressed his palm against it until the light went out and there was a smell in the air of scorched cloth.

He couldn't even lie to himself. Not for comfort. Not once.

Most people get to tell themselves small kind lies in the dark. *There was nothing I could have done.* Lies like that are how people get up in the morning. Zayn never got any. The truth was all he had: he had walked away, and watched the fire go out, and called it nothing.

"The sand remembers," he whispered afterwards. The old words.

The mark didn't burn. It wasn't a lie.

But I don't think he meant it the way he used to. I think he'd started to hear it the other way round. Not *the sand will remember you*, like a blessing. *The sand remembers*, like a threat. The sand remembers what you did.

I limped up the slope and sat down a little way off.

"It wasn't your fault," I said.

My tail lit up, bright and steady as a lamp. I wanted him to see it more than I've wanted most things.

He didn't turn his head.

"Don't," he said.

It was the first word he'd spoken in three days. Hoarse, as if his voice had rusted shut.

"It's true," I said. "Look at my tail. Look at it."

"It glowed at the City of Glass too," he said. "Right up until it didn't."

I had nothing for that. One small, merciful lie in fifteen years, and it had cost me every true thing I'd say to him afterwards. Remember that, if you ever think a lie is cheap.

He was quiet a while. Then, without looking at me: "Where were you?"

The wind moved over the dune.

"That night," he said. "After I walked away. You weren't with him. You weren't with me. Where were you?"

I opened my mouth.

I'm not going to tell you what I nearly said, or where I was. Not yet. It's my story, so I get to choose. That's the only power a storyteller has: to make you wait in the dark.

I closed my mouth.

He nodded slowly, as though I'd answered. Then he lay down with his back to me and the broken sword in his arms, and I sat beside him until the Embers went pale, and the burned place in my side whispered its three words to itself, and neither of us said anything at all.

---

There was a twist of waxed paper at the bottom of his bag. Three dates rolled in honey, the last of the four, gone sticky and dark, carried all the way from Harrow's End.

On the fourth evening he took them out and held them in his palm for a long time.

Then he did something I didn't understand until later. He put one to his lips, just touched it there, and closed his eyes, and I watched his face go hunting for something. Searching, the way a tongue searches for a missing tooth.

He was trying to remember what honey tasted like. The grey blade had taken it in the storm, and it had come back, mostly. *Mostly*. He was checking.

I don't know what he found. He wrapped them up again without eating any. Eating them would have meant this was as bad as it got, and some stubborn part of him wasn't ready to promise that.

Or else he was afraid to taste them and find nothing there at all.

I'm not sure, even now, which. I'm not sure it was hope, either way.

---

The fourth night was the one I'd been afraid of.

He stopped at the bottom of a long slope instead of the top. In fifteen years he had always slept high, where he could see what was coming. Now he walked down into the cold trough between two dunes, where the wind poured sand down the slip-face in hissing curtains, like water, like something being poured over a body.

And he lay down.

On his back, sword on his chest, arms folded over it, looking up at the Embers. He let the sand come.

It came softly. A trickle over his feet. A drift against his side. The dunes were moving the way they moved every night, and he had lain down in the path of one, and he was very still, and he was watching the Embers with an expression I'd seen before on other faces, a long time ago. Relief. That's the word. It's the most frightening expression a living face can make.

"Zayn," I said.

He didn't answer.

"Zayn. Get up."

The sand was over his ankles now. His knees. It was spilling into the folds of his shirt, into the hollow of his throat.

The dead are given to the dunes. That's what desert people do. He hadn't stayed to see the sand close over Soren. So he was giving them the next best thing.

"*Get up.*" I got my teeth in his sleeve and pulled with everything I had, which that night was less than a kitten has. My burned side split and smoked and I yelped, and his arm didn't move.

I could have changed. You're thinking it. I was thinking it. I could have let the dark half out and dug him free in three strokes.

He'd seen that half once, and his light had nearly burned it out of the world. To wake with that thing's breath on his face might be what finished him.

So I stood there in the sliding sand, shaking, a small burned fox shouting at a boy who wouldn't listen, while the desert buried him an inch at a time.

And the light came.

---

It wasn't his light. That's what I knew first.

His light starts at the mark and pours outward. This came from the slope beside him, from nowhere at all. It was soft and gold, and it didn't burn. It glowed the way a lamp glows in a window when someone is waiting up for you.

And it was cold. That's what nobody puts in the songs. The sand around it frosted. I watched the frost spread out from it in fine white feathers, and my breath came out of me in a cloud.

I didn't see anyone in it. I want to be honest about that. I only saw the light on Zayn's face, and his eyes open wide, and his lips move.

He told me later what he saw. I'll tell it the way he did.

Soren was sitting on the slope beside him. Cross-legged, elbows on his knees, the way he sat by the fire when he was about to say something annoying. The scar along his jaw. His hair coming loose. Light came through the edges of him, like a cloth hung in front of the Fire.

He was grey. Zayn told me that, and then he wouldn't tell me any more about it for a long time. Grey, like the hollow that morning. And the golden cracks were still in him, running across his face and down his throat, and they were glowing again. Brighter than they'd been. As if the thing that had been trying to get out of them had nearly made it.

The sand didn't shift under him. Not a grain. He had no weight at all.

His hand was bare. No ring. Zayn noticed that. He couldn't stop noticing it.

"You look terrible," said Soren. "That's weird."

His mouth moved a moment before the words came. Just a moment. Like someone speaking to you from the other side of a wide valley.

Zayn made a sound. It wasn't a word.

"You're lying in a hole," said Soren. "Letting a dune sit on you. Do you know how stupid that looks from where I am? Very. It looks very stupid."

"I left you," Zayn whispered.

Soren stopped smiling.

"Yeah," he said. "You did."

There was no anger in it. That was the worst part, Zayn told me. He said it the way you say *the sand is hot*. A plain fact laid down between them, like a stone on a grave.

"I left you," Zayn said again, "and you died."

"I know. I was there." Soren leaned forward. He laid his hand on Zayn's shoulder, and it was *warm*. Zayn swore to me it was warm, warm as a living hand, warm as the Fire on a cold morning, though the frost was spreading out from him across the sand the whole time. "Get up anyway."

"Who did it?" Zayn said. "Soren. Who—"

Soren's face went still. He opened his mouth, and for a moment nothing came out of it but the smell of cold ash, and his eyes went somewhere past Zayn's shoulder, into the dark, and stayed there. As if someone were standing there. As if he'd seen them before.

Then he looked back.

"I can't," he said, and for the first time he sounded young, and frightened, and seventeen. "I don't think I'm allowed. I think there's a lot I'm not allowed." His grip tightened. "Get up, Zayn."

"I can't."

"Yes, you can. You're just choosing not to. It's different." His eyes were very bright, and very pale, and they didn't blink. Not once, the whole time. "Never give up, Zayn. You promised."

Zayn shut his eyes.

He had. Under the full Ash, on the last night, Soren had said *Promise me you'll never give up*, and he had said *I promise*. And he could not lie. Not even lying in the sand with a dune on his chest.

He had promised. So it was true. So he had to make it true.

When he opened his eyes, the slope was dark and empty, and there was only a fox standing knee-deep in the sand beside him, with a tail blazing so bright it hurt to look at.

I hadn't seen anything. I'll swear to that.

But my tail had been burning gold the whole time, and it doesn't do that for dreams.

And the frost was still there on the slope, in the shape of someone sitting cross-legged. It stayed there until the Fire came up and took it.

---

He got up.

He dug himself out with his hands, the way he'd once dug himself out of collapsed rooms in the Old Ruins, and climbed the slope on hands and knees, dragging the sword.

Then he looked at me. Actually looked, for the first time in four days.

"Did you see him?" he said.

"No," I said.

My tail stayed bright. He watched it.

"But you believe me."

"Yes," I said. It stayed bright.

He looked at it a long moment. "He was warm," he said. "He was warm, Kiri. And everything around him was freezing." He swallowed. "Is that how it's supposed to be?"

I've walked with eleven bearers. I've seen a great many things come back to the people who loved them. Some of them were what they looked like.

"I don't know," I said.

That was true too. I wish it hadn't been.

He looked away over the dunes, but he didn't turn his back on me. That night he slept with me curled against his side, one hand on the sword and one on my back, and twice I felt him wake and look at the slope where the frost had been.

It's not nothing. In the desert, you learn to count the things that are not nothing. You learn to count them very carefully, because there aren't many.

---

This next part is where I went wrong.

In the morning he walked on, and I couldn't keep up.

The dune had cost me more than I'd thought. By mid-morning I'd folded up in the shade of a rock with my legs not listening, and he was four dunes ahead. I told myself I'd catch up when the heat broke.

Which is why I was a long way off, lying on a ridge, when the lanterns came.

You don't see lanterns in daylight. That's what made me lift my head. A string of camels coming over the eastern dunes, six or seven, with lanterns on their saddles still burning in the full morning Fire, the way they burn on the walls of Veimore because someone is afraid of what happens if they ever go out. Red tassels. Water skins so fat they sloshed.

They came straight to him. Not near him. *To* him, as if they'd known exactly which dune a tired boy would be standing on.

Fox ears are very good. Fox legs, that morning, were not. I dragged myself to the crest of the ridge and lay flat and listened.

The lead camel knelt. A man climbed down.

He was old, his hair silver and combed long over a robe the deep blue of the sky just after the Fire sets. His face was soft and kind and lined, someone's grandfather. His voice was low and gentle, the kind that makes you lean in.

And his eyes were grey.

Faded grey, flat and pale, like cloth left in the Fire for a hundred years. Like a Husk's eyes, before the white sets in.

The fur along my spine stood straight up, and the burned place in my side stopped whispering. All at once. Just went silent, the way insects go silent when something walks past in the dark.

I couldn't have told you why. That's not true. I could have. I'm not going to. Let's leave it there.

Behind the old man, another figure climbed down: the biggest man I had ever seen. Shaved head. Arms like the trunks of date palms. A face that didn't look as though it had ever made an expression it meant.

"Take the boy's bag," the old man said softly. "Can't you see he can barely stand?"

The giant lifted the bag gently from Zayn's shoulder and carried it back to the baggage camel and tied it there, among the water skins.

Remember that. I didn't. I was watching faces. I should have been watching the bag.

---

"You are the boy who glows," the old man said.

Zayn didn't answer. His hand had gone to the hilt of the broken sword on his back.

"My name is Orrin," said the old man. "I am the vizier of Veimore. I serve King Absalon." A pause. "You'll have heard my name. From him."

Zayn's knuckles went white.

He had. Soren had told him the whole story by the fire: the stable boy he'd hidden, the broken sword, and the soft old voice of the vizier calling it treason, calling it dark magic, until his father believed it.

"Yes," said Orrin. "I named him traitor." He bowed his silver head. "I was wrong. I've spent a turn of the Ash crossing this desert to tell him so. And I came too late."

He reached into his robe and held something out on his open palm, and I saw Zayn's whole body go still.

A ring. Heavy gold, worn smooth, the crest of Veimore cut into its face.

Soren's ring. The one he never took off.

"He gave me this," Orrin said gently.

On the ridge, my tail flickered.

I *think* it dimmed. I'm nearly sure. But the Fire was bright across the sand, and a tail is a hard thing to read in daylight, and I was in pain, and afraid. All of that is true. None of it is an excuse. I've had a very long time to try to make it one.

"When?" said Zayn. His voice cracked on it.

"On the night of the full Ash," said Orrin. "I found him at last. By his little fire. We spoke. He forgave me, I think, or began to. And he gave me his ring and said, *If anything happens to me, find the boy who glows. Look after him.*" The old man's voice shook, very slightly, in exactly the right place. "I didn't understand what he meant. Not until morning."

There was no bell. Hesset's bell was in a patched bag tied to a camel twenty paces away, among the water skins, and it made no sound. I've wondered since whether a bell can be smothered the way a voice can.

Zayn reached out, slowly, the way you reach for something that might vanish. Orrin let him take the ring. It was warm from the old man's robe. Zayn closed his fingers over it, and his face crumpled.

He'd last felt that gold on a living hand.

"Did you kill him?" Orrin asked quietly. Almost kindly. "They're saying you did."

Zayn's eyes were streaming. He could have said *no*, and it would have been true.

But the boy could not lie, and in his heart, the truth was something else.

"I left him," he said.

Orrin nodded slowly. "I know," he said. "So did I, once, a long time ago. Someone I loved." His grey eyes were wet. "Everyone leaves someone, child. It's what we do afterwards that matters."

He held out his hand. After a moment, Zayn gave the ring back. Orrin tucked it inside his robe, carefully, close to his heart.

"It should go to his father," he said. "And his brother. I'll see that it does. But you need food, and shade, and sleep. There's an oasis town two days east. I have a house there. Come with me." A pause, gentle as a hand on the back of the neck. "And when you're stronger, I'll tell you about your father."

Zayn's head came up.

"I knew him," said Orrin. "Long ago. He carried what you carry. I can tell you who he was. If you want to know."

---

By the time I got there, Zayn was already climbing onto a camel.

I came down the slope in a sort of controlled fall, my side smoking. Orrin's soft grey eyes found me, and he smiled.

"Ah," he said. "The fox. Of course."

There was no surprise in it at all. He looked at the burned place on my side, the dark showing through, and he looked at it the way you'd look at a door you already had the key to.

I stood at the camel's feet with my fur on end and looked up at the boy: one hand on the reins, the other pressed flat over his mark.

I could have said a great many things. I've had a long time to make a list.

I said, "I don't like him."

Four words. My tail glowed. They were perfectly true.

Zayn looked down at me. He looked tired beyond anything a boy of fifteen should be. "You don't like anyone," he said.

That was true too.

"And you told me *yes* once," he said, more quietly. "Remember?"

He turned the camel's head east and followed the old man's lanterns over the dune.

I stood there in the sand a long moment. Then I followed. What else was I going to do? I'd been following him for fifteen years.

The giant walked at the back of the line. He didn't look round once. But at the top of the dune he stopped, and he waited, very patiently, until I'd passed him, so that he was behind me all the way.

I didn't know then what it would cost, those four small, cowardly, true words, and all the words I didn't say after them. I know now. I'll tell you. Not yet.

The burned place in my side didn't whisper again all that day. Not once.

It was listening.

And above us the Ash was thinning, night by night, like a face turning slowly away, and I had started counting again.

---

*End of Chapter Thirteen*


---

# Chapter Fourteen
## The Ringing of the Bell

> *The bell does not lie, but it can be too late. A bell rung too late is only a funeral.*
> — a saying of the healers at the desert's edge

---

The town was called Thornwell, and it was the kind of place you could love if nobody in it was trying to kill you.

It sat in a shallow bowl between three dunes, around a spring so old the stones of its well had been worn into the shape of hands. Nine date palms stood over it. Somebody long ago had decided nine was lucky, so the people of Thornwell never cut one down and never planted a tenth. There was a market of striped awnings, a dyer's yard hung with red and saffron cloth, and a hundred flat-roofed mud-brick houses. A free town. No king, no walls. Just water, and people who'd decided to stay near it.

They hung lanterns in their doorways now, day and night, the way Veimore does. Rumours had come in on the trade roads of whole villages in the south going quiet. Nobody in Thornwell said the word *Hollow*. They kept their lamps lit and hurried their children indoors when the Fire went down.

Orrin's house stood on the edge of the market. Blue shutters, a courtyard with a fig tree, and a fountain that actually worked, which in the desert is about as close to showing off as you can get.

"Animals sleep in the yard," said the steward at the gate, looking down at me.

"I'm not an animal," I said. "I'm a guest with fur."

The steward went pale, the way people do the first time, and looked at Orrin.

Orrin smiled his soft smile. "The fox may go where he likes," he said. "Outside."

So I slept on the roof.

---

They gave Zayn the top room. A real bed with a real blanket, a basin of clean water, a window that looked out over the nine palms. He stood in the doorway looking at it the way you'd look at a meal you suspected was a trap.

Then he took off the broken sword and laid it on the bed, and his bag beside it. And for the first time since the full Ash, he slept through a whole night without the sword in his arms.

I watched him through the window from the roof's edge, my burned side pressed against the warm bricks.

I didn't sleep.

The servants in that house didn't speak. I noticed that on the first night. They moved through the rooms with lamps and basins and folded cloths, and they never said a word, not to Orrin, not to each other. When one passed below me in the courtyard I saw her lips moving, very slightly, the same small shape over and over, like someone saying a name to keep from losing it.

I told myself it was a prayer. I've told myself a lot of things.

---

You have to understand how good Orrin was at it.

Most liars are bad at it. They talk too much. They hurry. Orrin never hurried. He wanted you to feel he had all the time in the world, the way you feel the weight of a warm blanket.

On the first evening there were dates on the table. Dates rolled in honey, on a blue clay plate.

Zayn stopped in the doorway and stared.

"Soren told me you liked them," said Orrin gently. "Sit. Eat."

The bell was upstairs, in the bag on the bed. I was on the roof with my ear to a crack in the shutter, and my tail went dim as a dying coal. Nobody saw.

Zayn sat. He ate one date, slowly, eyes shut, and I watched him go searching for the sweetness the grey blade had taken in the storm, and find it, and his face broke open with relief. He didn't touch the twist of waxed paper in his pocket. He ate Orrin's dates instead, and his eyes filled, and Orrin pretended not to notice, which was the kindest thing anyone had done for him in days.

That's how it's done. Not with chains. With honey, and a real bed, and an old man who knows exactly when to look away.

---

Orrin wrote letters in the evenings.

He sat at a low table by the lamp with paper and ink and a little stone seal he wore on a chain around his neck. When each letter was done he folded it, dripped grey wax onto the fold, and pressed the seal in with his thumb. A falcon waited on a perch in the corner. Every night it flew out of the window with a letter tied to its leg, and every night, just before it went, it turned its head and looked at Zayn for a long moment with one black eye.

On the second evening Zayn sat across from him and watched the pen move.

"*Water,*" he said quietly. "That word. Upside down. It says *water*."

The pen stopped.

Orrin looked up. For a moment, only a moment, something moved behind those faded grey eyes, quick and cold as a fish turning in a dark well.

"You can read," he said.

"A little. Soren taught me."

"Did he." Orrin set down the pen. "He was a patient teacher?"

Zayn almost smiled, the first I'd seen since the full Ash. "No. He was terrible. He laughed every time I wrote an R. He said they looked like camels falling over."

"Then you must be a very good student," said Orrin, and went back to his letter, and folded it, and sealed it.

I remember the seal. The grey wax, and the soft way he pressed it, and the little curling mark it left. I didn't know why I was remembering it. Some part of me must have known. The part of me that always knows things a moment too late.

---

On the third evening he talked about his son.

The lamp had burned low, and the way conversations wander after dark, they'd come round to families.

"I had a son once," said Orrin.

He said it to the lamp, not to Zayn.

"And a brother. A long time ago, before I came to the palace. There was a sickness that year in the town where I was born. It came through like a wind. My brother first. Then my boy." He turned his cup slowly in his long fingers. "He was six. He used to sit on my knee while I wrote, and try to catch the pen."

My tail glowed gold on the roof. Every word was true.

That's the thing about truly dangerous liars. They hardly ever lie. They tell you the truth, and the truth is sad and real, and it opens you like a key in a lock. Then, once the door is open, they walk in and close it softly behind them.

"I'm sorry," said Zayn.

"So am I." Orrin smiled, tired and gentle. "I spent a long time trying to forget them. It doesn't work, you know. Not the way you'd hope." He looked at the lamp a moment longer. "Not unless you're willing to forget everything else as well."

Zayn was quiet. Then he said, "Tell me about my father."

Orrin set down the cup.

"He carried what you carry. The Light. Before you. The murals have a name for him. You've seen them, I think. In the glass city."

Zayn's hand went to his chest.

"*The Runner*," he whispered.

"The Runner. He was afraid of what the Light would ask of him. So he ran." A pause. "He's alive, Zayn."

Zayn's breath went out of him as if he'd been struck.

"Where?" His voice cracked. "Where is he?"

"When you're stronger," said Orrin, and reached across the table and laid a hand over Zayn's, very lightly. It was a dry hand, cool, and it stayed a moment too long. "I promise. When you're stronger, I'll take you to him."

It's a strange thing to sit on a roof and watch a boy's heart get caught on a hook. He leaned toward that old man the way a plant leans toward a window, not knowing the window looks out onto a wall.

He trusted him.

For the first time since Soren, Zayn trusted someone.

---

That night he climbed up to the roof.

He sat down beside me with his knees pulled up, looking out over the nine palms and the dark dunes beyond. The Ash was a half now, and shrinking.

"He's alive," Zayn said at last. "My father."

"I heard."

"He said he'd take me to him."

"I heard that too."

He turned and looked at me. "You don't believe him."

My tail stayed exactly as it was. I didn't say anything.

"Kiri."

"Wear the bell," I said.

He frowned. "What?"

"Hesset's bell. Take it out of your bag and wear it." I looked at the palms. "Humour an old fox."

"Why?"

I didn't answer.

"*Why?*" Sharper. "If you know something, *tell* me. If he's lying, tell me what about."

There it was. The door, wide open.

*Don't trust him.* Three words. And he'd have asked *why not*, and I'd have had to tell him where I'd been on the night of the full Ash, and what I'd been. I wasn't ready. Not with him. Not even with you, yet. And I had no proof but my own word, and my own word had gone dark in the City of Glass.

I'm not going to make excuses. I'll only tell you what I did.

I said nothing.

Zayn looked at me a long time. Then he made a small, tired sound that wasn't quite a laugh.

"Where were you?" he said. "That night."

I said nothing.

"That's what I thought," he said, and went back down the stairs.

Behind him, on the far roof across the courtyard, one of the silent servants was standing in the dark. I don't know how long she'd been there. She was looking at me. Her lips were still moving, still shaping that same small word. When she saw I'd seen her, she turned and went down, and I heard no footsteps on the stairs.

---

In the morning, he wore the bell.

Out of spite, I think, the way you'd taste a dish someone swore was poisoned just to show them. Or maybe for Hesset. You can't always tell why people do things. Sometimes they can't either.

He tied it to his belt on a short thread, under the fold of his scarf. A small bronze bell no bigger than a thumb.

It didn't ring all morning. Nobody lied to him all morning. Orrin was in his room, writing.

I went down into the market to find something to eat.

---

That's where I saw Bram.

I almost didn't know him. The boy I'd last seen in Harrow's End had been broad and loud and well-fed, nearly a prince. This one was thin as a stick. Rags. Feet bound in strips of cloth gone black. Lips split from thirst. He stood at the old well with his hands cupped, begging for water, and nobody gave him any. They looked at his face and stepped round him.

There was a grey streak down one side of it. Not the old one, from the Husk in the lane. A new one, running from his temple into his hair, and the hair there had gone grey too, all the way to the roots, as if someone had laid a cold thumb on him and pressed. When he blinked, the eye on that side was a little slow to follow.

I went very still in the shadow of an awning.

He didn't see me. He was watching the house with the blue shutters the way a starving dog watches a butcher's door. When the market got busy around noon he slipped off down a narrow alley between the dyer's yard and the wall.

I followed him.

The alley turned, and turned again, and ended in a little dead square full of broken pots, where the dyer's red and saffron cloths hung over the wall like curtains in a room where someone has died.

Someone was waiting there.

Grey robe. Smooth white mask.

I flattened myself behind a cracked water jar and stopped breathing.

---

"Well?" said the mask. Its voice was flat and dry, like sand poured from a cup. It didn't seem to come from behind the mask. It seemed to come from all around the little square at once.

"He's here," said Bram. His voice shook. "You were right. The house with the blue shutters. The top room, over the palms. He sleeps by the window. He's got a broken sword. He keeps it on the bed."

"You're certain it's him."

"I'd know him anywhere." Bram swallowed. "I've known him all my life."

"Good. Tonight, then."

"And Tobin?" Bram took a step forward. "You *promised*. When you found us in the desert, you said you'd keep him safe from the grey. You said if I found the glowing boy, you'd give my brother back."

"When we have the bearer," said the mask, "the child goes free."

My tail went dark.

Completely dark, like a lamp with the oil run out. I wanted to scream at him, *Look at me!* But he wasn't looking at me. He was looking at that blank white oval as if it were the only door left in the world.

"Everyone's gone grey," he said. His voice cracked in half. "Do you understand? All of them. My father sits by the well all day with the bucket in his hands, and he says—" He stopped. He made himself go on. "He says, *Draw it up slow, boys, there's plenty.* That's all. Over and over. That's what he was saying to us the morning it came. He doesn't know my name. He says it to me like I'm a stranger. Oszkar walks round and round the square. Garrem stands in his doorway counting tokens that aren't there. *Everyone.*" He pressed his hands over his face. "I took Tobin and I ran, and we walked for days, and then you came, and—" He stopped again. "He's seven. He's only seven."

"Tonight," said the mask.

It didn't turn to go. It just stood there with its blank face pointed at Bram, perfectly still, until Bram backed out of the square himself. And when I looked back, after Bram had gone, the mask was still standing there, facing the empty place where he had been.

Then, very slowly, it turned its face toward the water jar I was hiding behind.

It didn't come over. It didn't need to. It only tilted its head, the way a Husk does, as if it had heard a sound it almost remembered.

I have never in my life left a place so quickly.

---

Why would the Unnamed need a starving boy to tell them where Zayn slept, when Orrin had chosen the room himself? I understood later. A man like Orrin never lets his own hands be seen. If the bearer lived long enough to wonder how the masks had found him, it would be Bram's face he remembered. The boy who'd thrown the first stone. Not the kind old man with the dates.

It was a clever plan. It would have worked.

Except for a small bronze bell, no bigger than a thumb.

---

I ran.

Faster than I'd moved since the storm, my burned side tearing and smoking, across the market, under a camel, through a basket of figs.

The gate of the blue house was shut.

I found a place where the bricks were rough, and climbed. I'm not built for climbing. I'm built for running and digging and being rude. It took far too long. By the time I dropped into the courtyard, the Fire was going down red behind the palms, and the whole yard was the colour of the inside of an eyelid.

The giant was standing by the fountain.

He turned his head and looked at me. That was all. He stood between me and the door, and when I tried to go round him, he was there. When I tried the other way, he was there too. He never seemed to move. He was simply always where I was going.

I could have changed. I've told you that before. I'll tell you again before this story's done.

I was still standing there, shaking, deciding, when I heard the sound upstairs.

---

They were sitting at supper. That was the strange thing, Zayn told me later. Such an ordinary evening.

Bread and lentils and a little bowl of dates. Orrin had been telling him why nobody in Thornwell planted a tenth palm, and for one evening Zayn had almost forgotten to be sad.

Then footsteps on the stairs. Many of them, soft and quick and perfectly in step.

And under them, faint, a hum. No tune. No words.

The door opened.

Grey robes. White masks. Five, then eight, filling the doorway and spilling into the room, and the lamps all leaned away from them, every flame bending the same way, as if a cold wind had come in with them. In their hands were blades, long and thin, the colour of old ash, and they drank the lamplight instead of reflecting it.

Zayn was on his feet. The table went over. His hand went to his belt for the knife, and the knife wasn't there. It was upstairs in his bag, with the broken sword, on the bed by the window, exactly where Bram had said it would be.

And Orrin rose.

He rose smoothly from his cushion, the old man in blue, and stepped between Zayn and the masks. He put one arm out behind him, shielding the boy, the way a father shields a child.

"Stay behind me, child," he said. His voice was low and calm and full of love. "You're safe. No one will harm you while I live."

And the bell rang.

---

It wasn't loud. That's what I always remember. I heard it from the courtyard, through the shutters: one clear note, high and sweet as a drop of water falling into a well. A tiny sound.

It filled the whole room.

The masks stopped humming. All of them, at once.

Zayn went completely still.

He looked down at his belt. At the bell, swinging on its thread, still shivering with the note. Then he looked up at Orrin's back. The silver hair. The outstretched arm.

Orrin turned his head. The soft face didn't change at all. But the eyes did. They moved down to the bell and rested there, and something in them went flat and cold and very, very tired, like a man who has been wearing a heavy cloak for a long time and has finally been allowed to put it down.

"Ah," he said quietly. "Hesset."

Zayn's lips were white. "The ring," he whispered. "Did he give you the ring?"

"Of course he did," said Orrin.

The bell rang again. Louder. And it didn't stop shivering after, the way a bell does. It kept trembling on its thread, humming to itself, as if it had more to say and couldn't find the note.

Then it all came at once. Zayn told me he could feel the pieces falling into place one after another, each a small stone striking his back. The bag on the camel. The dates on the plate. *Soren told me you liked them.* The ring that had not been on Soren's hand. The ring that had been warm from Orrin's robe.

*He gave me this.*

"No," said Zayn. It came out of him like something tearing. "*No.*"

Orrin sighed. He lowered his arm.

"I should have searched you myself," he said, and stepped aside.

---

The masks came in.

Zayn fought. I want you to know that. No knife, no sword, no friend, and he fought anyway: fists, the overturned table, a lamp swung in a burning arc that sent two masks reeling back with their robes alight. They didn't scream as they burned. They just patted at the flames, calmly, and came on.

And the light came.

But it came *grey*.

Thin and dim and sputtering, the colour of fear and fury. It crawled out of his fists like smoke and did nothing at all. A mask stepped in close and laid a grey blade flat against his forearm, almost tenderly.

Zayn screamed.

It didn't cut. There was no blood. But where the blade touched him, the grey light went *into* it, drawn out like water into dry sand, and something left him. A memory. He'd tell me later he never knew which. Only that it had been warm, and now there was a cold place with smooth edges where it used to be, and that he sometimes caught himself reaching for it, in the dark, the way you reach for a step that isn't there.

That was when I came through the shutters.

I don't remember how I got past the giant. Then I was in the room in a spray of broken wood, going for the nearest mask with every tooth I had.

I got one ankle. That's all. It was cold. Cold as a cellar, and soft, like something kept too long out of the light. Then a hand like the side of a house closed round my middle, right over the burn, and the giant lifted me, and I made a sound I'm not proud of, and he threw me.

I hit the wall. The world went white, then grey, then very far away.

---

The last thing Zayn saw, before the sack came down over his head, was the doorway.

Someone was standing in it. Behind the masks, in the shadow at the top of the stairs. A thin boy in rags, feet wrapped in cloth, a grey streak down his face.

Bram.

They looked at each other across the wrecked room. The boy who'd thrown the first stone, and the boy it had hit.

"They've got Tobin," Bram said. His voice was barely there. "I'm sorry. Zayn, I'm sorry."

He had never once, in fifteen years, called him by his name. I don't think he'd even known it, until the masks told him.

The bell on Zayn's belt hung silent. Every word was true.

Then the sack came down, and it smelled of burned cloth and something sweeter underneath, and the grey blades came with it, and the room went dark.

---

I woke alone on the floor of a room that smelled of lamp oil and spilled lentils.

The masks were gone. The old man was gone. The broken sword was gone from the bed upstairs, and the bag with it. Out in the courtyard the fountain was still running, as if nothing had happened. The silent servants were moving through the rooms with their basins and cloths, cleaning up, setting the table back on its legs, and none of them looked at me. Not one.

On the floor near the table lay a scrap of snapped thread, and beside it, on its side, a small bronze bell.

I picked it up in my teeth. It was still faintly warm. And very faintly, so faintly I might have imagined it, it was still trembling.

I didn't know where they'd taken him. I didn't know whether there'd be anything left of him when I found him. I only knew that the bell had rung, and rung too late, and that I had been standing in a courtyard when it did, with the truth in my mouth, saying nothing.

Bram told me, many years later, that he never forgave himself for that night.

Neither did I.

---

*End of Chapter Fourteen*


---

# Chapter Fifteen
## The Buried Ship

> *A ship in the desert is a coffin waiting for the sea. What does it hold while it waits?*
> — a riddle the caravan children tell, which has no answer, and which they are told not to tell after dark

---

I'm going to tell you this part the way he told it to me.

He told it once, in the mountain caves, by a fire that kept going out, in a flat, quiet voice I never heard him use again. He looked at his hands the whole time. I spent most of it under a dead ship with sand in my mouth.

---

It took me a night and a day to find it.

I followed the camels, and under the camel-smell, the cold, empty smell of grey blades. And another, the closer I got: burning cloth, and hair, and something sweet and scorched beneath them, like honey left on a stove.

I carried Hesset's bell in my teeth the whole way.

At dusk on the second day, under an Ash thinned almost to nothing, a white rind, like the edge of an eye rolled back, I came over a long ridge and saw it.

A ship.

There's no sea in the Destiny Desert. There never has been. And yet there it lay between two great dunes: a vast wooden hull, longer than Harrow's End, sunk on its side like a drowned animal, its carved prow rearing out of the sand with its mouth open, its ribs pale through the broken planks.

And inside it, through every crack and gap, lanterns burned.

Grey lanterns, the colour of ash. Their light didn't spill onto the sand. It stayed close to the hull, like breath on cold glass.

I crept down the slope in the dark, and found a place where the sand had drifted up against the hull, and dug.

---

He woke up chained.

A great beam ran down the middle of the hold, and from an iron ring in it ran a grey chain to a cuff on each of his wrists. The chain was cold in a way iron isn't. When the light in his chest tried to rise, the chain drank it, with a faint sound like a child sucking at a cut finger.

The planks were covered in writing.

Names. Hundreds of them, scratched into the wood with nails and buckles and fingernails, as high as a man could reach. Every one had been scraped over afterwards until only a pale smear was left, like a word rubbed out of the sand. In places, scraped names lay over scraped names, years deep. He couldn't read a single one.

In the far corner, tossed in a heap like rubbish, were his bag and Soren's broken sword.

He stared at that sword a long time.

---

They brought him up on the first night, for the burning.

The upper deck lay tilted under the sky, one edge running down into the sand like a ramp. The Unnamed stood in a circle on it. Fifty of them, perhaps more, grey robes, smooth white masks, silent as Husks. In the middle stood an iron bowl, and in the bowl a fire burned.

The flames were grey, and gave off no heat. Only cold, the way cold comes off a cellar door.

To one side, in a line, stood the children.

There were eleven. The youngest perhaps five, the oldest perhaps twelve. Little grey robes. Shaved heads. They stood very straight and still, the way children stand when they've learned that moving gets you hurt.

They weren't crying. Not one. Not a sniff. Frightened children cry. These had been frightened so long that crying had been worn out of them, the way well-stones get worn into the shape of hands.

Behind them, on a row of pegs along the rail, hung eleven small white masks. Waiting.

One girl, six or so, kept glancing at her peg. Not in fear. The way a child glances at a door when she's been promised someone will come through it soon.

The Unnamed brought Zayn into the circle and forced him to his knees.

Then three young men stepped forward. They wore grey, but no masks. Each held a strip of white cloth, and on each, in charcoal, a word was written.

A name.

The circle began to chant: the wordless hum from the storm, fifty throats rising and falling like one breath. Then it flattened into words, fifty voices in one:

"*Names are chains. Memory is pain. Forget, and be free.*"

The first young man held his strip of cloth over the bowl. He said the name on it, once, out loud. His own name. He said it the way you'd say goodbye to someone at a gate.

He let it fall.

The cloth caught and curled, and as it burned he tried to say his name again. The first sound came. The second, slower. Then his lips stopped halfway through the shape of it and stayed open, puzzled, like a man who has walked into a room and forgotten why. The smell came off the bowl: scorched cloth, and that sweet burnt something underneath.

Then his face went slack. Not peaceful. *Empty*. The way a house looks the day after the family moves out.

A brother stepped up behind each of them and pressed a white mask to his face, and Zayn saw its edges *settle*, softening against the skin like wax near a flame. One of the young men raised a hand and felt for the edge, and couldn't find one. He lowered his hand.

Then the children were given little brooms to sweep the ash into a sack. The smallest hummed the chant while she swept, perfectly, every rise and fall.

Three men had walked into the circle. Three nobodies walked out.

Zayn told me it was the worst thing he ever saw. Worse than the Husks, because the Husks hadn't chosen it. And worse than the choosing, he said, was how *relieved* they looked.

---

Then a figure stepped into the firelight, and the circle bowed.

Grey robe, white mask, like all the others. But when it spoke, Zayn knew the voice before the first word was done.

"Hello again, child."

*The Voice*, they called him there. A voice was all he'd kept after he burned his name.

He crouched in front of Zayn and held out a strip of white cloth and a stick of charcoal.

"Write it," he said.

Zayn looked at the cloth.

"You can now, can't you?" said the Voice softly. "He taught you. Write it, and give it to the fire, and all of this stops. The grief. The guilt. That walk under the full Ash, every time you close your eyes. You saw their faces. Tell me they weren't at peace."

The charcoal hung in front of Zayn's face.

He thought of Soren drawing letters in the sand with a stick. *Z. Like a lightning bolt. Then A, like a tent. Then Y, like a man waving for help.* Laughing. Always laughing.

Zayn spat on the cloth.

The circle was very quiet.

The Voice looked down at the wet spot a moment. Then he folded the cloth carefully and tucked it into his robe, close to his heart, where he kept the ring.

"I must go back to Veimore," he said, rising. "The Night of Embers is close." He turned to the circle. "Drain him slowly. Make him remember. When he is empty enough to forget his own name, he will give it to us himself. They always do."

He rode off into the dark toward the red city. I'd like to tell you that was the last we saw of him. I can't.

---

They put him back on his chain in the hold, and the draining began.

I won't tell you all of it. But somebody should know what he survived.

They came in pairs, every few hours: one with a lantern, one with a grey blade.

A grey blade doesn't cut. It lies flat against you, cold as the bottom of a well, and pulls, and the Light comes out of you in thin gold threads and runs into the blade and goes grey.

And with each thread, a memory came.

Never a good one. The good ones they took. The bad ones they *played*. Zayn was six again, running with a stone in his back. Or kneeling with Tobin's hand in his while Bram screamed *Monster!*

Or he was on the dune under the full Ash.

Over and over: turning away, walking, watching the orange point of the fire go out. And each time, the memory changed a little. The fire went out sooner. Soren called after him, begging. By the second day he couldn't tell which version was true. In the version he saw by then, he'd heard Soren scream.

He hadn't. I know that much. One day I'll tell you how.

I couldn't tell him. I was under the hull, hearing every sound he made and making none, waiting for the one moment that would work. That's what I told myself. True things can still be cowardly.

---

Each time they finished with him, they marched the children past to watch. On the first day one boy flinched when Zayn cried out. On the second, nobody did. They looked at him with soft, patient faces, the way you'd watch rain.

On the second night, they let the Husks in.

Dozens of Husks were penned in the lower hold. The Unnamed didn't fight them. They *kept* them, like dogs. And Husks are drawn to light, the way moths are, with the same ending.

They shuffled out of the dark and gathered round the boy on the chain, close enough that he could smell them: dust and old breath and nothing at all, the smell of a house nobody lives in.

They were all muttering. That's what Husks do. Each one saying its own last words to itself, over and over, a soft dry babble like water in a ditch. *Mind the step. Mind the step.* *Is that you?* *Bring the goats in, it's going to blow.* *Tell her I said—tell her I said—tell her—*

Then, all at once, they stopped.

Every head turned to the same angle at once, the way the masks had in the storm. The silence was so complete he could hear the grey lanterns hiss.

Then they spoke.

Every mouth, together. One voice out of thirty throats, not a syllable apart. Not their voices. Not any voice they'd ever had.

"*Little light.*"

Zayn went rigid on his chain.

He knew that voice. He'd heard it in a dream, in the Valley of Echoes, leaning over him in the dark.

"*Little nameless light,*" said the Husks. Thirty jaws moving as one. "*They threw stones at you. They called you monster. They drove you into the desert to die. And you'd burn for them? Why? No one remembers the bearers. Ask the fox. Ask him how many he's buried. Ask him if anyone sings their names.*"

A pause. Thirty heads tilting, exactly together.

"*Ask him why it's always children.*"

Zayn shut his eyes.

"*Ask him who it is that's fed,*" the voice went on, gentle, almost sorry, "*every time one of you burns. Ask him what's on the other side of the Light. He's seen it. He's been inside it. He knows.*"

"*Let it go,*" said thirty voices. "*Let me hold it for you. It's so heavy, little light. I know. I carried it too.*"

Then the voice went softer. Almost shy. All thirty faces turned down, toward the floor of the hold, toward the sand where I lay.

"*Hello, Kiri,*" it said. "*I know you're down there. You always did listen at doors.*"

Under the hull, in the dark, I stopped breathing.

The burned place in my side said its three words louder. In his voice. It had always been his voice.

I won't tell you what that voice sounded like centuries ago, when it was a shepherd boy calling me in from the hills at dusk. That's mine. I'll only tell you that I didn't answer, and that it was the hardest thing I have ever not done.

---

The children came at dawn, with water.

One of them stopped in front of Zayn and stared.

He was seven. His head was shaved, his robe too big. And his eyes were brown, clear brown, not faded at all. Not yet.

"You're the monster," said Tobin.

He said it without fear. That was the horror of it. He said it the way you'd name a colour.

He'd last seen that face grey and empty in Harrow's End.

"Bram said you weren't," Tobin whispered. "After. He said he was wrong." Something moved in his face, deep down, like a fish rising in murky water. His lip shook. Just once. "Is Bram coming?"

Zayn opened his mouth. And closed it.

He wanted to say *yes. I promise. I'll take you home.* It's what you say to a frightened child in the dark.

But Zayn couldn't lie, and he didn't know.

"I'll try," he said. It was all he had. "I'll try. I swear I will."

Tobin nodded, as if *I'll try* from someone who couldn't lie was worth more than *I promise* from anyone else.

Then his lip went still again, and the fish sank, and his face smoothed over. He hummed two notes of the chant under his breath, without seeming to know he'd done it, and followed the others up the ladder.

---

That night, alone on the chain, Zayn reached for his name and couldn't find it.

The blades had been at him all day. He was so cold he couldn't feel his hands. And he thought, the way you think of something just to make sure it's still there, *My name is*—

And there was nothing.

A blank, smooth place, like the face of a mask. He could feel the edges of where his name should be, the way a tongue feels a lost tooth. He reached for the village, the patched tent, and they were greying at the edges like cloth left in the Fire.

Worse: it didn't hurt. It felt like setting something down. He understood, then, the young men's faces at the bowl.

*Write it,* the Voice had said. *They always do.*

His finger moved in the dust on the floor of the hold. Searching. He didn't know what for.

"That's not even a letter," said someone.

The hold was full of gold.

---

Soren was sitting on the beam above him, swinging his legs.

He looked the way he had on the dune, but dimmer, and the edges of him wouldn't hold still. They drifted like ash lifting off a fire just gone out, and where they drifted Zayn's breath smoked.

"I spend a whole turn of the Ash teaching you," he said, "and you give up on the first word. I'm insulted. I'm deeply insulted."

Zayn stared up at him.

"Z," said Soren. He leaned down and pointed at the dust. His finger left no mark. "Like a lightning bolt. Go on."

Zayn's finger moved. A line. A slant. A line.

"A. Like a tent." Soren was smiling now. "Y. Like a man waving for help, which is you, right now, honestly. And N. Two posts and a fence. Go on. Say it."

"*Zayn,*" whispered Zayn.

It came back all at once, like water into a dry well. A boy of seven at the edge of a village well, deciding he'd be called a word that sounded like wind over a dune.

"There you are," said Soren softly.

His hand came down on Zayn's shoulder. Warm. Warm as the Fire, though the frost was creeping out across the boards from where he sat, over the scraped-out names.

"You promised," he said. "Never give up." Then, quieter, with a grin that was almost the old grin: "Besides. There are eleven kids up there who are forgetting their names too. Somebody's going to have to teach them. I'd do it, but I'm a bit busy being dead."

Zayn grabbed at his sleeve. His fingers went through it into cold. "What's it *like*? Soren. Where you are. Is it—"

Soren's smile stayed exactly where it was. But his eyes went past Zayn again, to the dark at the end of the hold, the way they had on the dune. As if someone were standing there. As if someone were always standing there, wherever he went now.

"Crowded," he said.

Then the gold went out, and Zayn was alone in the dark, with four letters drawn in the dust beside his hand.

He didn't wipe them away.

In the morning, when the lantern came down the ladder, there were nine letters in the dust, not four. Under his own name, in his own hand, pressed in deep with one finger, the S the right way round:

SOREN.

He didn't remember writing it. He never did.

---

The guard who came at midnight on the third night was alone, and he was humming.

Zayn knew the tune before he knew the man: fire and rising wings, the song from the Singing Wells.

The masked figure came down the ladder with a lantern and two grey blades crossed on his back, sat a little way off, and went on humming as if he didn't know he was doing it. As if it were the last thing left in him, the way Husks have their last words.

Brother Seven. Always hunting him. Always, somehow, missing.

Zayn started to hum too.

It just came out of him: the next line, the one the masked man hadn't reached yet.

The humming stopped.

Brother Seven sat completely still. The white mask stared across the lantern-light.

"Where did you learn it?" Zayn whispered. "Who taught it to you?"

No answer. I never once, in all that time, heard him speak.

He stood and drew one of the grey blades. *Drain him slowly.* He knelt by the chain and laid the blade flat against Zayn's arm.

And Zayn's hand closed around his wrist.

He wasn't thinking about himself. That's the only reason it worked. He was thinking of eleven children who had stopped crying, and a boy asking whether his brother was coming.

The light came up out of his chest like the Fire coming over the dunes.

*Gold.*

It ran down his arm and into the grey blade and *through* it, faster than the blade could drink. Up the masked man's wrist. Up his arm. Into him.

And they both saw it.

---

A tent, at night. Lamplight. A storm howling outside, the walls of the tent bowing in and out like something breathing.

A small boy, five years old, sitting on a rug, very serious. In his arms, held carefully, the way he'd been shown, a newborn wrapped in grey cloth woven all over with strange symbols. The baby had one tiny fist round the boy's finger and wouldn't let go.

Somewhere above them, a man's voice was singing. Low and warm. Fire and rising wings.

The boy looked down at the baby and smiled. A gap-toothed smile, so proud he could burst.

Then the tent wall tore open, and there were white masks in the storm.

---

The vision broke.

So did the mask.

I heard it from under the hull: a clean crack, like ice splitting. A line ran down the white face from brow to chin, with gold shining through. Along the crack, Zayn saw, the mask had grown *into* him, fine pale threads running into the skin, and where they'd split they bled, very slightly, grey.

Through the gap, for one heartbeat, an eye. Wide and wet and pale.

Too pale for a desert child.

And Zayn saw what he always saw: the full Ash, the Crown Dune, the fire. Clearer. He could feel the heat on his shins now, his skin tightening. And there were people in it. A man on his knees in the sand, begging. A figure in a cracked white mask, standing very still.

It took a long time for his legs to stop burning.

Brother Seven tore free and scrambled backwards, one hand clamped over the crack, making a small broken wordless sound, like a child woken from a nightmare who can't find the door.

He stared at Zayn.

Then his blade flashed once. I can't tell you whether he meant to do it. I don't think he knew himself. But the grey chain parted between Zayn's wrists like a cut thread.

And Brother Seven fled. Up the ladder, through the hatch, out into the night. Humming again, I swear it, as he went. Broken, out of tune. But humming.

---

The cuffs were still on his wrists. It didn't matter. He was across the hold before the hatch stopped swinging, the broken sword in his hand and his bag over his shoulder.

He went up the ladder to the children.

They were in a narrow room near the prow, awake. Eleven small faces turned toward the boy in the doorway.

None of them moved. None of them made a sound. Two were wearing their masks already. He hadn't seen them put on.

"Up," he said. "All of you. Now. Hold hands. Don't let go."

They stared at him. They had been taught not to move.

He went to the nearest masked child and worked his fingers under the rim, gently, the way you'd work out a splinter, saying anything, *you've got a name, you've got a name*, and it came away with a soft sound like a lip unsticking. Under it was a girl's face: blank, then puzzled, then all at once crumpling.

She cried.

Zayn said, in the caves, that it was the most beautiful sound he ever heard.

Tobin moved first after that. He got up and took the smallest girl's hand. Then the next child. Then the next. The second mask came off easier.

That's when the shouting started.

Running feet. Grey lanterns swinging. Masks poured toward the prow from both ends, humming louder and louder. Zayn backed the children into the corner with half a sword and no way out, and the light in his chest flickered grey.

And the ship moved.

---

That was me.

Three nights under the hull with Malek's voice in my ears. When I felt the light go gold above me, I knew.

I let it out.

It came up through the burn like smoke through a crack, and all the voices came with it, his the loudest, glad, *there you are, there you are*. A shadow-beast with ember eyes and one silver eye burning cold, my back to the belly of a dead ship, my mouth full of teeth I didn't remember growing.

And I *lifted*.

The ship groaned like the whole desert sighing. Timbers that hadn't moved in a thousand years tore loose. Masks went tumbling down the deck. Grey lanterns smashed, and their light ran out over the boards like spilled milk and died. The hull split along its belly with a crack like thunder, and the night came pouring in.

I held it. Sand and wood and fifty masked men and all those scraped-out names, on my back.

Through the gap in the broken hull, Zayn looked down at me.

He'd seen this shape once before, and burned it.

He wasn't afraid now. I saw his face. I'll never forget his face.

"*Kiri,*" he said.

That was all. My name. Not *monster*. My name.

And all the voices inside me went quiet. Every one. Even his.

"*Hold it,*" said Zayn.

"*What do you think I'm doing?*" I said. My voice came out like a rockslide.

He laughed. In the middle of all that, he laughed. Then he was through the gap with eleven children holding hands behind him, sliding down the sand under the dark arch of me, one after another, Tobin last, the smallest girl on Zayn's back, crying, still crying, all the way down.

When the last bare foot touched the sand outside, I let go.

The ship fell back into its dune with a roar of splitting timber, and the sand closed over it like water over a stone.

The humming went on a little while, under the sand. Then it stopped.

---

I don't remember shrinking. I remember lying in the sand, small again, a fox again, with my side on fire and my legs not working.

I remember hands. His hands, picking me up. Holding me against his chest, right over the mark, where it was warm.

"I've got you," he said.

I had something in my mouth. I'd snatched it from the sand where I'd hidden it. I let it drop into his palm.

A small bronze bell, no bigger than a thumb.

"You dropped this," I said.

He looked at it. He looked at me. And behind us, over the ridge, came the sound I'd been dreading: claws on sand, the howl of Hounds, the drumming of lizards, and under it all, the chant. No words. A hundred voices, none of them breathing.

Zayn tied the bell to his belt. He tucked me into the front of his shirt. He took Tobin's hand.

Behind him, eleven children were holding on to one another in the dark. Some were crying. Some weren't. Two still hummed, under their breath, and didn't know they were doing it.

And he ran.

---

*End of Chapter Fifteen*


---

# Chapter Sixteen
## The Rider Unveiled

> *Every rider in the dark is someone's father. Pray he is yours.*
> — from a lullaby of the mountain herders, sung only to children who will not sleep

---

Have you ever tried to run across the desert at night with eleven children who have forgotten how to cry?

Don't.

Crying children are loud, but loud is a mercy. Loud means they still want something. These ones ran in silence, in a grey cloud of shaved heads, and when one of them fell he didn't call out. He lay with his face pressed into the sand until somebody bigger picked him up, and then he ran again, and his eyes never changed. Every so often, all together, they hummed. Two notes. No words. The chant from the ship's hold. They didn't know they were doing it.

And Zayn ran in front of them with a broken sword in one hand and Tobin's hand in the other and a half-dead fox stuffed down the front of his shirt, and he didn't stop once.

I could feel his heart through his ribs. It was going like something trying to get out.

Behind us, the Hounds were getting closer.

---

You'll remember the Hollow Hounds. Shadow wolves with ember eyes, and no smell, which is the worst of it. A real wolf stinks. You know it's coming. A Hound smells of nothing at all, the way a cold room smells of nothing, and the first you know of it is the cold.

They track the bearer's light the way a wolf tracks blood. Zayn's light was leaking out of him through the golden cracks on his arms, and on that night, with the Ash pared down to a fingernail, he must have shone like a wound.

They were calling to each other. Six voices. Then nine. One of them wasn't howling. One of them, off to the left, was saying *Zayn* in a small wet voice, over and over, the way a child says a word it has only just learned. The wind had carried his name a long way since that night on the dune above Harrow's End. Things had been practising it.
