We came over a ridge, and the far side fell away too steep to run. The children stopped in a huddle at the top. Then, all of them at once, they turned and looked back the way we had come. Calmly. Like people waiting for someone they'd been told would come.

That frightened me more than the Hounds did.

Below us, in the trough we'd just crossed, eyes were opening in the dark. Pairs of them, red as coals, spreading out in a ring.

"Behind me," said Zayn to the children. His voice was very calm. "Stay together. Don't look at their eyes."

He set Tobin's hand into the hand of the oldest boy, who looked at it as if he didn't know whose it was. Zayn lifted the broken sword.

"You can't," I said, from inside his shirt. "Not with half a blade and nothing left. There are too many."

"I know," he said.

My tail glowed. It was true. He knew.

He stood between the dark and the children anyway, and the light came up in him. Not grey. It came up gold, because he had stopped thinking about himself at all, and I have learned to be very afraid of that particular gold. It's the colour they go near the end.

The first Hound leapt.

---

He cut it out of the air.

Half a blade, the way Soren had taught him. The gold ran down his arm into the steel, and when the sword went through the Hound it came apart, and for a moment, before it became ash, I saw what was inside it. Faces. Small grey faces pressed against the inside of the shadow like children against a window. Then sparks, and nothing.

The second one knocked him flat, and I went flying out of his shirt into the sand. Its jaws were an inch from his face. Its breath frosted his eyelashes. He jammed the hilt between its teeth, light burst from his palm, and it shrieked in a voice that was almost a word and was gone.

Then there were four at once.

He fought. He was bleeding from his cheek, the golden cracks were spreading up his arms like a map of rivers, and he was getting slower. I lay in the sand trying to make my legs remember how to be legs, and I watched him get slower.

The fourth Hound came over his shoulder, straight at the children.

The children didn't move. One of the little ones smiled at it.

And a spear came out of the dark.

---

It took the Hound in mid-leap and pinned it, and the Hound burst into ash that fell on the children's upturned faces like grey snow.

Hoofbeats. A pale camel, almost white in the dark, like something that had been dead a while and hadn't been told. On its back, a tall figure in grey wrappings, his face veiled, leaning low from the saddle to wrench his spear free as he passed.

The Veiled Rider. Twice before he'd come out of the dark for Zayn, and never a word, never a face. This was the third time.

He rode through the Hounds like a scythe through dry grass. I'd forgotten how good he was. That's a lie. I hadn't forgotten. I'd hoped to. The spear went left and right, the pale camel spun on the spot, and Hounds burst into ash wherever he went. Zayn fought beside him with gold flaring off the broken sword. The lizard riders came over the far ridge, took one look, and wheeled away, their masks turning to watch us as they went, all at the same angle.

Then it was quiet. Not one of the children was crying. Zayn was on his knees, gasping, grey flakes settling in his hair.

The Rider slid down from his camel. He stood looking at the boy. Then, slowly, as though his hands belonged to someone else, he pulled the veil away.

---

He was a man of perhaps forty with the face of someone left out in the weather for a hundred years. Lean, burned dark, beard cropped short and shot with grey, cheeks fallen in. He had the look of a man who has slept alone in cold places for so long that he's started to forget what other people's voices sound like.

And his eyes were light. Too light for a desert man. Grey-gold, like sand at dawn.

Zayn's eyes.

I watched the understanding arrive in Zayn's face one piece at a time, like the Fire coming up over a dune, and burn everything it touched.

"Who are you?" he whispered.

The man's mouth moved. Nothing came out. He tried again.

"I'm your father," he said.

The little bronze bell on Zayn's belt hung perfectly still. And beside Zayn in the sand, my tail lit up so bright that the man flinched from it as if it were a blade.

---

Zayn didn't move for a long time.

"Three times," he said at last.

"More than that." The man's voice was rough, like a door that hasn't been opened in years and doesn't want to be. "There were nights you never woke."

"You were *there*." Zayn's voice cracked down the middle. "All this time, watching, and you never—" His chest was heaving. "Why did you leave me? In the sand? Why did you *leave* me there?"

The man went down on his knees in front of him.

"To protect you," he said. "They were hunting us both."

The bell stayed silent.

And that was when Zayn broke.

I'd seen him cry before. Not like this. This came from a place under the floor of him, like the catacombs under a city. Fifteen years of it. Every stone. Every *Nameless*. Every night in the patched tent wondering who had thrown him away, and whether they had been right to.

He hit the man once in the chest with his fist. There wasn't any strength in it.

Then he fell forward, and his father caught him.

They knelt in the ash, the boy sobbing into the grey wrappings and the man holding him so tightly I thought something would crack, crying without a sound into his son's pale hair. Up on the ridge the eleven children stood in a row and watched with no expression at all, and hummed their two notes, and I put myself between them and that embrace without quite knowing why.

---

His name was Daruun.

I knew it already. I'd walked beside him once, when he was young and had a feather on his chest and was so afraid of the Crown Dune that he couldn't say its name aloud. He woke screaming most nights that year. He told me he could smell his own hair burning in his sleep. He was the eleventh. The one the murals call *the Runner*. In the City of Glass the glass keeps him as a boy of sixteen, running. It always keeps them as children. Have you noticed that yet? You will.

When he finally looked up from his son, his eyes found me.

"Fox," he said.

"Runner," I said.

Neither of us said anything else. Anything else would have taken all night, and some of it would have been shouting. I'd known who the Rider was from the first spear in the dark, the night of the stones. And I'd said nothing to the boy.

I'm noticing a pattern. I expect you are too. Hold on to it. It gets worse.

---

The mountain caves were a night's walk north, where the desert ended against a wall of red rock like the edge of a grave.

The dunes can't reach the mountains. That's why he went there. The sand rolls up to the cliffs every night and falls back, and it can't write on stone. I went down to the foot of the cliff once, while the others slept, and watched the sand come up against the rock in the dark. It scratched at it. Long, patient strokes. Like something trying to write with its fingernails.

The caves were high up, reached by a path that switched back and forth across the cliff. Inside: a small fire, hides, spears along the walls, and dozens of leather sacks hanging from pegs, each one full of the grey ash of Hollow beasts he'd spent fifteen years hunting. When the fire popped, the sacks shifted on their pegs, all together, as if the ash inside had turned over in its sleep.

The children lay down on the hides in a heap and closed their eyes at the same moment. Zayn counted them twice. Then he sat staring at his own left forearm.

There was writing on it. Scratched into the skin, faint and pink, in the clumsy letters Soren had taught him: *DON'T FORGET HIS EYES.*

"Did you do that?" I said.

"I don't know," said Zayn. "I don't remember doing it." He looked at it a long time. "Whose eyes?"

I didn't answer. I think the grey blades in the ship had already taken the colour of them, and some part of him had felt it going and tried to hold on with a knife, and then forgotten that too.

Daruun sat down by the sleeping children and, very softly, without seeming to notice, began to sing. A low, warm voice. Fire and rising wings.

The children's humming stopped. Every one of them, at once.

Zayn went completely still. It was the voice from the Singing Wells, the man in the water singing over a small boy's hand. He'd been hearing it, somewhere under the floor of himself, his whole life.

---

Daruun told him everything that night. I'll keep it short. Some of it isn't mine to give away.

He had carried the Light before Zayn. He'd been afraid. Not of pain, though there is pain, and he described it, and Zayn listened with his hand pressed to his chest. He was afraid of being gone.

"And something else," he said, looking into the fire. "When you get close to the Crown Dune, you start to hear it. The thing that wants you to burn. It isn't the Hollow, son. It's on the other side. It's hungry too, and it's been hungry a long time." He rubbed his face. "I was sixteen. I didn't want to be eaten. So I ran."

My tail did nothing at all. It didn't glow and it didn't dim. It hung in the firelight like a dead thing, and Daruun looked at it, and then at me, and neither of us said a word.

Because he ran, the Hollow grew. Years later he came down from the mountains and had a family, and was happy for a while. Zayn asked about his mother.

"Another night," said Daruun, and his face closed like a door with something leaning on the other side. "I promise. Not tonight."

The bell didn't ring. He meant it. There wasn't time.

On the night Zayn was born, the Unnamed came out of a storm. They wanted the Light, and they knew it had passed into the newborn. Daruun fought. He got the baby out. But his first son, a boy of five, was dragged into the storm by grey hands, and the last thing Daruun heard was the boy's screaming stop. Not fade. Stop. As if someone had closed a lid.

He searched for weeks. He believed the boy was dead. And he knew that as long as the baby was with him, they would find them both. So he carried his son across the desert to a poor village called Harrow's End, laid him in the sand in the middle of a storm, wrapped in grey cloth, and walked away without looking back, because if he'd looked back he would never have stopped.

"The storm hid my tracks," he said. "That was the desert, protecting you. I had to believe that." He watched the fire. "Every night since, I've wondered whether it was protecting you, or keeping you. Saving you for later. The way you'd save a lamb."

Zayn was quiet for a long time.

"Your son," he said. "The one they took. What was his name?"

Daruun closed his eyes.

"Tamir," he said.

And Zayn told him.

---

The guard in the hold. The phoenix song, hummed in the dark. The light running up the grey blade. A five-year-old boy in a tent, holding a newborn wrapped in grey. The mask cracking down the middle, one pale eye staring out of the crack.

"He's alive," Zayn said. "He's one of them. Brother Seven. He's been hunting me since the start." He swallowed. "And he keeps letting me go. He doesn't know why."

Daruun didn't move. I watched the words go into him and keep going, the way a stone dropped down a well keeps going after you've stopped hearing it.

Then he stood, walked to the mouth of the cave, and went down on his knees on the cold ledge above the desert.

I've heard a great many terrible sounds. The sound Daruun made on that ledge was worse than any of them, because it went on, and then it turned into something that wasn't a sound at all, just his whole body heaving in silence. Fifteen years. His son alive the whole time, his name burned, his face taken, taught to steal other children. And his father hiding in a cave with sacks of ash, telling himself stories.

Zayn didn't go to him. Forgiveness doesn't work like that. Not quickly.

He turned to me.

"Did you know?"

I could have said nothing. I'd had a great deal of practice.

"Yes," I said.

My tail glowed. Something went across his face that I am still, in the long nights, trying to forget.

"You knew he was my *brother*."

"Yes."

He turned away and didn't speak again that night.

I told him the truth about that one. There was another truth I was still sitting on, a heavier one, with blood under it. One out of two. You decide whether that counts.

---

In the morning, we gave the children away.

In a valley at the foot of the cliffs, beside a spring, the Tamarisk clan of nomad herders were camped, as they were every year when the Ash went thin. Their headman was broad and grey-bearded, with a voice like gravel. He looked at the eleven shaved heads for a long time.

"Unnamed?" he said.

"Not anymore," said Zayn.

The children were standing in a row, the way they'd been taught, humming. The headman's wife came out of the nearest tent, saw them, and went back in.

"We'll keep them," he said at last. "Until someone comes for them, or the grey lifts." He lowered his voice. "We'll keep a lamp lit where they sleep. And we'll tie their ankles at night, the first while. The ones who come out of the ship, sometimes they walk."

Tobin held on to Zayn's waist a long time.

"Will you find Bram?" he said into Zayn's shirt.

Zayn couldn't lie. He didn't know.

"If I see him, I'll tell him where you are. I'll tell him you were brave."

That was true. Tobin let go.

The smallest child, a boy of five, tugged Zayn's sleeve. "I don't know my name," he whispered. "They burned it. I watched. It smelled like hair."

Zayn knelt in front of him.

"Then you'll find it," he said. "Or you'll make a new one. I did."

The boy looked at him with no expression at all. Then, very slowly, as if remembering how, he nodded.

My tail glowed all the way back up the cliff.

---

That evening, Daruun gave him two things.

The first was a pendant on a leather cord: a knot of old bronze, a loop that turned back through itself and never ended. The infinity knot.

"Every bearer has worn one," said Daruun. "I took it off the night I ran. It's yours."

Zayn put it on. It lay below the feather mark, and it was warm, the way a stone is warm when someone has been holding it. Nobody had been holding it.

The second was a small, battered compass in a dented brass case.

"It doesn't point north," said Daruun. "It points toward whatever you're running from. For fifteen years it pointed at the Crown Dune. I stopped opening it. I could feel it pointing through the case, like a finger in my back."

Zayn opened it. The needle spun, wobbled, and settled. Not on the desert. Not on the far place where the highest sand in the world waited under the full Ash.

It pointed at the broken sword, lying on the hides beside the fire.

---

He'd carried that sword every step since the full Ash and never once really looked at it, because looking at it meant looking at a body in the morning sand, grey and cracked with gold.

He turned it over now. Half a blade, notched and dull. A plain leather grip. A round bronze pommel.

The pommel was loose. Gritty, as if it had been unscrewed and screwed back in a hurry, in the dark, by someone with shaking hands. He twisted it, and twisted again, and it came away, and the hilt was hollow.

Inside was a roll of paper, tied with thread.

A broken seal of grey wax, pressed with a little curling mark we both knew, because we had watched an old man press that same seal every evening in Thornwell by the lamp, with the falcon on its perch not blinking.

Orrin's seal.

Zayn read it himself. Slowly. Out loud, the way Soren had taught him, his finger under each word.

"*To the Brothers,*" he read. "*On the Night of Embers, when every lantern in Veimore is lit, the king will die. Be ready on the palace stairs. When he falls, put out the lanterns. Then the city. The eldest son is gone. The youngest will come to me. Speak no names.*"

His finger stopped at the bottom.

"*The Voice.*"

---

The fire cracked. On their pegs, the sacks of ash stirred. Down at the foot of the cliff, the sand went on scratching.

"He knew," Zayn said at last. "Soren. He was carrying this. Why didn't he tell me?"

"Perhaps he meant to," said Daruun. "Perhaps he ran out of time."

My tail stayed exactly as it was. I kept my mouth shut and looked at the fire. Let me keep it a little longer. It's the only thing I have that's heavier than I am.

"Soren taught me to read," Zayn said.

It came out of him like something breaking open. As if every evening at the Wells, every backwards S and falling-over R, every time Soren laughed and made him try again, had been leading to this cave and this paper.

As if it had all been for something.

I wanted to believe that with him. It frightened me too. Because when everything in a life starts lining up to lead somewhere, little one, you should ask who drew the line.

Later I saw him unwrap Hesset's broken golden feather. The break had nearly closed. It was mending as he walked toward the end, a little more every day, like a wound closing over a splinter. I looked at it and hated it.

He slid the letter back into the hilt and screwed the pommel tight. Then he stood.

"I'm going to Veimore," he said.

Daruun was on his feet. "Every gate in that city is waiting for the Prince-Killer. You'll walk straight into Orrin's hands."

"Probably," said Zayn.

The bell didn't ring.

"The king has to see this. Soren's little brother is in that palace. And Soren died carrying it." He picked up the sword. "I promised him I'd never give up."

Daruun looked at him a long moment. Then he put a hand on his son's shoulder.

Zayn let it stay. He didn't lean into it. He didn't call him father. But he didn't step away.

It's not nothing. In the desert, you learn to count the things that are not nothing. You count them the way the people of Veimore count their lantern oil: by the drop, knowing exactly how little is left.

---

*End of Chapter Sixteen*


---

# PART FIVE: THE END IS THE BEGINNING


---

# Chapter Seventeen
## The Walls of Veimore

> *Behind every wall burns a lantern; behind every lantern, a fear; beneath every street, a knock. Do not answer it.*
> — a saying of the lantern-keepers of Veimore

---

Veimore was red.

Not the warm red of a sunset. The dark, wet red of something that has been bleeding a long time and has forgotten how to stop. The whole city was built of the same blood-coloured stone, and when the Fire sank behind it, the walls didn't reflect the light. They drank it. In the evenings, if you put your paw against them, they were damp, though it hadn't rained in Veimore in living memory.

People call it the City of the Dead and Darkness. Desert people are dramatic, but Veimore people are only accurate. For as long as anyone remembers, the dead of the whole desert have been carried there on slow camels, wrapped in white, and laid in the catacombs beneath its streets. Above them, on every street, the living paint the names of the dead on shrine walls, so that no one is forgotten.

The names don't stay.

That's the part the songs leave out. The paint fades. Not over years. Over days. A name painted at dawn is pale by sunset and gone in a week, as if something on the other side of the stone were slowly drinking it. So Veimore has name-painters the way other cities have water-carriers: hundreds of pale, quiet men with pots of red paint, working the streets day and night, going over and over the letters. Some names have been painted so many times the stone has worn into grooves, and still they fade.

And the lanterns. Thousands of them, on the walls, the rooftops, in every window and under every arch, burning day and night, because the people of Veimore believe that the moment the last lantern goes out, the darkness will walk in. Their smoke hangs over the city so that even at noon the sky is the colour of an old bruise.

We lay at the top of the last dune and watched the grey press against the walls as night came on. It didn't come in. It sat just beyond the lantern-light, patient as a dog outside a butcher's door.

"It's waiting," said Zayn.

"Everything out here is waiting," I said. "That's what the desert is for."

I want you to see him properly, because there isn't much time left to look. He was thinner than in Harrow's End, his hair bleached nearly white. Golden cracks ran along both forearms and up his neck, fading so slowly now that the old ones hadn't gone before the new ones came. The words on his arm, *DON'T FORGET HIS EYES*, had scabbed over. I'd caught him reading them with a puzzled frown, like a stranger's letter. Soren's broken sword was strapped across his back, the proof inside the hilt. He touched it every hundred breaths, the way he touched his mark.

Behind us, Daruun stood by the pale camel, and I could feel him not saying things. It ran in the family.

"I'll stay out here," he said. "The Unnamed won't come through the gates like honest men. I'll see them first." His voice snagged on the name, the way it still did. "And Zayn. Come back out to me. After. Promise me."

The boy was quiet for a long time.

You know why. He *couldn't* promise. He'd started waking in the night beating at his own chest, at flames that weren't there, the smell of scorched cloth in his nose. If he'd said *I promise*, the mark would have burned him like a coal.

"I'll try," he said.

My tail glowed. It was completely true. It was nothing like enough.

Daruun rode away along the hollow between the dunes, and the wind smoothed out his tracks behind him, the way it does for things the desert intends to keep.

---

Every guard on every gate knew what to look for. *A pale-haired boy, a fox with odd eyes, a broken blade. The Prince-Killer.*

So we didn't use the gates.

"He told me about it," Zayn said. We were crouched in the reeds where the oasis water slid out under the wall through a low arch furred black with moss. "An old water channel. It runs under the city and comes up beneath the palace kitchens. He used to sneak out through it."

"And back in? For what?"

Zayn smiled, small and crooked and full of grief. "Honey cakes."

The water was waist-deep on Zayn, which made it considerably more on me. I rode his shoulder. His mark glowed through his wet shirt and gave us just enough light to see by.

Which was a pity, because then we could see the walls.

Halfway along, the channel ran through the catacombs. The stone on either side was cut into shelves going up into the dark and on into the dark, and on every shelf lay a long white shape, wrapped and still. Above each, a name in faded red. Down here there were no painters. Down here the names had been left to do what names in Veimore do, and most were nearly gone: a ghost of a letter, a smear like a thumbprint.

And the catacombs breathed.

I don't mean the air moved. I mean it went *in*, all of it, down the length of the tunnel, slow and steady, pulling at the fur on my face. A long pause. Then it came back out, warm, smelling of old linen and something sweet underneath, like fruit gone soft in a closed jar. The water rose a finger's width against Zayn's chest on the in-breath and sank on the out.

He read some of the names as we passed. A boy who's only just learned to read reads everything.

"*Hamid*," he whispered. "*Little Dara. Joss the potter. Sefa, beloved.*" As he said *Sefa*, the last red letters slid down the stone like a drop of water and were gone. He stopped. "Did I do that?"

"No," I said. My tail glowed. "They do that by themselves."

Where the channel bent there was an alcove, older than the rest, with eleven shelves in it. Small ones. Too short for a grown man. Child-length. Empty. Above them no names at all, only eleven little painted feathers, so old they were barely brown.

Zayn stood in the water and looked at the eleven short shelves for a long time.

"Kiri," he said. "Who paints the names of the bearers?"

I didn't answer. There was nothing true I could have said that he needed to hear. I'll tell you, though, because you'll need it. Nobody does. Nobody ever has. The desert remembers the *bearers*. Not the children.

Then, from somewhere under us, under the water and the stone floor of the channel, came a knock.

Three knocks. Slow. Patient. The way you'd knock on the door of someone you knew was home.

Neither of us moved. The catacombs drew in a long breath and held it.

The knocking didn't come again. After a while the air went out, warm and sweet, and Zayn walked on much faster, and didn't read any more names.

The channel ended in slippery steps and a wooden hatch. Zayn pushed it up an inch, and through the gap came a smell so wonderful I nearly fell off his shoulder: bread, onions, cinnamon, and honey.

He climbed out into a storeroom full of sacks and jars and hanging garlic, and found himself face to face with a cleaver.

---

The woman holding it was the largest person I had seen in some centuries.

Nearly as tall as Daruun and twice as wide, with forearms like a blacksmith's and a face as round and red as one of the city's bricks. She held the cleaver close to the hatch, at the height of a head coming up out of it, the way you would if you'd been waiting.

"Back down," she said. Her voice could have stripped paint. "Whatever you are. I heard the knocking. You've got a count of three to show me you're breathing on your own—"

Then she saw the sword. The wrapping had come loose in the channel, and the lamplight fell across the hilt.

The cleaver dropped an inch.

"Where," she said, in a completely different voice, "did you get that?"

Zayn stood dripping on her clean floor and told her the truth, because it was the only thing he had ever known how to do.

"It was Soren's. He was my friend. I didn't kill him."

I sat on a sack of lentils and let my tail light up gold, so bright the jars threw shadows.

"His tail does that when someone's telling the truth," Zayn said, a little apologetically.

"It also does it when it's warm and dry," I said. "Which it isn't. I've been waist-deep in your dead. Thank you for asking."

The woman shrieked. Then she clapped a hand over her mouth, and her eyes went wide, and then wet. The cleaver rang on the stone.

"I knew it," she whispered through her fingers. "I *knew* it. I told them, and nobody listens to the cook."

She was Mother Yusa. She'd run the palace kitchens for thirty years and half-raised the king's two sons, because the king was busy being a king and somebody had to make sure the boys ate. She told us this while she sat Zayn on a crate and put lentils and honey cakes in front of him.

"That boy," she said, pointing a floury finger at the hilt as if Soren himself were inside it, "stole honey cakes from my kitchen for ten years. Ten years! Came up out of that drain smelling of the dead, every single time, and thought I didn't know." Her chin wobbled. She steadied it. "A boy like that doesn't kill anyone. And neither do you. Eat."

Zayn ate slowly, the way he'd eaten Auntie Sabba's bundles. When he got to the honey cakes, he held one in his palm.

"He told me about these," he said. "He said nobody in the world made them like you."

Mother Yusa turned round to stir a pot that didn't need stirring, and her big shoulders shook for a while.

---

She hid us behind the oil jars in the back storeroom, great clay things taller than Zayn.

"Don't knock them," she said. "That's three months' light for this whole wing. People in this city have been killed for less oil than you could carry in your mouth."

She wasn't exaggerating. On our way to the walls I'd seen the oil-sellers at the outer gate with their little brass spoons, measuring it out a thumb at a time to silent, grey-faced queues. A thumb of oil for a day's water. In Veimore they don't say *I'd give my right arm*. They say *I'd give my oil*, and they mean it the way other people mean blood.

The king did not eat, she told us, as if that were the worst of it. Since the news of Soren's death he'd sent back every plate, and the only person he would listen to was his vizier.

"Orrin," said Zayn. She spat into the fire.

"Grey eyes. Soft voice. You never hear him coming. He's brought the king Soren's ring. Said he'd taken it back from the Prince-Killer's own hand, in some oasis town. Proof, he called it, that the glowing boy robbed our Soren's body." She looked at Zayn sideways. "The king wears it on a chain now. Over his heart."

Zayn's hand had gone to the hilt.

"They painted Soren's name on the shrine wall in the palace court," she said, lower. "Under the traitors. Orrin's idea. And the painters aren't allowed to touch it up. So it's fading. Every day there's a bit less of him." Her voice thinned. "And the little one, Idan, sits in front of it every night at sunset. He stole a pot of red paint from the painters. He goes over the letters with his finger. He doesn't say a word."

Then she asked the question I'd been dreading.

"How did he die? Our Soren. Did it… did it hurt him?"

Zayn opened his mouth and closed it. He didn't know, and it had been cutting him every night for a turn of the Ash.

"I wasn't there," he said. "I should have been."

My tail glowed. I looked at the floor.

Mother Yusa looked at me. She was a woman who'd spent thirty years knowing which kitchen boy had broken a jar by the set of his shoulders. She didn't ask me anything.

I was grateful. I was ashamed of being grateful.

I'll tell you about that night soon, little one. Not yet. Let me be a coward a little longer.

---

Idan came down after midnight.

Bare feet on the back stairs, and then someone very carefully lifting the lid off the honey-cake jar, exactly the way someone else had probably taught him.

Zayn stood up behind the oil jars. His mark glowed.

The boy froze with a cake halfway to his mouth. He was ten, small for it, with his brother's dark eyes and none of his swagger, and red paint dried under every fingernail, as if he'd been scratching at a wall. The cake fell out of his hand.

"That's Soren's," he whispered.

"Yes."

"They said you killed him."

"I didn't."

Gold, steady and warm, from the tip of my tail. Idan looked at it.

"Your brother tried to eat a scorpion once," I told him. "Was sick for a whole day. Turned the colour of a lizard. I've never been prouder of anyone."

I've outlived too many children to be good with them. But Idan made a small cracked sound, and it took me a moment to understand it was a laugh.

Then he sat down on the floor and cried, and Zayn sat beside him, close enough that their shoulders touched, the way you'd sit beside someone at a fire to share the warmth.

"His name's going," Idan said into his knees. "I painted it three times today and at dinner the S was gone again." He looked up. "Is that what happens? If nobody remembers you hard enough?"

Zayn didn't answer. Under the floor, very faintly, something knocked three times, and the boy didn't even flinch. He'd grown up with it.

Later, Zayn took out the letter. It was soft from handling, the grey wax still holding its little curling mark.

"You can read?" said Idan.

"Your brother taught me. In the sand. With a stick." Zayn touched the letters as if they were still warm. "He was a bad teacher. He kept laughing."

Idan read it by the light of Zayn's mark. I watched his face change halfway down. *On the Night of Embers, when every lantern in Veimore is lit, the king will die. When he falls, put out the lanterns. Then the city.*

"The Night of Embers is tomorrow," Idan said. "And the Ash will be full."

Zayn touched his mark and winced. It was hot. It was hot all the time now.

"Father hears petitions in the morning," Idan said. "I could take you in."

---

The throne hall at the heart of the black-and-red palace had no windows. It was lit only by lanterns, hundreds of them, hanging on long chains from a ceiling so high and dark they looked like Embers in a starless sky. The smoke was thick enough to taste. Every sound went up into the shadows and came back softer and slightly wrong, a word you hadn't said, a cough a beat late, as though the dark were listening and practising.

At the far end, seven steps rose to a chair of black rock, and in it sat the saddest man I have ever seen.

King Absalon had been tall and broad once. You could see the shape of it, the way you see the shape of a house after the roof has gone. A heavy ring hung on a thin gold chain at his neck, and every few breaths his hand drifted up to touch it.

One step below the throne stood Orrin. Silver hair, smooth face, soft robes the colour of sand. His faded grey eyes found Zayn the instant the boy stepped out from behind Idan.

He smiled.

Not the smile of a man who's won. A small one. The smile of a man who sets a trap and comes back in the morning to find the fox in it, exactly where he'd pictured it.

"The Prince-Killer!" someone shouted, and then everyone was shouting. Spears came down. A courtier shrieked "*Monster!*" in a voice like a goose, and I saw the gold on Zayn's skin flicker grey for a breath.

"*Stop!*" Idan had planted himself in front of Zayn with his arms out, shaking. "Father, he has something for you. You have to *listen*."

The king looked at his son a long time. Then he lifted one tired hand, and the spears went up.

Zayn climbed to the bottom step and read the letter aloud. Every word. His voice didn't shake. Soren had made him practise until it didn't.

When he finished, the hall was so quiet I could hear the lanterns hissing, and under the floor, far down, the slow breathing of the catacombs.

"That is Vizier Orrin's seal," said Zayn. "Soren took this from one of the Unnamed and hid it in his sword. They killed him before he could bring it home."

The king looked at the seal for a long time. Then at Orrin.

And Orrin sighed, gently, like a teacher whose favourite pupil has made a small mistake.

"Your Majesty will remember that I reported my seal stolen on the night of the prince's exile, and had a new one cut. It is in the palace records." He spread his soft hands. "I did not wish to believe Soren had taken it. But here it is. A forgery in a broken sword, carried by the boy who killed him, on the very eve of the Night of Embers. It is clever. I'll grant them that." His eyes went, gently, to the ring on its chain at the king's breast. "Your Majesty already wears the proof of what that boy took from Soren's body."

Long before Soren found that letter, Orrin had made sure that if it was ever found, it would look like a lie. 

"Did you kill Soren?" said Zayn. It came out of him the way the Light did, without permission.

Orrin looked down at him, soft and sad. "I loved that boy," he said, "as if he were my own son."

From the cord at Zayn's belt, Hesset's little bronze truth bell rang.

In that hall it sounded like a stone dropped down a well. Every head turned. And up in the dark above the lanterns, the hall rang it back, faint and wrong, a dozen tiny bells that weren't there.

"A desert trinket," said Orrin, without looking. "Bells ring when boys shake them, Majesty."

"He didn't shake it," said Idan.

"I didn't kill your son," Zayn said to the king, very low. "I left him alone that night. We argued, and I walked away, and when I came back he was gone. That's the worst thing I've ever done. But I didn't kill him."

The mark on his chest didn't burn. My tail blazed so bright the nearest courtiers shaded their eyes.

I watched the king waver, like a dune shifting at night. Believing us meant believing he had exiled his son for a lie and worn his ring over his heart because the man who killed him had handed it to him. No father can look at that straight on. Not all at once.

"Hold the boy," King Absalon said at last. "In the east tower, under guard. After the Night of Embers, I will hear this again. From both of you." He looked at Orrin. "Both."

"Of course, Majesty," said Orrin, and bowed. "Shall we cancel the festival? For safety?"

The king closed his eyes. "No. The people need their lanterns."

As the guards took Zayn's arms, I saw the vizier's face, for an instant, when he thought no one was looking.

He was still smiling.

---

The tower room was small and round, with a window facing east. They took the sword and the bell. Nobody had worked out how you arrest a fox.

The Fire went down. All over the city, doors were barred, hundreds of bolts sliding home one after another like a great slow clock. Then the whispering began from every rooftop and courtyard: the people of Veimore reading the names of their dead off the walls before the paint could fade any further. *Hamid. Dara. Joss. Sefa.* And under the whispering, all across the city, so faint you'd think it was your own heart, the knocking. Three. Pause. Three. From under every street at once.

"They're answering," Zayn said at the window. "Something down there's answering the names."

I didn't tell him he was wrong. My tail wouldn't have let me.

The Ash came up over the dunes. One more night and it would be perfectly round.

A soft sound at the door. Not a guard. Guards stamp.

Orrin's face appeared at the little barred window in the wood. In the dark his eyes looked almost white, like a Husk's.

"Thank you," he said gently, "for coming. I've wondered for a turn of the Ash how to bring you inside these walls on the Night of Embers, under the full Ash. And you came by yourself, up through the dead, with a letter in your hand." He sounded almost fond. "The desert really does write the sands, doesn't it? It wants you here as much as I do. Ask yourself why, bearer."

Then, softer still: "Tomorrow night, the lanterns go out."

My tail glowed bright gold in that little room, and Zayn saw it, and I saw him understand. Everything Orrin had said was true. That was what made it so terrible.

The footsteps went away down the stair.

Zayn went to the window, and I hopped onto the sill. Below, the city lay under its haze, ten thousand small flames holding back the dark, each one bought a thumb at a time. And on the roof opposite, at the very edge of the lantern-glow, a figure in a grey robe crouched with two grey blades across his knees. His mask was a smooth white face with a crack running down the middle, brow to chin.

He was looking up at our window.

And very faintly, under the whispering of all those names and the knocking beneath them, so faint I might have been the only one in the world who could hear it, he was humming.

---

*End of Chapter Seventeen*


---

# Chapter Eighteen
## The Night of Embers

> *On the Night of Embers, every lantern is a name. When the lanterns go out, so do the names.*
> — painted above the shrine gate of the palace court, Veimore; repainted daily

---

Once a year, on the night the Ash comes up perfectly round, the whole desert lights lanterns for the lost bearers.

You'll see it soon enough. In the small villages it's a few clay lamps on doorsteps. Everyone goes to bed feeling vaguely holy and slightly relieved, the way you feel after a funeral that wasn't yours.

In Veimore they did it the Veimore way. Every family carried one extra lantern through the streets at dusk, filled with oil they had saved for a year, a thumb at a time, and hung it on the shrine walls where the names of the dead were painted, so that for one night the names glowed and nothing could drink them. And on the great black stairs of the palace, eleven bronze lanterns of coloured glass were set out in a row. One for each bearer who had come before.

I sat on the sill of the tower window and watched them being lit, one by one, by a priest with a long taper.

The Shepherd. The Weaver's Daughter. The Smith. The Twin Who Stayed. The Last King.

I knew every one of them. I knew which ones had wet themselves on the last night and been ashamed, and which ones had cried for their mothers. The Boy of Salt was nine. Nobody in that city knew any of their names. That's the joke. Not one person down there could tell you who they were remembering, or how old, or what they'd smelled like burning.

The Stone Singer. The Boy of Salt. The Falconer. The Blind One. The Dreamer. The Runner.

Eleven.

Behind me, Zayn sat against the wall with his eyes shut. He wasn't asleep. The mark was glowing steadily, brighter than I'd ever seen it, as though it knew what night it was and was looking forward to it.

"How many?" he asked.

"Eleven," I said.

He was quiet a moment.

"There should be another one," he said. "Tomorrow."

I didn't say anything. My tail said it for me.

---

The first lantern went out just after the Ash cleared the walls.

It was on the far western wall, one tiny flame among thousands, and then it simply wasn't there. Then the one beside it. Then the next. A gap of darkness opened along the top of the wall, small at first, then spreading, like a black thread being drawn slowly through a golden cloth.

Down in the city, the whispering stopped.

Every night of their lives, the people of Veimore had read the names of their dead into the dark. They had never once stopped. Now, all at once, ten thousand voices went quiet, and ten thousand faces turned west toward the black gap in the wall.

In the silence, from under every street in the city, came the knocking.

Not three knocks. Not patient. Hundreds of fists, all at once, hammering on the underside of the city like people trapped beneath ice.

Then somebody screamed, and every lantern on the southern wall went out together.

"Kiri," said Zayn. He was on his feet.

"I see it."

There were shapes on the walls. Grey robes, white masks. The Unnamed walked the battlements in a line, and as they passed each lantern they cupped a hand over it, almost tenderly, the way you'd close a dead man's eyes, and it died. They were humming the wordless chant from the ship.

And where the light died, the grey came in.

It poured over the top of the walls like fog over a cliff edge and slid down into the streets. Where it touched the shrine walls, the names didn't fade. They *ran*. The red paint went liquid and slid down the stone in long streaks, a hundred years of names bleeding off the walls at once into the gutters, and the grey lapped them up as they went.

Then the grates in the streets began to lift.

The iron grates over the catacomb shafts. All over the city they rose, slowly, pushed from below, and out of them came hands. Then arms. Then figures. Grey, white-eyed, wrapped in rotted white linen that trailed behind them. They had been down there a long time, knocking, waiting to be let in.

The people of Veimore saw them climbing out of the ground in grave-wrappings and thought their dead were rising.

Some of them were right.

That was when the real screaming started.

---

Someone was at the door.

There had been two guards on the stair, and I'd heard nothing from them for some time. The bolt slid back. The door swung open.

Brother Seven stood in the doorway.

Through the crack in his mask I could see a thin line of his real face: the edge of one pale eye, a stripe of cheek so white it had never seen the Fire.

He had Soren's sword in one hand and Hesset's bell in the other.

He held them out.

Neither brother moved. The only sound was the screaming below and the knocking under it.

"Tamir," said Zayn.

The figure flinched as if struck. The sword and the bell clattered to the floor. He stepped back, then again, and his hands came up to his mask and gripped the edges of it, and pulled. I saw the skin of his jaw stretch where the mask's rim met it. It didn't come away. It had been on fifteen years. It had grown there. He let go, shaking, with a sound like a hurt dog.

He hummed two notes. Just two. Not the cult's chant. The beginning of the phoenix song.

Then he turned and ran.

Zayn picked up the sword. His hands were trembling.

"He's still in there," he said.

"Yes," I said. My tail glowed. I'd known since the first night he let Zayn go. I put that on the pile with the rest.

---

The streets were full of grey.

We ran down through them into the lanes where the families had gone to hang their lanterns. Most of the lanterns were out. The ones still burning lay smashed on the stones, oil spreading in burning pools, and people were on their knees beside them scooping the burning oil up in their bare hands to save it, screaming as they did it, and not stopping.

And everywhere, Husks.

They moved through the crowds slowly, reaching out, touching. Every touch, someone stopped running. Stood in the street with their hands at their sides and their eyes going white and lost. And then, a moment later, the emptied ones began to speak. Each of them said the last thing they had said before the touch. Over and over, in a flat, pleasant voice, smiling with faces that didn't know how to smile anymore.

*Bar the door. Bar the door. Bar the door.*

*Mama, I'm here. Mama, I'm here.*

*Where's the oil, where's the oil, where's the oil.*

At a shrine wall halfway down the lane, a little girl stood alone. She couldn't have been more than six. A Husk had touched her cheek, and the grey was spreading from it across her face like a stain through cloth, and she was looking up at the wall where her family's names had been. There was nothing on it now but long red streaks running down into the gutter.

"Whose names were those?" she said to no one. "I can't… whose were they?"

Then she said it again, in exactly the same voice. And again. The grey had reached the corner of her mouth.

Zayn was on his knees in front of her before I could stop him.

"You'll bring every Hound in the city down on us," I said.

"Yes," said Zayn, and put his hands on her face.

The light came gold. It always did, by then, when it was for someone else. It poured over her cheek, and the grey shrank back from it like frost from the Fire. Her eyes cleared. On the wall behind her, a single name glowed faint and gold for a moment where the paint had run. *Mira.* Her grandmother. Then it faded. But the girl remembered it. I watched her remember.

And Zayn saw.

Her past: a woman's wrinkled hands braiding her hair. Her future: a grown girl reading the names aloud on this same wall.

And then his own death came, the way it always came now. Not a picture anymore. A place he was *in*.

The full Ash, close enough to touch. The cold sand of the Crown Dune under his bare feet. And the heat. Starting in his chest, under the mark, the way a coal starts in kindling. He felt his skin split along the golden lines. He smelled it: hair, cloth, and something under them that was himself. A small boy's hand slipping out of his. His father on his knees in the sand. Grey hands plunging into the gold. Letters, far below, moving across the dunes, a word he could almost read. And behind the letters, something else, at the very edge of the world, something that was watching the fire the way a man watches a pot come to the boil.

He came back screaming.

He'd never done that before. He knelt on the stones with his hands beating at his own chest, at flames that weren't there, and the little girl stared at him, and I put my paws on his face and said his name until he stopped. The golden cracks had spread along his jaw. There was a smell of scorching coming off his shirt. Real scorching. The cloth over his mark had browned in the shape of a feather.

The girl's mother found her and dragged her away into the dark without a word.

"I saw a word," Zayn said. His voice was raw. "In the sand. And something past it."

"Later," I said, because across the lane three Hounds had come out of the grey with their ember eyes fixed on him, and their lips were peeling back, and the one in front was saying *Zayn, Zayn* in a little wet child's voice.

---

They never reached us.

A spear came out of the dark and took the first through the chest, and it burst into black sand. The pale camel came down the lane like a ghost, and on its back was Daruun, his veil torn half away, his spear already swinging for the second. He was always going to come. Fifteen years late, but always.

"*The palace!*" he shouted. "They're inside! Go!"

The third Hound sprang at Zayn's throat.

It met me instead.

I don't like to talk about the other shape. But it happened on that street, in front of a dozen people of Veimore, and I didn't care who saw. I broke out of my small fox body like a coal bursting from a fire. I was huge and black and burning, my eyes were embers, and the silver half of me that belongs to the Hollow howled with joy. That's the part I hate. Not the size. The joy. The Hound came apart in my jaws like smoke, and it tasted like home.

The people in the lane screamed at me the way they'd screamed at the things in the grates. They weren't wrong to.

The old burn on my side, where Zayn's light had caught me in the storm, tore open.

Behind us, the grey was rolling down the lane like a wave, full of smiling faces.

"Go," I said to Zayn. My voice shook the shutters. "I'll hold them. *Go.*"

He looked at me. At the monster I was, the thing I'd hidden from him and from every bearer before him.

He wasn't afraid of me. He hadn't been for a long time. I hadn't noticed when it changed.

"Don't die," he said.

"I'm a great deal harder to kill than you," I said. "That's sort of the whole problem."

He ran.

---

The palace stairs were black stone, wide enough for twenty men abreast, rising in one long climb from the court to the great doors. The eleven bronze lanterns stood along them. Seven had gone out. The other four guttered, throwing coloured light across the steps: red, then green, then gold. In the court below, Soren's name was gone from the shrine wall, the stone perfectly clean, Idan's little pot of paint smashed beneath it.

Brother One was waiting at the top.

He was the largest man I've ever seen, and I had recently met Mother Yusa. He stood before the doors with his feet planted wide and a grey blade as long as Zayn was tall. Tonight, for the first time I'd seen, he wore a mask, smooth and white and perfect. He had never once, in all his years at Orrin's side, been heard to speak.

He didn't speak now. He came down the stairs.

Zayn met him halfway.

The first blow would have taken Zayn's head if he hadn't dropped flat onto the steps; it struck the stone and threw sparks. The second came straight down. Zayn rolled, and the edge skimmed across his shoulder.

It didn't cut skin. Grey blades don't need to. But the light dimmed where it touched, and Soren's face was gone.

Not blurred. Gone. He knew he'd had a friend. He knew the friend had been important, more important than anyone. He reached for the face and found a blank, smooth space where it should have been, as smooth as the mask above him. He reached for the eyes, for *DON'T FORGET HIS EYES*, and there was nothing to not forget.

He lay on the stairs and couldn't remember, and worse, he could feel himself beginning not to mind.

Then, out of the grey, a voice. Not the face. Just a voice, laughing at him by a fire a long way back.

*A short blade means you have to get close. Most people are afraid to get close. So be the one who isn't.*

Soren had said that on the third night, waving half a sword and showing off. Zayn had rolled his eyes. He remembered rolling his eyes. And from that one small, stupid memory the rest came back, all at once, like water into a footprint: the scarred jaw, the crooked grin, the dark eyes always laughing. *Dark.* His eyes were dark. *There* you are.

Zayn got up.

Brother One swung again, wide, to keep him away. Zayn didn't go away. He went *in*. Under the swing, up inside it, so close the long grey blade was useless, so close he could hear the man breathing behind the mask, quick and shallow, like someone very frightened.

He raised Soren's broken sword.

And the light came.

Down his arm, into the hilt, along the broken blade, and where the steel ended it kept going. For one heartbeat Soren's sword was whole again, the missing half made of gold as bright as the Fire. It met the grey blade where hilt joined steel.

The grey blade shattered.

The pieces fell down the black stairs, ringing like bells. Brother One stumbled. His heel caught the edge of a step, and he fell, a long way, all the way down to the bottom, where he lay still beside the last guttering lantern.

His mask had cracked in half. Under it was a tired grey-bearded man with his eyes closed. He was breathing. His lips were moving. He was trying, I think, to say a name. He'd been silent so long he'd forgotten how.

Zayn stood on the stairs with the golden half-blade fading in his hand.

Then, from inside the throne hall, a child screamed.

---

The great doors stood open, and inside, the dark had won.

All those hundreds of lanterns on their chains had gone out but one. It hung high above the throne, a single small flame swinging slowly, and by its light I could see grey robes and white masks standing in rows along the walls. Unnamed. Dozens of them. Humming. And at the top of the seven steps, beside the black stone throne, Orrin.

He had Idan.

He held the boy against his chest with one arm, quite gently, the way you'd hold a sleepy child. In his other hand was a grey blade, small and thin as a reed.

And on the steps below him lay the king.

Zayn ran to him. Nobody stopped him. That was the worst of it.

King Absalon lay on his back on the black steps with one hand still stretched toward his son. There was no blood. Grey blades don't do blood. The grey was spreading out from his chest like ink through water, and as it spread, it took him. His beard was going white in streaks. The colour was leaving his lips. His eyes were open, and they were fading from the edges in, the brown draining out of them toward the middle.

"He stepped in front," said Orrin softly, from above. "The blade was meant for the boy, to make him easier to carry. His Majesty stepped in front of it. I didn't expect that. He hasn't moved quickly in years."

Zayn put his hands on the king's chest.

The light came, gold, and the grey drank it. That's what a grey blade does. It was like pouring water into sand. He pushed harder, and harder, until the cracks on his own skin blazed and split wider and began, at the edges, to smoke. The king's eyes cleared for a moment.

And Zayn saw. The king, young, swinging a small dark-haired boy onto his shoulders. The same boy dragged out of this hall in chains while the king didn't look up. Then no future. Only dark.

And then his own death again, and this time he didn't scream, because he didn't have the breath.

"Soren," the king whispered. "I didn't listen to him. I should have… listened."

"I know," said Zayn.

"Tell him." The king's hand fumbled at the chain around his neck and found the ring. He pulled, and the chain broke, and he pressed the ring into Zayn's palm. "Tell him I'm sorry. And give this to Idan. When he's king."

And Zayn said, "I'll tell him."

My tail glowed.

I was in the doorway by then, small again, limping, bleeding from the old burn. I saw my tail light up bright gold in that dark hall, and I understood what it meant, and I wanted to be sick. The boy couldn't lie. He believed, with his whole heart, that before the night was over he would see Soren to tell him.

The king's eyes went past Zayn to his younger son. He smiled at Idan, a small, broken, loving smile, and the grey reached his eyes as he did it, and the smile stayed on his face after the rest of him had gone. It stayed there, fixed and pleasant, a Husk's smile on a king's mouth.

Idan stopped screaming.

---

"How touching," said Orrin.

High above the throne, the last lantern in the hall went out.

In the dark, Zayn's light was the only light. It came off him in waves, and it wasn't gold anymore. It had gone grey, dim and flickering, because he was angrier than I had ever seen him, angry enough to tear that hall down stone by stone and the people in it.

That was what Orrin wanted. The light goes grey when the bearer hates. Grey light is easy to take.

"*Zayn,*" I said. "*Don't.*"

He heard me. He shut his eyes and breathed, and slowly the grey went out of him. Not all the way. Enough.

When he opened them, Orrin was at the back of the hall, by the open door to the eastern balconies, with Idan under one arm. Beyond came the hiss of sand lizards in the court and the howling of Hounds.

Idan wasn't crying. He wasn't making any sound at all. He was humming. Two notes. The chant. Orrin had been holding him for only a few minutes, and already he was humming.

"The Crown Dune, bearer," said Orrin. His soft voice filled the dark the way it always had. "Under the full Ash. You know the way. You've been there every time you've closed your eyes. Come and give me your name. Or the boy will give me his. He's already halfway to forgetting it."

Then he was gone, and the masks along the walls went with him like smoke.

By the time we reached the balcony, the lizards were out through the eastern gate and running, a dark knot of riders with the Hounds loping around them. Orrin rode at the front with Idan before him. And at the very back, a lone rider with twin blades turned in his saddle and looked up at the palace.

He looked a long time. Then he rode after the others.

---

We stood on the eastern balcony, the boy and the fox, and a moment later Daruun came up the stairs behind us with grey dust on his clothes and his spear dark to the elbow. Below us the city was black. Every lantern was out. In the streets, the Husks had stopped moving, and stopped speaking. They stood in the lanes like statues, every white face turned the same way.

East.

We turned to look.

The desert had moved in the night.

It stood a little way out from the walls, so close you could have walked there before the Ash went down. A dune, higher than any dune in the world. Higher than the mountains where Daruun had hidden for fifteen years. It rose out of the flat sand in one long, impossible curve, and as I watched, the sand on its flanks moved. Slowly. Up. Like the side of something breathing. At its very top, white and enormous and perfectly round, sat the full Ash.

The Crown Dune.

It had come to fetch him. Things that are hungry enough don't wait for their food to walk.

Daruun made a sound beside me that I have heard only a few times in all my life. It's the sound a father makes when he understands he can't stop what is coming, and that when he ran from it at sixteen, all he did was keep it waiting for his son.

Zayn looked down at the ring in his hand. Soren's ring. He closed his fingers round it and put it on the cord at his neck, beside the infinity knot. His knuckles were cracked with gold. Where his fingers touched the ring, it smoked.

"It's time," he said.

Neither of us answered. There was nothing left that was true and kind at the same time.

---

*End of Chapter Eighteen*


---

# Chapter Nineteen
## The Crown Dune

> *He'll walk where the dunes write the words of the sky,*
> *And the light will be born on the day he will die.*
> — from the Song of the Phoenix-Born; the verse mothers in the desert villages will not sing aloud

---

Mother Yusa found us on the palace stairs as we were leaving.

She'd come up out of the kitchens with a ladle like a club and a long red scratch down her cheek where something had reached for her in the dark. She saw the king's ring on the cord at Zayn's neck and understood everything at once. She didn't cry. She didn't ask. She pushed a small cloth bundle into his hands, took his face between her two enormous palms, and kissed his forehead hard, the way you'd stamp a seal into wax.

She pulled back with her lips blistered. His skin was that hot. She didn't say a word about it.

Then she went back down into the dark to look for Idan's nurse, and I never saw her again. I have never asked what found her first.

Inside the bundle were dates. Rolled in honey.

---

We rode out through the eastern gate, Daruun in front and Zayn behind him and me in the boy's lap, bleeding quietly into his shirt.

The Husks still stood where they'd stopped, every one facing east. As we passed, they began to say their last words again, very softly, all together, so that we went out of Veimore through a long low murmur of *bar the door* and *Mama, I'm here* and *where's the oil*. Then, one by one, they stopped speaking and only smiled.

Zayn unwrapped the bundle and held the dates in his palm.

He ate one. He held one out over Daruun's shoulder, and after a moment his father took it. Then he gave one to me, and I ate it, though I prefer scorpions and always will.

"This is the hardest day," Zayn said. "I don't think there's a harder one."

My tail glowed.

The Crown Dune rose ahead of us until it filled half the sky, and its flanks went on moving. Up. Pause. Down.

"Father," said Zayn.

It was the first time he'd called him that. I felt Daruun go rigid in the saddle.

"I forgive you."

Daruun didn't turn round. His shoulders shook. "You can't. I ran. If I hadn't run, none of this— your brother, the Hollow, all of it. You can't forgive that."

"I just did," said Zayn. "And I can't lie. So."

My tail lit up so bright it threw our shadow out ahead of us across the sand, long and gold, like a road.

Daruun reached back without looking, and Zayn took his hand, and they rode like that all the way to the foot of the dune. Zayn's hand was so hot that Daruun's palm blistered. He didn't let go.

---

The Husks were waiting.

Not only the ones from Veimore. All of them. They had come from the whole desert in the night, from the grey wastes and the emptied villages, from Harrow's End and the caravan roads, and they stood in a ring around the foot of the Crown Dune a hundred deep. Herders. Traders. Old women. Children. Thousands of white faces turned up toward the top of the dune, all smiling the same smile, all humming the cult's two notes so low that the sound came up through the sand into our legs. It was like standing on the skin of a drum.

They parted for us like water.

I saw Garrem from the trading post among them, and old Oszkar, his beard full of sand. I didn't see Bram.

We climbed. The sand slid under our feet, and under the sand something moved with the breathing, so that every step went down a little further than it should. Halfway up, Zayn turned and picked me up without a word and carried me the rest of the way. His arms singed my fur. I let him. I was too tired to be proud.

And then we were at the top, and I looked up, and it was the vision.

I had seen it in his eyes every time he healed. Here it was, every piece falling into place like the last stones of a tomb.

The Ash hung full and white above the Crown Dune, so close and so swollen it looked like something that had fed. The crest was pale sand, so cold after his skin that it ached. Below us, on every side, the desert went away silver and grey to the edge of the world.

Orrin waited at the far end of the crest, with Idan. Around him stood twenty of the Unnamed, white masks shining like small moons. A little apart stood Brother Seven, the crack in his mask so wide now that half his real face showed through, the skin around it raw where he'd been tearing at it.

Idan stood quite still in Orrin's grip, eyes open, humming.

And between us and them, the grey was gathering.

---

It rose out of the sand like smoke from a fire put out with water. It thickened. It took a shape.

A boy. Smaller than Zayn, barefoot and thin, in a shepherd's tunic, with a shepherd's crook. Everything about him was grey. Only his eyes were different: black, completely black, like two holes cut into the night, and if you looked too long you could see things moving far down in them, small and pale, like faces at the bottom of a well.

I knew that face. I had known it before there were cities in this desert, when it was brown and laughing and smelled of sheep.

"Hello, Malek," I said.

"Hello, little fox." A child's voice, and under it a thousand others, a beat behind. "You've come to watch another one burn."

"I've come to watch one *choose*. It's different. You'd know, if you'd done it."

The grey boy smiled. The smile went a little too far back on both sides.

Then he turned his black eyes on Zayn.

"Look at them," Malek said softly, pointing his crook down at the Husks. "That one threw stones at you when you were six. Those ones called you monster and watched you starve. And you'd burn for them?"

Zayn's light flickered grey at the edges.

"And here's what the fox won't tell you," said Malek, gentle as sand. "Ask him how many. Not bearers. *Children*. Ask him how old the Boy of Salt was. Ask him why, every time, it's a child. Ask him what's waiting on the other side of the light with its mouth open. It isn't me, Zayn. I'm only the one who said no."

Zayn looked at me.

My tail glowed. Gold. Steady. I couldn't stop it. I've never been able to stop it.

I saw it go into him.

"And after," said Malek, "they'll light a lantern. One more in a row of eleven. In a hundred years no one will remember what it was for. Bearers' names come off the walls like the names in Veimore. You'll be forgotten." A whisper. "You'll be forgotten, Zayn. Just like you always were."

My tail went dark. Pitch dark, cold as a dead coal.

But it didn't matter what my tail said. It was the oldest fear he had, the one he'd carried since he was a baby crying in the sand. *Nobody will remember me.* And now, beside it, a new one I had put there: *and something is hungry for me, and always has been.*

His light went grey all the way through.

"There's a way out," said Orrin.

He stepped forward with one hand on Idan's shoulder and the thin grey blade in the other. His soft voice carried along the crest the way it had filled the throne hall.

"Give me your name. That's all. Say it to the wind. Your true name, the one in your blood. Malek will take it, and the Light with it, and it's over. No burning. You'll simply forget. And then everyone will forget, every loss, every grief, every dead son." Something moved in his faded eyes, far down. "I promise you, child, it doesn't hurt. I burned my own name to stop remembering. Forgetting is the only mercy there is."

My tail flickered. Neither dark nor bright.

He believed it. That's the worst kind of lie, little one: the kind the liar has told himself so long it sounds like truth.

"Give me your name," said Orrin.

And Zayn, grey and shaking, opened his mouth.

---

Bells.

Small bronze bells, a great many of them, ringing faintly somewhere below on the slope. Even the Husks' hum faltered.

Someone was climbing the Crown Dune on hands and knees through the sliding sand. Around his waist hung a belt of tiny truth bells that chimed with every movement, and all the way down the slope the Husks had turned to watch him pass, and none of them had touched him.

Old Hesset.

For three hundred years he'd been bound to the edge of the desert, the price of the night he ran. To leave was to die.

He had walked across the whole Destiny Desert anyway.

He was dying as he climbed. You could see it. His beard was thin as smoke. His skin had gone papery and was splitting at the knuckles, and what came out of the splits wasn't blood but a fine pale dust. He climbed toward our voices as if they were a fire on a cold night.

He reached the crest and knelt, gasping. Malek turned to him.

"Hesset," said the grey boy, warmly. "My old friend."

Every bell on Hesset's belt rang at once.

"I was never your friend," said Hesset. "You only told me what I wanted to hear, so I'd run. I've had three hundred years in the dark to work that out." He turned his blind face. "Boy. Are you there?"

"I'm here."

"I asked you once, in my tent. *Would you die for them?* You couldn't answer." The old man swallowed. "Someone asked me the same, on this sand, three hundred years ago. I said no, and I ran, and the last thing I ever saw was the top of this dune. I hid from you that bearers die, because I was ashamed. And I hid something else. When I said no, up here, I *heard* it, child. I heard it be disappointed." His hands clenched in the sand. "I'm sorry. I'm so sorry."

Not a single bell rang.

"Don't answer for me," whispered Hesset. "Don't answer for them. Don't answer for whatever's listening. Answer for you."

Zayn stood very still on the top of the Crown Dune.

He looked at Malek. He looked at me, and at my tail, which had told him a thing about the Light he would never now stop knowing. He looked down at the Husks. He looked at Idan, humming, forgetting his own name a little more with every breath. He looked at his father.

Then he untied Hesset's truth bell from his belt and held it up in front of him, so everyone could see.

"Yes," he said.

The bell didn't ring.

And the grey went out of his light like a shadow when the Fire comes up. It turned gold. A deeper gold than I had ever seen, the gold of honey held up to the light. It was beautiful. I want you to know that. It was the most beautiful thing I have ever seen.

It was also the colour of a coal just before it falls apart.

Malek took a step backward.

---

"Brother Seven," said Orrin sharply. "Cut him."

Tamir raised his twin grey blades, the only things in the world that could cut the Light. The blades were shaking, and through the great crack in his mask one pale eye stared, wet and wide.

He was humming. Not the chant. The phoenix song. He didn't know he was doing it.

Zayn walked straight up to him, until the grey blades were a finger's width from his chest and the steel began to frost and smoke at once where the Light met it.

"You held me," he said. "When I was born. I saw it. You were five, and you held me, and you were scared you'd drop me."

The blades trembled. Then, slowly, Tamir lowered them.

Zayn walked past him to Orrin. The vizier backed away, dragging Idan, and laid the grey blade against the boy's throat. Zayn didn't stop. He put his hand over Orrin's hand on the grip, and the gold went into it.

Orrin cried out and let go. His hand was grey to the wrist where the Light had touched it, crazed with fine cracks like a glaze. The blade fell into the sand.

Idan stopped humming. He blinked. He said, in a small, astonished voice, "Idan," as if he'd found it in a pocket. Then he ran, straight to Daruun, and Daruun caught him, and the boy buried his face in the grey wrappings and shook.

Zayn knelt before him. He untied Soren's ring from beside the infinity knot and pressed it into Idan's hand. It left a red mark on the small palm. Idan closed his fingers on it anyway.

"Your father wanted you to have this. And so would Soren."

Idan was ten. He wasn't stupid.

"Are you going to die?"

"Yes."

"Like Soren?"

Zayn thought about it. "No," he said. "Soren didn't get to choose. I do."

---

Daruun let go of Idan and took his son's hands, and didn't flinch at the heat.

"Let me," he said.

It was not the voice of the Veiled Rider.

"Let me do it. It was mine before it was yours, and I ran. Feed it me. I'm old, I'm nothing, let it have me instead. I ran so you could live. Don't make that for nothing. Please, son. Not you. Not you."

Zayn shook his head. He couldn't speak for a moment.

"It doesn't want you," he said, very gently. "It wants the Light, and the Light's in me." He tightened his grip. "And it wasn't for nothing. You left me in the sand, and the desert kept me, and I grew up alone, so I know what it's like to be the one nobody wants. That's why I can do this. It all meant something. Even the bad parts. *Especially* the bad parts."

My tail was so bright I couldn't look at it.

I'll tell you what I thought, little one. I thought: *it meant something. And I no longer know whether that's a comfort or the worst thing in the world.*

Daruun bowed his head over their joined hands. I have never seen anything as brave as what he did next.

He let go.

One finger at a time, slowly, like a man lowering something precious into deep water. The skin of his palms stuck to his son's hands and tore, and he let go anyway. When the last finger slid free, he stepped back beside Idan and Hesset and didn't move again.

The hardest choice of his life. He made it.

---

Zayn turned to me last.

I was sitting in the sand, too tired to stand, my fur dark with my own blood, my silver eye aching the way it always aches near the end. He knelt so our faces were level. Heat came off him like an open oven.

"You lied to me once," he said.

"I know."

"Tell me the truth now. Will it hurt?"

"Yes," I said. My tail glowed. "More than anything has ever hurt."

"Will it matter?"

"Yes."

"Is Malek right? Is something waiting?"

"I don't know," I said.

And my tail did nothing at all. Neither gold nor dark. Some truths are too big even for a fox's tail.

He looked at it a long moment. Then he smiled, the small, crooked, rusty smile he never wasted, and put his forehead against mine. It singed the fur between my ears. I'll carry the mark until the world ends.

"Look after them," he said.

For the first time in all my long life, I couldn't think of a single word.

He walked to the very end of the crest, where the dune fell away on every side into the silver dark, and stood under the full Ash with his back to us all.

"I always wanted to be a hero," he said quietly. To the boy in the patched tent, maybe. "Like in the stories."

He took a breath.

"I am the hero."

It wasn't a boast. It was only true. My tail had never burned so bright.

Then he lifted his face to the Ash, and to whatever was beyond it, and he said the last words he would ever say.

"I'm coming, Soren."

---

And here I have to tell it the way I told it at the very beginning, because it has never stopped happening. It's happening now, somewhere behind my eyes, and I can still smell it.

He is burning.

He stands at the top of the Crown Dune with his arms open, as if he's about to catch something falling out of the sky. The fire doesn't start at his hands. It starts in his chest, under the mark, the small golden feather that has glowed in the dark his whole life. It splits like a seed splits, from the inside. I hear it crack. Light comes through every golden line that every healing left on him, and the lines split wider, and his skin curls back from them like paper from a flame. It runs down his arms and up his throat. It comes out of his eyes, and his eyes go with it.

The smell reaches me. Honey and hot metal, and under that, his hair. I will never stop smelling it.

And Orrin lunges.

Grey robes, grey hand, grey eyes wide and starving. He's waited his whole life for this light, and he throws himself into it with both hands, grabbing, as if he could tear it out of the boy's chest and keep it.

"*Mine,*" he says.

It isn't. It never could be. The Light can't be taken. It can only be given.

It turns grey in his hands. The gold he's grasping goes dull, then ashen, and the grey runs up his fingers and wrists and arms like frost climbing a window, cracking as it climbs. For a single moment his face changes. He sees something in the stolen light: a small boy, and a brother, and a name he burned long ago. His mouth opens to say it.

"Ez—"

His jaw goes first.

It doesn't fall. It crumbles, like an old grey statue left too long in the wind, and the rest of him follows from the face down, still kneeling, still reaching. The last of him to go is his hands, clenched in the light, trying to hold on. The wind lifts the dust off the Crown Dune and carries it into the dark, the way it carries away a name spoken carelessly.

There is nothing left of him. Not even the name. He never finished it.

And the boy doesn't scream.

That's the part I always remember. He is burning to the bone and he doesn't scream, and I have never understood how, and I have never wanted to.

He looks down. Below him, all across the desert, the dunes are moving, shifting and folding and rising, writing across the sand in letters miles high. The same shapes they wrote for him on the first nights after Harrow's End, when he couldn't read a word and I wouldn't tell him what they said.

He can read them now. A boy with a broken sword taught him, laughing the whole time.

*A*, the dunes write. *E. R. O. N.*

*Aeron.* In the old tongue, *the one who rises.*

His true name, the one the Weaver wrote for him before he was born, the one the wind could never find, because the desert kept it safe all this time. The way you keep something safe that you mean to use. He reads it, the last thing he will ever read, with eyes that are more light than eye now, and I see what's left of his mouth smile.

Then he's looking at something only he can see. But I see it too, for a moment, in the light pouring out of him.

The Singing Wells, under a thin Ash. The water humming. A young man on the edge of the well with half a sword across his knees, a scarred jaw and a crooked grin and dark eyes, laughing at him the way he always did. Holding out his hand.

*You took your time,* says Soren.

And behind Soren, in the dark beyond the well, standing in a row as if they've waited a very long time, I see the others. Eight small figures, the eight who gave themselves. Some barely taller than the well's edge. A boy of nine with salt-white hair. A smith's apprentice with burned hands. A girl holding a weaver's shuttle. All looking at Zayn. None of them smiling. Their faces are patient and gentle and very, very tired, the faces of children who have stood in a queue too long.

Zayn laughs, the way he laughed the first time, by a fire, when a prince tried to eat a scorpion. He takes the hand. The row of children opens to let him in, and he takes his place at the end of it.

There's a space beside him. An empty space at the very end of the row, the width of one small child.

I see it every time. I have never once been able to look away from it.

The light keeps growing until it isn't a boy anymore. It's a shape, two great wings of gold unfolding across the dark, wide enough to cover the dunes from one edge of the sky to the other.

Then it falls.

Gold rains down over the desert, and everywhere it lands, the grey draws back.

Somewhere below, someone is shouting a name.

It's his father, on his knees in the sand a little way down the crest, his arms around a crying prince, his raw hands, his face turned up into the falling gold. Shouting it over and over, the only name he was ever allowed to call his son.

"*Zayn! Zayn!*"

And the wind takes it, gently, and carries it out over the whole desert. Not to the dark this time.

There isn't any dark left.

That's what I believed, that night. I want you to know I believed it. For one whole night, I did.

---

*End of Chapter Nineteen*


---

# Chapter Twenty
## Ash into Gold

> *Ash into gold and gold into flame —*
> *The end is the start, and the start is the same.*
> — from the Song of the Phoenix-Born; the last verse, which is also the first

---

The gold fell all night.

It came down soft and slow, like the first warm rain after a long drought, and everywhere it touched, the grey let go.

I watched from the top of the Crown Dune. I couldn't have moved if I'd wanted to. Down at the foot of the dune, the great ring of Husks stood with their faces turned up into the falling light, and one by one, the white went out of their eyes. Brown came back. Black. Green. An old herder looked down at his own hands and began to cry. A woman spun round and round in the sand, calling a child's name, and somewhere in the crowd a child's voice called back.

Not all of them. I promised you no more lies, so I'll tell you. Some of them woke screaming, because the gold gave them back everything, including what their hands had done while they were empty. And some didn't wake at all. The gold touched them, and they lay down in the sand, still smiling, and didn't get up.

Then there was *sound*. Thousands of people talking and weeping and shouting names. The whole desert was saying them.
